Doubly Free-Boundary Macdonald Processes: Reflection Identities and Jack Asymptotics Thanks: Corresponding author: Zhongyang Li.
Zhongyang Li, Kaili Shi

Abstract
. We introduce a doubly free-boundary Macdonald process on rail-yard interlacings and develop a reflection calculus for its observables. Boundary Cauchy–Littlewood identities, combined with Neguţ operators, yield exact multipoint contour formulas for arbitrary L / R L/R words. Under the Jack scaling q = t α , t = e − n ​ β ​ ϵ , q=t^{\alpha},\qquad t=e^{-n\beta\epsilon}, and piecewise-periodic data, these formulas imply a Laplace-transform law of large numbers and a weak slope-measure limit shape at L L -type columns. For arbitrary piecewise-periodic L / R L/R backgrounds and finitely many L L -type marked columns, under the stated contour, branch, and normal-convergence hypotheses, the centered height-Laplace observables converge jointly to a Gaussian vector. Its covariance exhibits a boundary–deformation separation: the Jack parameter and the microscopic rail-yard data enter through the one-point spectral factors and the normalization, whereas the two-point interaction is the logarithmic derivative of an annular prime function generated by the two boundary reflections. Thus the deformation changes the spectral map while preserving the annular image geometry of the Schur specialization. For β = 1 \beta=1 , in the all- L L sector and under explicit signed zero–pole and root-localization hypotheses, we characterize regular liquid and frozen points through the nonreal-root structure of the characteristic equation and show that nondegenerate regular interfaces lie on the real double-root locus. The half-space Macdonald-process formulas are recovered in the continuous degeneration v ↓ 0 v\downarrow 0 , which forces the right boundary partition to be empty.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 60K35; Secondary 82B20, 05E05, 60B20

1.
Introduction

Random tiling and dimer models provide a basic class of exactly solvable probability
measures in which deterministic limit shapes coexist with Gaussian height fluctuations. In
many Schur and determinantal models, the analysis is ultimately controlled by correlation
kernels and their steepest-descent asymptotics. Macdonald and Jack deformations usually
remove this determinantal structure. This paper asks what remains of the limit-shape and
Gaussian-fluctuation theory when a Jack deformation is combined with two free boundaries in
rail-yard geometry.

The answer is that the two free boundaries affect both orders of the asymptotic theory.
At first order they enter the Laplace-transform law of large numbers through reflected
one-point factors and hence modify the deterministic limit shape. More importantly,
they also change the image structure of the second-order field. For the height
Laplace-test observables considered here, the limiting covariance is an annular
reflected-image kernel. This kernel is produced by repeated reflection of Cauchy factors
between the two free boundaries; it is absent in the empty-boundary Schur degeneration and
survives in the Jack scaling limit.

1.1.
The boundary mechanism

Rail-yard graphs form a flexible class of bipartite graphs whose perfect matchings are
encoded by interlacing sequences of partitions. In Schur degenerations this encoding leads
to Schur processes and free-boundary Schur processes. The model studied in this paper is
the corresponding Macdonald-deformed measure on partition sequences, represented
geometrically as doubly free-boundary dimer states on rail-yard graphs. Except in
Schur-type degenerations, this should not be confused with a local product dimer Gibbs
measure: the rail-yard geometry supplies the interlacing dynamics, while the weights are
Macdonald weights with boundary specializations.

The essential new feature is algebraic reflection at the free boundaries. Ordinary
full-space Macdonald processes are controlled by skew Cauchy commutations, which interchange
two adjacent bulk skew factors and produce a Cauchy factor. Free boundaries require an
additional step: skew Littlewood identities sum over the boundary partition and turn an
incoming skew factor into a reflected outgoing one, multiplied by a boundary factor. Iterating
this reflection between the two boundaries produces the infinite one-point products and the
two-boundary covariance correction.

This places the paper at the interface of two established theories, while the
asymptotic phenomenon studied here is specific to the doubly free-boundary geometry.
Ahn’s work on Macdonald plane partitions
[
1
]
treats the full-space
jack-process side: skew plane partitions, equivalently lozenge tilings or
honeycomb dimers, are analyzed by difference-operator asymptotics and yield
deterministic limit shapes and Gaussian free field fluctuations as
q,t\to 1
.

The boundary side is closer in spirit to the half-space Macdonald-process theory of
Barraquand–Borodin–Corwin
[
3
]
. In that theory, the ordinary Cauchy
summation identity of full-space Macdonald processes is replaced by Littlewood
summation identities, and new exact observables are needed. The one-free-boundary
degeneration of the present model belongs to this half-space framework. Here we
analyze the Jack scaling of the doubly free-boundary formulas themselves. The two
free boundaries generate repeated reflections, producing infinite one-point factors and
a two-boundary covariance correction which survives in the scaling limit. Thus the main result is not merely a Macdonald-process analogue of
full-space plane-partition asymptotics: it shows that the annular
reflected-image geometry generated by two free boundaries persists
under the non-determinantal Jack deformation.

A useful point of comparison is the Schur-weighted doubly
free-boundary rail-yard model studied in
[
11
]
. In that setting
the measure is the local edge-product dimer measure, and the repeated
boundary reflections already produce infinite reflected products and
an annular covariance for height-Laplace observables. The present work
concerns a genuinely Macdonald-deformed, non-determinantal measure. We
develop the corresponding two-boundary Cauchy identities and
multipoint Neguţ contour formulas, and analyze their Jack limit under

q=t^{\alpha},\qquad t=e^{-n\beta\epsilon}.

The main structural conclusion is a deformation-stability phenomenon:
the Jack parameter and the microscopic
L/R
data modify the
one-point spectral factors and the normalization, whereas the
two-boundary image geometry of the limiting covariance remains
annular. Thus the present results provide a Macdonald reflection
theory for the two-free-boundary geometry, rather than a formal
specialization of the Schur formulas.

1.2.
Exact formulas

The first contribution is algebraic. We prove boundary Cauchy identities for Macdonald
polynomials, including the even and conjugate-even boundary specializations used by the free
boundaries. These identities are combined with Neguţ operators to obtain exact contour
formulas for exponential height-Laplace observables. In the present non-determinantal Jack
setting, these contour formulas play the role that correlation kernels play in Schur models.
They are also the point at which the two-boundary mechanism becomes visible: after
normalization, the integrands contain both infinite one-point reflected products and a
non-trivial two-point free-boundary correction.

The formulas are stated for the general
L/R
rail-yard process. They contain the empty-
and one-free-boundary cases as limits. The half-space formulas arise by taking the
appropriate one-boundary limit of the doubly free-boundary partition and contour identities;
in the contour formulas this limit is taken together with the associated reflected products,
pole separation, and branch choices.

1.3.
Law of large numbers and weak limit shape

The second contribution is asymptotic. We work in the exact Jack scaling

q=t^{\alpha},\qquad t=e^{-n\beta\epsilon},

with piecewise periodic rail-yard data. Under the analytic admissibility and branch
hypotheses stated below, the one-point contour formula gives a Laplace-transform law of
large numbers at
L
-type marked columns. The limiting transform is naturally written in
terms of

S_{\chi}(w):=\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{r\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,r}(w),

where all non-integer powers are taken with the compatible branch system specified in the
main assumptions.

The deterministic limit shape is formulated in the natural Macdonald coordinate. We set

x=e^{-n\beta\kappa},

so that the integer moments of the pushed-forward slope measure are exactly the moments
provided by the Neguţ contour formula. With this choice of variable, the transform asymptotics become moment asymptotics for the
pushed-forward slope measures. An exponential moment bound gives compact moment
determinacy, so these moment asymptotics determine a unique weak limit. This yields the
deterministic limit shape in the weak slope-measure sense, without requiring analytic
continuation of the Laplace exponent.

1.4.
Annular Gaussian fluctuations

The main probabilistic result is the fluctuation theorem. For arbitrary piecewise-periodic
L/R
backgrounds, the centered
height-Laplace observables at finitely many
L
-type marked columns,
written in the charge-centered
(q,t)
-column coordinate, converge
in finite-dimensional distributions to a Gaussian vector under the
stated contour, branch, and normal-convergence hypotheses. The limiting
covariance is not the pullback of the standard upper-half-plane image covariance. Instead,
the two free boundaries produce an annular logarithmic-derivative kernel.

In the notation used below, the
L
-chart covariance kernel is

\mathsf{K}_{LL}(z,w)=\partial_{z}\partial_{w}\log\frac{\Theta_{\mathfrak{q}}(z/w)}{\Theta_{\mathfrak{q}}(u^{2}zw)},\qquad\mathfrak{q}=(uv)^{2}.

Equivalently, in nested contour coordinates this is the bulk double-pole term together with
the annular image term and the two reflected-boundary image terms. The free-boundary
correction is not a lower-order error: it is produced by mixed reflections between the two
boundaries and survives as
\epsilon\to 0
. Thus the second-order effect of the two free
boundaries is genuinely macroscopic.

The term “annular Gaussian free field” is used here in this precise Laplace-test sense. We
prove convergence of the
L
-chart height-Laplace observables and identify their covariance
with the annular prime-function logarithmic-derivative kernel.

1.5.
Frozen boundary

The frozen-boundary analysis is an application of the limit-shape theorem and is kept
separate from the Gaussian result. For the root-count classification we restrict to
\beta=1
. In this case
the characteristic equation is

S_{\chi}(w)=e^{-n\kappa}.

Under Assumptions
6.6
–
6.7
and the
nonexceptionality conditions of Theorem
2.8
, regular liquid
points correspond to a unique nonreal conjugate pair, while regular
frozen points correspond to the all-real-root regime.
Consequently nondegenerate regular interfaces lie on the real double-root locus

\begin{cases}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\displaystyle\prod_{r\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,r}(w)=e^{-n\kappa},\\[5.69054pt]
\displaystyle\frac{d}{dw}\log\!\left(\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{r\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,r}(w)\right)=0.\end{cases}

The conditional hypotheses are kept explicit.
Assumptions
6.6
–
6.7
provide a concrete separated
all-
L
regime for the finite signed zero–pole order, while
Lemmas
6.9
and

6.10
supply the local pole-free
no-escape input under endpoint nonexceptionality.

In contrast with the mixed-
L/R
Schur analysis
of
[
11
]
, the regular root classification proved here is
currently restricted to the all-
L
sector. For mixed
L/R
Jack
data, the
R
-type spectral factors carry the generally non-integral
exponent
\alpha
, so the finite truncations are branch-dependent
rather than rational and the Schur polynomial root-count argument does
not apply directly.

1.6.
Organization

Section
2
defines the Macdonald-deformed doubly free-boundary rail-yard measure,
introduces the height observables, and states the main theorems. Section
3

proves the partition-function formula by boundary reflection and commutation identities for
Macdonald polynomials. Section
4
derives the contour formula for the expectation
of the height-Laplace transform using Neguţ operators. Section
5
performs the
Jack asymptotic analysis of the one-point and multi-point formulas; the infinite reflected
products produce the functions
\mathcal{F}_{u,v,r}
, and the two-boundary part produces the
covariance correction.

Section
6
proves the Laplace-transform law of large numbers, upgrades it to the
weak slope-measure limit shape in the variable
x=e^{-n\beta\kappa}
, and derives the
conditional regular frozen-boundary equations. It also gives explicit separated all-
L
sufficient conditions
for the finite signed zero–pole order and proves the local
root-localization input used in the regular root classification. Section
7
identifies the fluctuation covariance
with the annular reflected-image kernel and proves the annular Laplace-test Gaussian theorem.
Section
8
records the one-boundary degeneration corresponding to the
half-space Macdonald-process geometry. Appendix
A
collects the Macdonald
polynomial identities and technical inputs used in the proof.

2.
Main Results

In this section, we review rail-yard graphs and dimer coverings, and state the main results proved in this paper.

2.1.
Weighted rail-yard graphs

The rail-yard graph was introduced in
[
5
]
as a broad class of graphs on which random perfect matchings give rise to Schur processes. It is believed to be the most general known family of graphs for which perfect matchings naturally correspond to measures governed by Schur-process-type dynamics. We now recall the definition.

Let
l,r\in{\mathbb{Z}}
with
l\leq r
, and set

[l..r]:=[l,r]\cap{\mathbb{Z}}.

For a positive integer
m
, we also write

[m]:=\{1,2,\ldots,m\}.

Consider two sequences indexed by integers in
[l..r]
:

•

the
LR
-sequence
a=(a_{l},a_{l+1},\ldots,a_{r})\in\{L,R\}^{[l..r]}
;

•

the sign sequence
b=(b_{l},b_{l+1},\ldots,b_{r})\in\{+,-\}^{[l..r]}
.

The
rail-yard graph

RYG(l,r,a,b)
associated with
(l,r,a,b)
is the bipartite graph with vertex set

[2l-1..2r+1]\times\left({\mathbb{Z}}+\frac{1}{2}\right).

A vertex is called
even
(resp.
odd
) if its abscissa is an even (resp. odd) integer. Each even vertex
(2m,y)
,
m\in[l..r]
, is incident to three edges: two horizontal edges joining it to
(2m-1,y)
and
(2m+1,y)
, and one diagonal edge joining it to

•

(2m-1,y+1)
if
(a_{m},b_{m})=(L,+)
;

•

(2m-1,y-1)
if
(a_{m},b_{m})=(L,-)
;

•

(2m+1,y+1)
if
(a_{m},b_{m})=(R,+)
;

•

(2m+1,y-1)
if
(a_{m},b_{m})=(R,-)
.

See Figure
2.1
for an example.

Figure 2.1.
A rail-yard graph with
LR
-sequence
a=\{L,R,R,L\}
and sign sequence
b=\{+,+,-,-\}
. Odd vertices are represented by red points, and even vertices are represented by blue points. Dark edges represent a dimer covering. Assume that above the horizontal line
y=4
, only horizontal edges with an odd vertex on the left are present in the dimer configuration; and below the horizontal line
y=-4
, only horizontal edges with an even vertex on the left are present. The corresponding sequence of partitions (from left to right) is
\emptyset\prec(2,0,\ldots)\prec^{\prime}(3,1,1,\ldots)\succ^{\prime}(2,0,\ldots)\succ\emptyset
.

The left boundary (resp. right boundary) of
RYG(l,r,a,b)
consists of all odd vertices with abscissa
2l-1
(resp.
2r+1
). Vertices that do not lie on the boundary are called
inner
vertices. A face of
RYG(l,r,a,b)
is called an
inner face
if all vertices on its boundary are inner vertices.

We assign edge weights to
RYG(l,r,a,b)
as follows:

•

every horizontal edge has weight
1
;

•

each diagonal edge adjacent to an even vertex with abscissa
2i
has weight
x_{i}
.

These local weights are parameters entering the Macdonald-deformed measure defined in
Section
3
; except in Schur-type degenerations, the probability measure used
below is not merely the product of local occupied-edge weights.

2.2.
Dimer coverings

A
perfect matching
, or
dimer covering
, of a graph is a subset of edges such that each vertex is incident to exactly one edge; see
[
9
,
8
]
. For rail-yard graphs with free boundary, we use the following variant.

Definition 2.1

(Free-boundary dimer covering)
.

A
free-boundary dimer covering
of
RYG(l,r,a,b)
is a subset
M
of edges such that

(1)

each inner vertex is incident to exactly one selected edge;

(2)

each left-boundary or right-boundary vertex is incident to at most one selected edge;

(3)

only finitely many selected edges are diagonal;

(4)

there exists
N>0
such that the following two reference phases hold:

(a)

if
y>N
, then for every even vertex
(2i,y)
,
i\in[l..r]
, the selected edge incident to
(2i,y)
is the horizontal edge joining
(2i-1,y)
to
(2i,y)
;

(b)

if
y<-N
, then for every even vertex
(2i,y)
,
i\in[l..r]
, the selected edge incident to
(2i,y)
is the horizontal edge joining
(2i,y)
to
(2i+1,y)
.

Equivalently, sufficiently high in the graph every present edge is the horizontal edge lying to the right of its odd endpoint, while sufficiently low in the graph every present edge is the horizontal edge lying to the left of its odd endpoint.

Definition 2.2

(Partitions, conjugates, and parity)
.

A
partition
is a non-increasing sequence

\lambda=(\lambda_{i})_{i\geq 1}

of nonnegative integers that is eventually zero. Let
\mathbb{Y}
denote the
set of all partitions. The size of
\lambda
is

|\lambda|=\sum_{i\geq 1}\lambda_{i}.

The
conjugate partition

\lambda^{\prime}
is the partition whose Young
diagram is obtained from that of
\lambda
by reflection across the main
diagonal. Equivalently,

\lambda^{\prime}_{i}:=\bigl|\{j\geq 1:\lambda_{j}\geq i\}\bigr|,\qquad i\geq 1.

A partition
\lambda
is called
even
if all of its parts are even,
that is,

\lambda_{i}\in 2\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\qquad\text{for all }i\geq 1.

It is called
conjugate-even
if its conjugate partition is even, namely

\lambda^{\prime}\text{ is even}.

Equivalently, every column length of the Young diagram of
\lambda
is even.

For two partitions
\lambda,\mu
, we write
\lambda\succ\mu
, or

\mu\prec\lambda
, if

\lambda_{1}\geq\mu_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\mu_{2}\geq\lambda_{3}\geq\cdots.

Equivalently,
\lambda/\mu
is a horizontal strip. We write

\lambda\prec^{\prime}\mu

if

\lambda^{\prime}\prec\mu^{\prime}.

Equivalently,
\mu/\lambda
is a vertical strip.

Definition 2.3

(Particle–hole configuration and column partitions)
.

Let
M
be a dimer covering of
RYG(l,r,a,b)
in the sense of
Definition
2.1
. We associate a particle–hole configuration to the odd vertices as
follows. If an odd vertex is incident to a selected edge on its right, we put a hole at that
vertex; if it is incident to a selected edge on its left, we put a particle there. At the two
free boundaries we use the exterior-edge convention: an unmatched left-boundary odd vertex
is regarded as matched to a virtual exterior edge on its left, and is therefore a particle;
an unmatched right-boundary odd vertex is regarded as matched to a virtual exterior edge on
its right, and is therefore a hole.

For
m\in[l..r+1]
, this gives a Maya diagram on the odd column
x=2m-1
. By the
tail condition in Definition
2.1
, this Maya diagram has only holes sufficiently far
above and only particles sufficiently far below. It therefore has finite charge and determines
a partition

\lambda^{(M,m)}=\bigl(\lambda^{(M,m)}_{1},\lambda^{(M,m)}_{2},\ldots\bigr)\in\mathbb{Y}

as follows. List the particles on the column
x=2m-1
from top to bottom. For
i\geq 1
,
let
\lambda^{(M,m)}_{i}
be the number of holes strictly below the
i
-th highest particle.
The tail condition implies that this sequence is weakly decreasing and eventually zero, so it
is an ordinary Young partition. We call
\lambda^{(M,m)}
the partition associated with the
odd column
x=2m-1
.

In particular, the left and right boundary columns determine the boundary partitions

\lambda^{(M,l)},\qquad\lambda^{(M,r+1)}.

When no confusion can arise, we write
M
also for the covering together with this induced
particle–hole configuration and its associated column partitions.

See Figure
2.1
for an example.

For a free-boundary dimer covering
M
of
RYG(l,r,a,b)
, we define the associated height function
h_{M}
on faces of
RYG(l,r,a,b)
as follows. Definition
2.1
(4) implies that there exists
N>0
such that when
y<-N
, only horizontal edges with an even vertex on the left are present. Fix a face
f_{0}
whose midpoint lies on the horizontal line
y=-N
, and set

\overline{h}_{M}(f_{0})=0.

For any two adjacent faces
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
sharing at least one edge, define the increment of
\overline{h}_{M}
by the following rules:

•

If moving from
f_{1}
to
f_{2}
crosses a horizontal edge with an odd vertex on the left, then

\overline{h}_{M}(f_{2})-\overline{h}_{M}(f_{1})=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if the edge is present in }M,\\
-1,&\text{if the edge is absent from }M;\end{cases}

•

If moving from
f_{1}
to
f_{2}
crosses a diagonal edge with an odd vertex on the left, then

\overline{h}_{M}(f_{2})-\overline{h}_{M}(f_{1})=\begin{cases}2,&\text{if the edge is present in }M,\\
0,&\text{if the edge is absent from }M.\end{cases}

These rules specify the increment for oriented dual crossings for which the odd endpoint of the crossed edge lies on the left. If the same edge is crossed in the opposite direction, or equivalently if the odd endpoint lies on the right for the chosen orientation, the increment is defined to be the negative of the increment assigned to the reversed crossing. With this antisymmetry convention the preliminary height function is well defined up to the choice of the initial face. Equivalently, for the vertical lines used below, one may take the explicit formulas (
2.2
)–(
2.3
) as the definition of the normalized height.

Let
\overline{h}_{0}
be the preliminary height function associated with the dimer configuration in which

•

no diagonal edge is present; and

•

every present edge is horizontal with an even vertex on the left.

The height function associated with
M
is then defined by

(2.1)

h_{M}=\overline{h}_{M}-\overline{h}_{0}.

Let
m\in[l..r]
. The vertical line
x=2m-\frac{1}{2}
crosses only edges whose left endpoint is odd. Therefore, for each point
\left(2m-\frac{1}{2},y\right)
lying in a face of
RYG(l,r,a,b)
, we have

(2.2)

h_{M}\!\left(2m-\frac{1}{2},y\right)=2\left[N_{h,M}^{-}\!\left(2m-\frac{1}{2},y\right)+N_{d,M}^{-}\!\left(2m-\frac{1}{2},y\right)\right],

where
N_{h,M}^{-}\left(2m-\frac{1}{2},y\right)
is the number of present horizontal edges in
M
crossed by
x=2m-\frac{1}{2}
below height
y
, and
N_{d,M}^{-}\left(2m-\frac{1}{2},y\right)
is the number of present diagonal edges in
M
crossed by the same vertical line below height
y
. Both quantities are finite for each finite
y
.

Similarly, the vertical line
x=2m+\frac{1}{2}
crosses only edges whose left endpoint is even. Hence, for each point
\left(2m+\frac{1}{2},y\right)
lying in a face of
RYG(l,r,a,b)
,

(2.3)

h_{M}\!\left(2m+\frac{1}{2},y\right)=2\left[J_{h,M}^{-}\!\left(2m+\frac{1}{2},y\right)-N_{d,M}^{-}\!\left(2m+\frac{1}{2},y\right)\right],

where
J_{h,M}^{-}\left(2m+\frac{1}{2},y\right)
is the number of absent horizontal edges in
M
crossed by
x=2m+\frac{1}{2}
below height
y
, and
N_{d,M}^{-}\left(2m+\frac{1}{2},y\right)
is the number of present diagonal edges crossed by the same vertical line below height
y
. Again, both quantities are finite for each finite
y
.

Lemma 2.4

(Height normalization and finiteness)
.

For every free-boundary dimer covering
M
, the vertical-line formulas (
2.2
)–(
2.3
) define the same normalized height as the face-increment construction above. In particular the normalized height is path-independent on faces. Moreover, for every fixed column
m
and every
k>0
, the height-Laplace integrals

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left|h_{M}\!\left(2m\pm\frac{1}{2},y\right)\right|e^{-ky}\,dy

are finite. The same assertion holds for the column-wise
(q,t)
-coordinate system of Definition
4.4
after replacing
y
by that coordinate.

Proof.

By Definition
2.1
(4), below a sufficiently low horizontal line the normalized height is zero. Above that line, along a fixed vertical line the height changes only when the line crosses a horizontal edge, or an occupied diagonal edge. There are finitely many occupied diagonal edges, and the horizontal contribution grows at most linearly in the vertical coordinate. It follows that
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left|h_{M}\!\left(2m\pm\frac{1}{2},y\right)\right|e^{-ky}\,dy
is finite.
∎

We use the notation

i_{\equiv_{n}}=\begin{cases}i\bmod n,&\text{if }i\bmod n\neq 0,\\
n,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

2.3.
The Macdonald-deformed doubly free-boundary measure

We now define the probability measure used in the main theorems. The factorized formula
for its normalizing constant will be proved in Section
3
.

Let
q,t\in(0,1)
, let
u,v\in(0,1)
, and let

c_{l},c_{r}\in\{el,oa,deel,eoa\}
be the left and right boundary types. For a partition

\lambda
, and for a box
s=(i,j)\in\lambda
, let

a_{\lambda}(s):=\lambda_{i}-j,\qquad l_{\lambda}(s):=\lambda^{\prime}_{j}-i

be its arm and leg lengths, respectively; equivalently,
a_{\lambda}(s)
is the number of
boxes of
\lambda
strictly to the right of
s
, and
l_{\lambda}(s)
is the number of boxes
strictly below
s
.

Set

b_{\lambda}(s;q,t)=\frac{1-q^{a_{\lambda}(s)}t^{l_{\lambda}(s)+1}}{1-q^{a_{\lambda}(s)+1}t^{l_{\lambda}(s)}}.

Define

b_{\lambda}(q,t):=\prod_{s\in\lambda}b_{\lambda}(s;q,t),

and

b_{\lambda}^{el}:=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\lambda\\
l_{\lambda}(s)\ {\rm even}\end{subarray}}b_{\lambda}(s;q,t),\qquad b_{\lambda}^{oa}:=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\lambda\\
a_{\lambda}(s)\ {\rm odd}\end{subarray}}b_{\lambda}(s;q,t).

We also set

(2.4)

\overline{b}_{\lambda}^{el}:=\frac{b_{\lambda}(q,t)}{b_{\lambda}^{el}},\qquad\overline{b}_{\lambda}^{oa}:=\frac{b_{\lambda}(q,t)}{b_{\lambda}^{oa}}.

For the even and conjugate-even boundary types we use the same
b
-weights:

b_{\lambda}^{deel}:=b_{\lambda}^{el},\qquad b_{\lambda}^{eoa}:=b_{\lambda}^{oa},

and

\overline{b}_{\lambda}^{deel}:=\overline{b}_{\lambda}^{el},\qquad\overline{b}_{\lambda}^{eoa}:=\overline{b}_{\lambda}^{oa}.

Let

(2.5)

\mathbf{1}_{c}(\lambda):=\begin{cases}1,&c\in\{el,oa\},\\
\mathbf{1}_{\{\lambda\ {\rm conjugate\text{-}even}\}},&c=deel,\\
\mathbf{1}_{\{\lambda\ {\rm even}\}},&c=eoa.\end{cases}

For a single-variable specialization
[x]
and its dual specialization
[x]^{\prime}
, the skew Macdonald symmetric functions are given by (see the remarks on p. 346 of
[
12
]
)

(2.6)

\displaystyle P_{\lambda/\mu}([x];q,t)

\displaystyle=\delta_{\mu\prec\lambda}\,\psi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)x^{|\lambda|-|\mu|},

\displaystyle Q_{\lambda/\mu}([x];q,t)

\displaystyle=\delta_{\mu\prec\lambda}\,\varphi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)x^{|\lambda|-|\mu|},

and

(2.7)

\displaystyle P_{\lambda/\mu}([x]^{\prime};q,t)

\displaystyle=\delta_{\mu^{\prime}\prec\lambda^{\prime}}\,\varphi^{\prime}_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)x^{|\lambda|-|\mu|},

\displaystyle Q_{\lambda/\mu}([x]^{\prime};q,t)

\displaystyle=\delta_{\mu^{\prime}\prec\lambda^{\prime}}\,\psi^{\prime}_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)x^{|\lambda|-|\mu|}.

Here
\psi
,
\varphi
,
\psi^{\prime}
, and
\varphi^{\prime}
are given on p. 341 of
[
12
]
:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)

\displaystyle=\prod_{s\in C_{\lambda/\mu}}\frac{b_{\lambda}(s;q,t)}{b_{\mu}(s;q,t)},

\displaystyle\psi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)

\displaystyle=\prod_{s\in R_{\lambda/\mu}\setminus C_{\lambda/\mu}}\frac{b_{\mu}(s;q,t)}{b_{\lambda}(s;q,t)},

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)

\displaystyle=\prod_{s\in R_{\lambda/\mu}}\frac{b_{\mu}(s;q,t)}{b_{\lambda}(s;q,t)}=\prod_{s\in C_{\lambda^{\prime}/\mu^{\prime}}}\frac{b_{\lambda^{\prime}}(s;t,q)}{b_{\mu^{\prime}}(s;t,q)}=\varphi_{\lambda^{\prime}/\mu^{\prime}}(t,q),

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)

\displaystyle=\prod_{s\in C_{\lambda/\mu}\setminus R_{\lambda/\mu}}\frac{b_{\lambda}(s;q,t)}{b_{\mu}(s;q,t)}=\psi_{\lambda^{\prime}/\mu^{\prime}}(t,q),

where
C_{\lambda/\mu}
(respectively,
R_{\lambda/\mu}
) denotes the union of columns (respectively, rows) intersecting
\lambda/\mu
.
The coefficients
\psi,\varphi,\psi^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}
are the standard Macdonald
branching coefficients; they are products of
b_{\nu}(s;q,t)
-ratios and are
positive when
0<q,t<1
.

Let

\boldsymbol{\mu}=(\mu^{(l)},\mu^{(l+1)},\ldots,\mu^{(r+1)})

be a sequence of partitions satisfying the local interlacing relations determined by

RYG(l,r,a,b)
(See Lemma
3.1
):

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ll}(a_{i},b_{i})=(L,+):&\mu^{(i)}\prec\mu^{(i+1)},\\
(a_{i},b_{i})=(L,-):&\mu^{(i)}\succ\mu^{(i+1)},\\
(a_{i},b_{i})=(R,+):&\mu^{(i)}\prec^{\prime}\mu^{(i+1)},\\
(a_{i},b_{i})=(R,-):&\mu^{(i)}\succ^{\prime}\mu^{(i+1)}.\end{array}

Here
\prec
denotes horizontal-strip interlacing and
\prec^{\prime}
denotes the
corresponding vertical-strip interlacing after conjugation.

For such a sequence, set

\displaystyle A(\boldsymbol{\mu})

\displaystyle:=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[l..r]\\
(a_{i},b_{i})=(L,+)\end{subarray}}P_{\mu^{(i+1)}/\mu^{(i)}}([x_{i}];q,t)\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[l..r]\\
(a_{i},b_{i})=(L,-)\end{subarray}}Q_{\mu^{(i)}/\mu^{(i+1)}}([x_{i}];q,t)

(2.12)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[l..r]\\
(a_{i},b_{i})=(R,+)\end{subarray}}Q_{[\mu^{(i+1)}]^{\prime}/[\mu^{(i)}]^{\prime}}([x_{i}];t,q)\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[l..r]\\
(a_{i},b_{i})=(R,-)\end{subarray}}P_{[\mu^{(i)}]^{\prime}/[\mu^{(i+1)}]^{\prime}}([x_{i}];t,q).

Here
P,Q
are (skew) Macdonald polynomials; see Appendix
A
, (
2.6
), (
2.7
).

The unnormalized Macdonald weight of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

W_{c_{l},c_{r}}(\boldsymbol{\mu}):=\mathbf{1}_{c_{l}}(\mu^{(l)})\mathbf{1}_{c_{r}}(\mu^{(r+1)})\,u^{|\mu^{(l)}|}v^{|\mu^{(r+1)}|}\frac{b_{\mu^{(r+1)}}^{c_{r}}}{\overline{b}_{\mu^{(l)}}^{c_{l}}}A(\boldsymbol{\mu}).

The normalizing constant is

(2.13)

Z_{c_{l},c_{r}}:=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}W_{c_{l},c_{r}}(\boldsymbol{\mu}),

where the sum is over all partition sequences satisfying the above local interlacing
relations. Whenever

0<Z_{c_{l},c_{r}}<\infty,

we define the doubly free-boundary Macdonald rail-yard measure by

(2.14)

\mathbb{P}_{c_{l},c_{r}}(\boldsymbol{\mu}):=\frac{W_{c_{l},c_{r}}(\boldsymbol{\mu})}{Z_{c_{l},c_{r}}}.

Via Lemma
3.1
, this is also the probability measure
on the corresponding canonical free-boundary dimer states.

Assumption 2.5

(Piecewise periodic Jack scaling)
.

Let

\{RYG(l^{(\epsilon)},r^{(\epsilon)},a^{(\epsilon)},b^{(\epsilon)})\}_{\epsilon>0}

be a sequence of rail-yard graphs, and let
x_{i}^{(\epsilon)}
denote the weight of a
diagonal edge adjacent to an even vertex with abscissa
2i
.

(1)

Piecewise periodicity of the graph.

For a positive integer
n
and real numbers

V_{0}<V_{1}<\cdots<V_{m}
, we say that the sequence is
n
-periodic with transition
points
V_{0},\ldots,V_{m}
as
\epsilon\to 0
if the following hold.

(a)

For each
\epsilon>0
, there exist integer multiples of
n

l^{(\epsilon)}=v_{0}^{(\epsilon)}<v_{1}^{(\epsilon)}<\cdots<v_{m}^{(\epsilon)}=r^{(\epsilon)}

such that

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon v_{p}^{(\epsilon)}=V_{p},\qquad p\in\{0\}\cup[m].

(b)

The sequence
a^{(\epsilon)}
is
n
-periodic on

[l^{(\epsilon)}..r^{(\epsilon)}]
and does not depend on
\epsilon
.
More precisely, there exist
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\{L,R\}
such that

a_{i}^{(\epsilon)}=a_{i_{\equiv_{n}}}.

(c)

For each
p\in[m]
, the sequence
b^{(\epsilon)}
is
n
-periodic
on
(v_{p-1}^{(\epsilon)},v_{p}^{(\epsilon)})
and does not depend on

\epsilon
, although it may depend on
p
. More precisely, there exist

b_{p,1},\ldots,b_{p,n}\in\{+,-\}
such that for

i\in(v_{p-1}^{(\epsilon)},v_{p}^{(\epsilon)})
,

b_{i}^{(\epsilon)}=b_{p,i_{\equiv_{n}}}.

(2)

Periodicity of weights.

The weights
x_{i}^{(\epsilon)}
are periodic
q
-volume weights:

x_{i}^{(\epsilon)}=\begin{cases}e^{-\epsilon(i-i_{\equiv_{n}})}\tau_{k},&b_{i}^{(\epsilon)}=+,\\
e^{\epsilon(i-i_{\equiv_{n}})}\tau_{k}^{-1},&b_{i}^{(\epsilon)}=-,\end{cases}

where
k=i_{\equiv_{n}}
and
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{n}>0
are independent of

\epsilon
.

(3)

Exact Jack scaling and marked columns.

We work in the exact Jack specialization

(2.15)

q=t^{\alpha},\qquad\alpha>0,

and use the exact mesh parametrization

(2.16)

t=e^{-n\beta\epsilon},

where
\beta>0
is independent of
\epsilon
.

Let
s
be a positive integer. For each
d\in[s]
, assume that there exists a
sequence
i_{d}^{(\epsilon)}\in[l^{(\epsilon)}..r^{(\epsilon)}]
such that

(2.17)

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon i_{d}^{(\epsilon)}=\chi_{d},

with
\chi_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\chi_{s}
, and such that
i_{d}^{(\epsilon)}\bmod n
is
independent of
\epsilon
. Equivalently, there exist

i_{1}^{*},\ldots,i_{s}^{*}\in[n]
for which

\bigl(i_{d}^{(\epsilon)}\bigr)_{\equiv_{n}}=i_{d}^{*}.

When
s=1
, we suppress the index and write

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon i^{(\epsilon)}=\chi,\qquad\bigl(i^{(\epsilon)}\bigr)_{\equiv_{n}}=i^{*}.

For later use, we set

l^{(0)}:=V_{0},\qquad r^{(0)}:=V_{m}.

Slope-measure convention.

Let
F:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a right-continuous nondecreasing function.
Its
slope measure
in the
\kappa
-direction is the distributional
derivative
dF
, equivalently the positive Borel measure characterized by

dF((a,b])=F(b)-F(a),\qquad a<b.

Thus, if
F
is differentiable, then
dF=F^{\prime}(\kappa)\,d\kappa
; if
F

has a jump of size
J
at
\kappa_{0}
, then
dF
has an atom of mass

J
at
\kappa_{0}
.

In the theorem below,

H_{\epsilon}(\kappa)=\epsilon h_{M^{(\epsilon)}}^{(q,t)}\left(i^{(\epsilon)},\frac{\kappa}{\epsilon}\right)

is the rescaled height profile along a marked
L
-type column. By the
vertical-line formula (
2.2
), this height is twice the number of occupied
horizontal and diagonal edges below the observation height. Hence

H_{\epsilon}
is nondecreasing in
\kappa
. The lower reference phase fixes
the additive constant by making the normalized height equal to zero for all
sufficiently negative
\kappa
.

Assume the one-point admissibility condition of
Assumption
5.7
at the marked column
\chi
. set

(2.18)

\displaystyle S_{\chi}(w):=\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{r\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,r}(w),

where
\mathcal{G}_{\chi}
and
\mathcal{F}_{u,v,r}
are defined in
(
5.12
) and (
5.13
). On the admissible one-point contour

\mathcal{C}
of Proposition
5.11
, define

(2.19)

\displaystyle T_{\chi}(w):=\exp\!\left\{\beta\,\mathrm{Log}_{\chi,\mathcal{C}}S_{\chi}(w)\right\},

with the logarithm fixed by
Assumption
5.7
.

Theorem 2.6
.

(Laplace law of large numbers and weak slope-measure limit shape.)

Fix boundary types
c_{l},c_{r}\in\{el,oa,deel,eoa\}
and parameters

u,v\in(0,1)
. For each
\epsilon>0
, let
M^{(\epsilon)}
be sampled
from the doubly free-boundary Macdonald measure
\mathbb{P}_{c_{l},c_{r}}
in
(
2.14
). Suppose Assumption
2.5
holds, and suppose that the
one-point admissibility condition of
Assumption
5.7
holds at the marked column.

Let
i^{(\epsilon)}
be a marked
L
-type column satisfying (
2.17
),
with fixed residue class modulo
n
, and write

\epsilon i^{(\epsilon)}\to\chi\in(V_{0},V_{m})\setminus\{V_{1},\ldots,V_{m-1}\}.

In the charge-centered
(q,t)
-coordinate of
Definition
4.4
, set

H_{\epsilon}(\kappa):=\epsilon h^{(q,t)}_{M^{(\epsilon)}}\left(i^{(\epsilon)},\frac{\kappa}{\epsilon}\right).

Then, for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,

(2.20)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-n\beta k\kappa}H_{\epsilon}(\kappa)\,d\kappa\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}\frac{1}{n^{2}\alpha k^{2}\beta^{2}\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\mathcal{C}}T_{\chi}(w)^{k}\,\frac{dw}{w}.

Moreover, let
\nu_{\chi}^{(\epsilon)}
be the pushed-forward slope measure
defined by

(2.21)

\nu_{\chi}^{(\epsilon)}(B):=\int_{\{\kappa:\,e^{-n\beta\kappa}\in B\}}n\beta e^{-n\beta\kappa}\,dH_{\epsilon}(\kappa),\qquad B\subset[0,\infty).

Then
\nu_{\chi}^{(\epsilon)}
converges weakly in probability to a
deterministic compactly supported finite measure
\nu_{\chi}
, whose moments are

(2.22)

\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{k-1}\nu_{\chi}(dx)=\frac{1}{\alpha k\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\mathcal{C}}T_{\chi}(w)^{k}\,\frac{dw}{w},\qquad k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}.

The associated macroscopic height profile is

(2.23)

\mathcal{H}(\chi,\kappa):=\frac{1}{n\beta}\int_{(e^{-n\beta\kappa},\infty)}\frac{1}{x}\,\nu_{\chi}(dx).

Definition 2.7

(Regular liquid and frozen points)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}(\chi,\kappa)
be the deterministic limit shape obtained from
Theorem
2.6
. A point
(\chi,\kappa)
is called a
regular slope point
if

\chi\in(V_{0},V_{m})\setminus\{V_{1},\ldots,V_{m-1}\}

and the vertical slope

\partial_{\kappa}\mathcal{H}(\chi,\kappa)

is well defined at this point.

At a regular slope point, we call
(\chi,\kappa)

liquid
if

\partial_{\kappa}\mathcal{H}(\chi,\kappa)\in\left(0,\frac{2}{\alpha}\right),

and
frozen
if

\partial_{\kappa}\mathcal{H}(\chi,\kappa)\in\left\{0,\frac{2}{\alpha}\right\}.

The
frozen boundary
is the interface between liquid and frozen regular
slope points.

Theorem 2.8

(Regular liquid and frozen points in the separated all-
L
regime)
.

Fix boundary types

c_{l},c_{r}\in\{el,oa,deel,eoa\}

and parameters
u,v\in(0,1)
.
Suppose Assumption
2.5
holds and that the relevant one-point
contour and spectral-branch admissibility conditions of
Assumption
5.7
hold.
Use the limit shape
\mathcal{H}
supplied by
Theorem
2.6
.

Assume further that

\beta=1

and that Assumptions
6.6
–
6.7
hold.

Let

S_{\chi}(w)=\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{r\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,r}(w)

be defined with the compatible logarithm fixed by
Assumption
5.7
.
For
K\geq 1
, set

S_{\chi,K}(w):=\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{r=1}^{K}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,r}(w),\qquad C_{\chi,K}:=\lim_{w\to\infty}S_{\chi,K}(w),

where the latter limit is taken in the reduced rational expression.

Let
(\chi,\kappa)
be a regular slope point and put

x:=e^{-n\kappa}.

Assume that the following nonexceptionality conditions hold.

(i)

The Stieltjes boundary-value formula (
6.11
) holds at

x
, and the root labels and logarithms in
Lemma
6.13

admit continuation to
x+\mathbf{i}0
through the upper half-plane.

(ii)

S_{\chi}(0)\neq x.

(iii)

There exists
K_{*}\geq 1
such that

S_{\chi,K}(0)\neq x,\qquad C_{\chi,K}\neq x,\qquad K\geq K_{*}.

Then the characteristic equation

S_{\chi}(w)=x

has at most one nonreal conjugate pair, counted with multiplicity.

Moreover,

(\chi,\kappa)\text{ is liquid}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad S_{\chi}(w)=x\text{ has a unique nonreal conjugate pair}.

Writing its upper-half-plane member as
w_{+}
, one has

\frac{\partial\mathcal{H}(\chi,\kappa)}{\partial\kappa}=\frac{2}{\alpha}-\frac{2\arg w_{+}}{\pi\alpha},\qquad\arg w_{+}\in(0,\pi).

Equivalently,

(\chi,\kappa)\text{ is frozen}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad S_{\chi}(w)=x\text{ has only real roots}.

Let
\mathscr{N}
denote the set of regular slope points satisfying
conditions
(i)
–
(iii)
above. At every point of

\operatorname{int}\mathscr{N}
where the characteristic equation
has no multiple real root, the number of upper-half-plane roots is
locally constant. Consequently, the portion of the regular frozen
boundary lying in
\operatorname{int}\mathscr{N}
is contained in
the real multiple-root locus

(2.24)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle S_{\chi}(w)=e^{-n\kappa},\\[4.0pt]
\displaystyle\frac{d}{dw}\mathrm{Log}_{\chi,\mathcal{C}_{\chi}}S_{\chi}(w)=0,\end{cases}\qquad w\in\mathbb{R}.

At a nondegenerate fold point the multiple root has multiplicity
two, so (
2.24
) is the usual double-root system.

The next theorem describes finite-dimensional fluctuations of the
unrescaled height-Laplace transforms in the charge-centered

(q,t)
-column coordinate. For arbitrary piecewise-periodic

L/R
backgrounds, with all marked columns of
L
-type and
under the stated multipoint contour, branch, and normal-convergence
hypotheses, the limiting vector is Gaussian.

Theorem 2.9

(Annular Laplace-test Gaussian fluctuations)
.

Fix boundary types
c_{l},c_{r}\in\{el,oa,deel,eoa\}
and parameters
u,v\in(0,1)
. Let

\{RYG(l^{(\epsilon)},r^{(\epsilon)},a^{(\epsilon)},b^{(\epsilon)})\}_{\epsilon>0}
be a sequence of rail-yard graphs satisfying Assumption
2.5
. Assume that all marked columns are of
L
-type:

a_{i_{d}^{(\epsilon)}}^{(\epsilon)}=L,\qquad d\in[s],

for all sufficiently small
\epsilon
.
Assume further that the multipoint contour, branch, and
normal-convergence hypotheses used in Theorem
5.15
hold. For each
\epsilon>0
, let
M^{(\epsilon)}
be the random canonical free-boundary dimer state corresponding, via Lemma
3.1
, to a partition sequence sampled from (
2.14
).

Fix
s\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
and positive integers
k_{1},\ldots,k_{s}
. For each
d\in[s]
, choose a sequence
i_{d}^{(\epsilon)}\in[l^{(\epsilon)}..r^{(\epsilon)}]
such that

\epsilon i_{d}^{(\epsilon)}\to\chi_{d}\in(V_{0},V_{m})\setminus\{V_{1},\ldots,V_{m-1}\},\qquad(i_{d}^{(\epsilon)})_{\equiv_{n}}\ \text{is independent of }\epsilon.

In the definition below the coordinate-system height is the charge-centered height

h^{(q,t),\circ}_{M^{(\epsilon)}}(i_{d}^{(\epsilon)},\cdot)
from Definition
4.4
. Set

X_{k_{d}}^{(\epsilon)}(i_{d}^{(\epsilon)}):=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(h^{(q,t),\circ}_{M^{(\epsilon)}}\left(i_{d}^{(\epsilon)},\frac{\eta}{\epsilon}\right)-\mathbb{E}h^{(q,t),\circ}_{M^{(\epsilon)}}\left(i_{d}^{(\epsilon)},\frac{\eta}{\epsilon}\right)\right)e^{-n\beta k_{d}\eta}\,d\eta.

Then

\left(X_{k_{1}}^{(\epsilon)}(i_{1}^{(\epsilon)}),\ldots,X_{k_{s}}^{(\epsilon)}(i_{s}^{(\epsilon)})\right)\Longrightarrow\left(\mathcal{X}_{k_{1}}(\chi_{1}),\ldots,\mathcal{X}_{k_{s}}(\chi_{s})\right)

in distribution, where the limit is a centered Gaussian vector. Its covariance is

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}\bigl[\mathcal{X}_{k_{d}}(\chi_{d}),\mathcal{X}_{k_{h}}(\chi_{h})\bigr]

(2.25)

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{4}{\alpha n^{2}\beta^{2}k_{d}k_{h}(2\pi\mathbf{i})^{2}}\oint_{\mathcal{C}_{d}}\oint_{\mathcal{C}_{h}}\Phi_{\chi_{d},k_{d}}(z)\Phi_{\chi_{h},k_{h}}(w)\left[\frac{zw}{(z-w)^{2}}+\mathscr{B}_{u,v}(z,w)\right]\frac{dz}{z}\frac{dw}{w}.

Here

\Phi_{\chi,k}(z):=\bigl[\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(z)\bigr]^{k\beta}\prod_{r\geq 1}\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,r}(z)\bigr]^{k\beta},

\mathscr{B}_{u,v}
is defined in (
5.26
), and the contours are as in Theorem
5.15
; in particular they are chosen so that all four series defining
\mathscr{B}_{u,v}(z,w)
converge normally on
\mathcal{C}_{d}\times\mathcal{C}_{h}
. If
\chi_{d}=\chi_{h}
, the two contours are taken to be disjoint nested contours around the same pole set. By Definition
5.13
and Proposition
5.14
, the bracketed kernel in (
2.25
) is the logarithmic-derivative annular free-boundary image covariance kernel on the Laplace-test class.

Remark 2.10
.

The phrase “annular free-boundary image covariance” is used in the precise image-kernel sense of Definition
5.13
. The terms involving
u^{2}zw
and
v^{2}/(zw)
are the two free-boundary images. Positivity of the covariance matrix for the test observables follows from the proof in Section
7
: for every finite collection of observables, the displayed matrix is the limit of covariance matrices of the prelimit centered random variables.

3.
Partition Function

In this section, we compute the partition function of perfect matchings on doubly free-boundary rail-yard graphs with Macdonald weights. For general positive edge weights, the corresponding partition function was computed in
[
11
]
, inspired by the partition-function computation for doubly free-boundary steep tilings in
[
6
]
. In the Macdonald-weighted setting, however, several additional combinatorial identities for Macdonald polynomials are needed; these will be established below.

3.1.
Dimer Coverings and Interlacing Partitions

The free-boundary dimer-covering definition of Definition
2.1
, together with the particle–hole convention of Definition
2.3
, is formulated at the level of edge subsets and boundary Maya states. With this convention every free-boundary dimer covering determines the boundary partitions
\lambda^{(M,l)}
and
\lambda^{(M,r+1)}
, and conversely these boundary partitions determine the states of all unmatched boundary vertices.

Lemma 3.1

(Rail-yard dimer–partition correspondence)
.

Under the particle–hole convention of Definition
2.3
, a
free-boundary dimer covering
M
of
RYG(l,r,a,b)
determines a sequence
of partitions

\bigl(\lambda^{(M,l)},\lambda^{(M,l+1)},\ldots,\lambda^{(M,r+1)}\bigr)\in\mathbb{Y}^{r-l+2}.

This correspondence is bijective between free-boundary dimer coverings and
partition sequences

(\mu^{(l)},\mu^{(l+1)},\ldots,\mu^{(r+1)})

satisfying, for every
i\in[l..r]
, the local interlacing rule (
2.3
).

If boundary labels
c_{l},c_{r}\in\{el,oa,deel,eoa\}
are imposed, the same
bijection is restricted by the endpoint conditions

\mathbf{1}_{c_{l}}(\mu^{(l)})=\mathbf{1}_{c_{r}}(\mu^{(r+1)})=1,

where
\mathbf{1}_{c}
is the boundary indicator defined in
Section
2.3
. Equivalently,
deel
imposes
that the conjugate endpoint partition is even,
eoa
imposes that the
endpoint partition itself is even, and
el,oa
impose no parity restriction.

Proof.

The local rail-yard correspondence is the transfer-matrix correspondence of

[
5
, Equations (12)–(14), (19)–(20), Proposition 8]
. In that
statement, an admissible rail-yard covering with prescribed left and right
Maya boundary states is encoded by a matrix element

\langle l|\Gamma_{a_{l}b_{l}}(x_{l})\Gamma_{a_{l+1}b_{l+1}}(x_{l+1})\cdots\Gamma_{a_{r}b_{r}}(x_{r})|r\rangle.

The four operators
\Gamma_{L+},\Gamma_{L-},\Gamma_{R+},\Gamma_{R-}

act on partitions exactly by the four horizontal- or vertical-strip
interlacing relations displayed above.

Our free-boundary convention only changes how unmatched boundary odd vertices
are completed into Maya diagrams. By Definition
2.3
, unmatched
left-boundary odd vertices are treated as particles and unmatched right-boundary
odd vertices as holes; hence the two boundary columns also determine ordinary
Maya diagrams and therefore endpoint partitions. Thus the same column-by-column
bijection applies with arbitrary endpoint partitions

\mu^{(l)}
and
\mu^{(r+1)}
.

The conditions attached to
deel
and
eoa
are not additional local matching
rules. They are endpoint support restrictions coming from the Macdonald
free-boundary weights and are encoded by the indicators

\mathbf{1}_{c_{l}}
and
\mathbf{1}_{c_{r}}
.
∎

We define the
(q,t)
-charge on column
2m-1
by

\displaystyle c^{(M,m,q,t)}

\displaystyle=\bigl[\text{number of particles on column }(2m-1)\text{ in the upper half-plane}\bigr]

(3.1)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{\log q}{\log t}\bigl[\text{number of holes on column }(2m-1)\text{ in the lower half-plane}\bigr].

For partitions
\lambda
and
\mu
, we write
\lambda\supseteq\mu
if
\lambda_{i}\geq\mu_{i}
for all
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Let
c_{l},c_{r}\in\{el,oa,deel,eoa\}
.

Consider a sequence of partitions

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}=(\mu^{(l)},\mu^{(l+1)},\ldots,\mu^{(r+1)})\in{\mathbb{Y}}^{r-l+2}

satisfying:

(a)

the local interlacing rule (
2.3
);

(b)

If
c_{l}=deel
(respectively,
c_{r}=deel
), then
[\mu^{(l)}]^{\prime}
(respectively,
[\mu^{(r+1)}]^{\prime}
) is even;

(c)

If
c_{l}=eoa
(respectively,
c_{r}=eoa
), then
\mu^{(l)}
(respectively,
\mu^{(r+1)}
) is even.

Set

(3.2)

\mathfrak{Z}_{c_{l},c_{r}}(z):=\sum_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{Y}}}\mathbf{1}_{c_{l}}(\lambda)\mathbf{1}_{c_{r}}(\lambda)\frac{b_{\lambda}^{c_{r}}}{\overline{b}_{\lambda}^{c_{l}}}z^{|\lambda|}.

We now derive a factorized formula for
Z_{c_{l},c_{r}}
; defined as in (
2.13
). The formula is explicit up to the terminal free-boundary normalization
\mathfrak{Z}_{c_{l},c_{r}}(uv)
defined in (
3.2
), which is carried along as a separate boundary factor. We begin with several standard identities for Macdonald polynomials.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let

\displaystyle(a;q)_{\infty}:=\prod_{r=0}^{\infty}(1-aq^{r}).

Let
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots)
and
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots)
. The following identities appear on p. 349, 4(ii) and 4(iv), of
[
12
]
:

(3.3)

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda}b_{\lambda}^{el}P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\prod_{i}\frac{(tx_{i};q)_{\infty}}{(x_{i};q)_{\infty}}\prod_{i<j}\frac{(tx_{i}x_{j};q)_{\infty}}{(x_{i}x_{j};q)_{\infty}}:=\Theta_{el}(\mathbf{x}),

(3.4)

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda}b_{\lambda}^{oa}P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\prod_{i}\frac{(qtx_{i}^{2};q^{2})_{\infty}}{(1-x_{i})(q^{2}x_{i}^{2};q^{2})_{\infty}}\prod_{i<j}\frac{(tx_{i}x_{j};q)_{\infty}}{(x_{i}x_{j};q)_{\infty}}:=\Theta_{oa}(\mathbf{x}).

The following identities appear on p. 349, 4(i) and 4(iii), of
[
12
]
:

(3.5)

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda:\,\lambda^{\prime}\ \mathrm{even}}b_{\lambda}^{el}P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\prod_{i<j}\frac{(tx_{i}x_{j};q)_{\infty}}{(x_{i}x_{j};q)_{\infty}}:=\Theta_{de,el}(\mathbf{x}),

(3.6)

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda:\,\lambda\ \mathrm{even}}b_{\lambda}^{oa}P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\prod_{i}\frac{(qtx_{i}^{2};q^{2})_{\infty}}{(x_{i}^{2};q^{2})_{\infty}}\prod_{i<j}\frac{(tx_{i}x_{j};q)_{\infty}}{(x_{i}x_{j};q)_{\infty}}:=\Theta_{e,oa}(\mathbf{x}).

Define

(3.7)

\displaystyle\Pi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y};q,t):=\prod_{i,j}\frac{(tx_{i}y_{j};q)_{\infty}}{(x_{i}y_{j};q)_{\infty}}.

The Cauchy identity on p. 324, (4.13), of
[
12
]
is

(3.8)

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda}P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)Q_{\lambda}(\mathbf{y};q,t)=\Pi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y};q,t).

The identities on p. 323, (7.1
′
), and p. 324, (7.5), of
[
12
]
are

(3.9)

\displaystyle P_{\mu}P_{\nu}=\sum_{\lambda}f_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}P_{\lambda},

(3.10)

\displaystyle Q_{\lambda/\mu}=\sum_{\nu}f_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}Q_{\nu}.

Finally,

(3.11)

\displaystyle Q_{\lambda/\mu}=\frac{b_{\lambda}(q,t)}{b_{\mu}(q,t)}P_{\lambda/\mu}.

Lemma 3.3
.

(3.12)

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda}b_{\lambda}^{el}P_{\lambda/\eta}(\mathbf{x};q,t)

\displaystyle=\Theta_{el}(\mathbf{x})\sum_{\mu}b_{\mu}^{el}Q_{\eta/\mu}(\mathbf{x};q,t),

(3.13)

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda}b_{\lambda}^{oa}P_{\lambda/\eta}(\mathbf{x};q,t)

\displaystyle=\Theta_{oa}(\mathbf{x})\sum_{\mu}b_{\mu}^{oa}Q_{\eta/\mu}(\mathbf{x};q,t).

Proof.

We use
*
to denote either
el
or
oa
, and let
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots)
. By (
3.3
), (
3.4
), and (
3.8
),

\displaystyle\Theta_{*}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\phi}b_{\phi}^{*}P_{\phi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\Theta_{*}(\mathbf{x})\Theta_{*}(\mathbf{y})\Pi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y};q,t)

\displaystyle=\Theta_{*}(\mathbf{x})\left[\sum_{\mu}b_{\mu}^{*}P_{\mu}(\mathbf{y};q,t)\right]\left[\sum_{\phi}P_{\phi}(\mathbf{x};q,t)Q_{\phi}(\mathbf{y};q,t)\right].

Using (
3.9
) and (
3.10
), we obtain

\displaystyle\Theta_{*}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\Theta_{*}(\mathbf{x})\sum_{\mu,\phi}b_{\mu}^{*}Q_{\phi}(\mathbf{x};q,t)P_{\mu}(\mathbf{y};q,t)P_{\phi}(\mathbf{y};q,t)

\displaystyle=\Theta_{*}(\mathbf{x})\sum_{\mu,\phi}b_{\mu}^{*}Q_{\phi}(\mathbf{x};q,t)\sum_{\lambda}f_{\mu\phi}^{\lambda}P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{y};q,t)

\displaystyle=\Theta_{*}(\mathbf{x})\sum_{\mu,\lambda}b_{\mu}^{*}P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{y};q,t)\sum_{\phi}f_{\mu\phi}^{\lambda}Q_{\phi}(\mathbf{x};q,t)

(3.14)

\displaystyle=\Theta_{*}(\mathbf{x})\sum_{\mu,\lambda}b_{\mu}^{*}P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{y};q,t)Q_{\lambda/\mu}(\mathbf{x};q,t).

On the other hand,

(3.15)

\displaystyle\Theta_{*}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\sum_{\phi}b_{\phi}^{*}P_{\phi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\sum_{\phi,\lambda}b_{\phi}^{*}P_{\phi/\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{y};q,t).

Comparing the coefficients of
P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{y};q,t)
in (
3.14
) and (
3.15
) yields

\displaystyle\sum_{\phi}b_{\phi}^{*}P_{\phi/\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)=\Theta_{*}(\mathbf{x})\sum_{\mu}b_{\mu}^{*}Q_{\lambda/\mu}(\mathbf{x};q,t),

which is exactly the claim.
∎

The following identities were proved in Proposition 2.1 of
[
2
]
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}):=\sum_{\mu^{\prime}\ \mathrm{even}}b_{\mu}^{el}Q_{\lambda/\mu}(\mathbf{x}).

Then

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu^{\prime}\ \mathrm{even}}b_{\nu}^{el}P_{\nu/\lambda}(\mathbf{x})=\Theta_{de,el}(\mathbf{x})\sum_{\mu^{\prime}\ \mathrm{even}}b_{\mu}^{el}Q_{\lambda/\mu}(\mathbf{x})=\Theta_{de,el}(\mathbf{x})\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}).

Lemma 3.5
.

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu\ \mathrm{even}}b_{\nu}^{oa}P_{\nu/\lambda}(\mathbf{x})=\Theta_{e,oa}(\mathbf{x})\sum_{\mu\ \mathrm{even}}b_{\mu}^{oa}Q_{\lambda/\mu}(\mathbf{x}).

Proof.

The proof is the same as that of Lemma
3.3
, except that one sums only over even partitions at the relevant steps.
∎

Corollary 3.6
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{C}
, let
v\in\mathbb{C}
with
|v|<1
, and let
*\in\{el,oa\}
. Then

(3.16)

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda}b_{\lambda}^{*}P_{\lambda/\eta}([x];q,t)v^{|\lambda|}

\displaystyle=\Theta_{*}(vx)\sum_{\mu}b_{\mu}^{*}Q_{\eta/\mu}([v^{2}x];q,t)v^{|\mu|},

(3.17)

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\ \mathrm{even}}b_{\lambda}^{oa}P_{\lambda/\eta}([x];q,t)v^{|\lambda|}

\displaystyle=\Theta_{e,oa}(vx)\sum_{\mu\ \mathrm{even}}b_{\mu}^{oa}Q_{\eta/\mu}([v^{2}x];q,t)v^{|\mu|},

(3.18)

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}\ \mathrm{even}}b_{\lambda}^{el}P_{\lambda/\eta}([x];q,t)v^{|\lambda|}

\displaystyle=\Theta_{de,el}(vx)\sum_{\mu^{\prime}\ \mathrm{even}}b_{\mu}^{el}Q_{\eta/\mu}([v^{2}x];q,t)v^{|\mu|}.

Proof.

By (
3.12
) and (
3.13
),

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda}b_{\lambda}^{*}P_{\lambda/\eta}([x];q,t)v^{|\lambda|}

\displaystyle=v^{|\eta|}\sum_{\lambda}b_{\lambda}^{*}P_{\lambda/\eta}([vx];q,t)

\displaystyle=v^{|\eta|}\Theta_{*}(vx)\sum_{\mu}b_{\mu}^{*}Q_{\eta/\mu}([vx];q,t)

\displaystyle=\Theta_{*}(vx)\sum_{\mu}b_{\mu}^{*}Q_{\eta/\mu}([v^{2}x];q,t)v^{|\mu|}.

This proves (
3.16
). The identities (
3.17
) and (
3.18
) follow similarly from Lemmas
3.5
and
3.4
, respectively.
∎

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
*\in\{el,oa\}
. Then

(3.19)

\displaystyle\sum_{\phi}\frac{1}{\overline{b}_{\phi}^{*}}Q_{\phi/\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)

\displaystyle=\Theta_{*}(\mathbf{x};q,t)\sum_{\mu}\frac{1}{\overline{b}_{\mu}^{*}}P_{\lambda/\mu}(\mathbf{x};q,t),

(3.20)

\displaystyle\sum_{\phi\ \mathrm{even}}\frac{1}{\overline{b}_{\phi}^{oa}}Q_{\phi/\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)

\displaystyle=\Theta_{e,oa}(\mathbf{x};q,t)\sum_{\mu\ \mathrm{even}}\frac{1}{\overline{b}_{\mu}^{oa}}P_{\lambda/\mu}(\mathbf{x};q,t),

(3.21)

\displaystyle\sum_{\phi^{\prime}\ \mathrm{even}}\frac{1}{\overline{b}_{\phi}^{el}}Q_{\phi/\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)

\displaystyle=\Theta_{de,el}(\mathbf{x};q,t)\sum_{\mu^{\prime}\ \mathrm{even}}\frac{1}{\overline{b}_{\mu}^{el}}P_{\lambda/\mu}(\mathbf{x};q,t).

Proof.

We prove only (
3.19
); the proofs of (
3.20
) and (
3.21
) are analogous. By (
3.11
),

\displaystyle\sum_{\phi}\frac{1}{\overline{b}_{\phi}^{*}}Q_{\phi/\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)=\sum_{\phi}\frac{1}{\overline{b}_{\phi}^{*}}\frac{b_{\phi}(q,t)}{b_{\lambda}(q,t)}P_{\phi/\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t).

Using (
2.4
), this becomes

\displaystyle\sum_{\phi}\frac{1}{\overline{b}_{\phi}^{*}}Q_{\phi/\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)=\frac{1}{b_{\lambda}(q,t)}\sum_{\phi}b_{\phi}^{*}P_{\phi/\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t).

Applying Lemma
3.3
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\phi}\frac{1}{\overline{b}_{\phi}^{*}}Q_{\phi/\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)=\frac{1}{b_{\lambda}(q,t)}\Theta_{*}(\mathbf{x})\sum_{\mu}b_{\mu}^{*}Q_{\lambda/\mu}(\mathbf{x};q,t),

and a second use of (
3.11
) yields (
3.19
).
∎

Corollary 3.8
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{C}
, let
v\in\mathbb{C}
with
|v|<1
, and let
*\in\{el,oa\}
. Then

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda}\frac{1}{\overline{b}_{\lambda}^{*}}Q_{\lambda/\eta}([x];q,t)v^{|\lambda|}

\displaystyle=\Theta_{*}(vx)\sum_{\mu}\frac{1}{\overline{b}_{\mu}^{*}}P_{\eta/\mu}([v^{2}x];q,t)v^{|\mu|},

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\ \mathrm{even}}\frac{1}{\overline{b}_{\lambda}^{oa}}Q_{\lambda/\eta}([x];q,t)v^{|\lambda|}

\displaystyle=\Theta_{e,oa}(vx)\sum_{\mu\ \mathrm{even}}\frac{1}{\overline{b}_{\mu}^{oa}}P_{\eta/\mu}([v^{2}x];q,t)v^{|\mu|},

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}\ \mathrm{even}}\frac{1}{\overline{b}_{\lambda}^{el}}Q_{\lambda/\eta}([x];q,t)v^{|\lambda|}

\displaystyle=\Theta_{de,el}(vx)\sum_{\mu^{\prime}\ \mathrm{even}}\frac{1}{\overline{b}_{\mu}^{el}}P_{\eta/\mu}([v^{2}x];q,t)v^{|\mu|}.

Proof.

The proof repeats the argument of Corollary
3.6
with Lemma
3.7
replacing Lemma
3.3
. The only change is that the conjugate-even boundary specialization is used at each reflection step; the Cauchy factors and terminal parity indicator are otherwise unchanged.
∎

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
\lambda,\mu\in{\mathbb{Y}}
. Then

\displaystyle\sum_{\phi}P_{\phi/\lambda}(x;q,t)Q_{\phi/\mu}(y;q,t)

\displaystyle=\Pi(x,y;q,t)\sum_{\sigma}P_{\mu/\sigma}(x;q,t)Q_{\lambda/\sigma}(y;q,t),

\displaystyle\sum_{\phi}Q_{\phi^{\prime}/\lambda^{\prime}}(x;t,q)Q_{\phi/\mu}(y;q,t)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{0}(x,y)\sum_{\sigma}Q_{\mu^{\prime}/\sigma^{\prime}}(x;t,q)Q_{\lambda/\sigma}(y;q,t),

\displaystyle\sum_{\phi}P_{\phi/\lambda}(x;q,t)P_{\phi^{\prime}/\mu^{\prime}}(y;t,q)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{0}(x,y)\sum_{\sigma}P_{\mu/\sigma}(x;q,t)P_{\lambda^{\prime}/\sigma^{\prime}}(y;t,q),

where

(3.22)

\displaystyle\Pi_{0}(x,y)=\prod_{i,j}(1+x_{i}y_{j}).

Proof.

See p. 352 of
[
12
]
.
∎

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
\lambda,\mu\in{\mathbb{Y}}
. Then

(3.23)

\displaystyle\sum_{\phi}P_{\phi/\lambda}(x;q,t)P_{\mu/\phi}(y;q,t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu}P_{\nu/\lambda}(y;q,t)P_{\mu/\nu}(x;q,t),

(3.24)

\displaystyle\sum_{\phi}P_{\phi/\lambda}(x;q,t)Q_{\mu^{\prime}/\phi^{\prime}}(y;t,q)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu}Q_{\nu^{\prime}/\lambda^{\prime}}(y;t,q)P_{\mu/\nu}(x;q,t).

Proof.

Since the skew Macdonald polynomial
P_{\mu/\lambda}(x,y;q,t)
is symmetric in
x
and
y
,

\displaystyle P_{\mu/\lambda}(x,y;q,t)=\sum_{\phi}P_{\phi/\lambda}(x;q,t)P_{\mu/\phi}(y;q,t)=\sum_{\nu}P_{\nu/\lambda}(y;q,t)P_{\mu/\nu}(x;q,t),

which proves (
3.23
). Then (
3.24
) follows by applying
\omega_{q,t}
to the variable
y
.
∎

For a symmetric function
f\in\Lambda_{X}
, write
f(\rho):=\rho(f)
. A specialization
\rho
is determined by its generating function

\displaystyle H(\rho;z;q,t):=\exp\left(\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{1-t^{n}}{1-q^{n}}\frac{p_{n}(\rho)z^{n}}{n}\right).

If
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
are specializations and
c\in\mathbb{C}
, define new specializations
\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}
,
c*\rho
, and
c\rho
by

\displaystyle H(\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2};z)

\displaystyle:=H(\rho_{1};z)H(\rho_{2};z),

\displaystyle H(c*\rho;z)

\displaystyle:=H(\rho;z)^{c},

\displaystyle H(c\rho;z)

\displaystyle:=H(\rho;cz).

Equivalently, for all
n\geq 1
,

(3.25)

\displaystyle p_{n}(\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=p_{n}(\rho_{1})+p_{n}(\rho_{2}),

\displaystyle p_{n}(c*\rho)

\displaystyle=c\,p_{n}(\rho),

\displaystyle p_{n}(c\rho)

\displaystyle=c^{n}p_{n}(\rho).

Define also

(3.26)

\displaystyle H(\rho_{1};\rho_{2};q,t)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{Y}}}P_{\lambda}(\rho_{1})Q_{\lambda}(\rho_{2})=\exp\left(\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{1-t^{n}}{1-q^{n}}\frac{p_{n}(\rho_{1})p_{n}(\rho_{2})}{n}\right).

In particular, if
\rho_{2}
is the specialization of a single variable
z
, then
H(\rho_{1};\rho_{2};q,t)=H(\rho_{1};z;q,t)
. Moreover,

\displaystyle H(\rho;\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2};q,t)=H(\rho;\rho_{1};q,t)H(\rho;\rho_{2};q,t).

The following formulas for
\Theta_{el}
and
\Theta_{oa}
will be useful.

Lemma 3.11
.

Assume
q,t,x_{i}\in(0,1)
. Then

(3.27)

\displaystyle\Theta_{el}(\rho;q,t)

\displaystyle=\exp\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1-t^{n}}{1-q^{n}}\left(\frac{\rho(p_{2n-1})}{2n-1}+\frac{[\rho(p_{n})]^{2}}{2n}\right)\right],

(3.28)

\displaystyle\Theta_{oa}(\rho;q,t)

\displaystyle=\exp\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{q^{n}(q^{n}-t^{n})\rho(p_{2n})}{n(1-q^{2n})}+\frac{\rho(p_{n})}{n}+\frac{1-t^{n}}{1-q^{n}}\frac{[\rho(p_{n})]^{2}-\rho(p_{2n})}{2n}\right],

(3.29)

\displaystyle\Theta_{de,el}(\rho;q,t)

\displaystyle=\exp\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1-t^{n}}{1-q^{n}}\left(-\frac{\rho(p_{2n})}{2n}+\frac{[\rho(p_{n})]^{2}}{2n}\right)\right],

(3.30)

\displaystyle\Theta_{e,oa}(\rho;q,t)

\displaystyle=\exp\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(1-t^{n}q^{n})\rho(p_{2n})}{n(1-q^{2n})}+\frac{1-t^{n}}{1-q^{n}}\frac{[\rho(p_{n})]^{2}-\rho(p_{2n})}{2n}\right].

Proof.

Starting from (
3.3
), we compute

\displaystyle\Theta_{el}(\rho;q,t)

\displaystyle=\exp\Biggl[\sum_{i}\sum_{r\geq 0}\bigl(\log(1-tx_{i}q^{r})-\log(1-x_{i}q^{r})\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum_{i<j}\sum_{r\geq 0}\bigl(\log(1-tx_{i}x_{j}q^{r})-\log(1-x_{i}x_{j}q^{r})\bigr)\Biggr]

\displaystyle=\exp\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1-t^{n}}{1-q^{n}}\left(\sum_{i}\frac{x_{i}^{n}}{n}+\sum_{i<j}\frac{(x_{i}x_{j})^{n}}{n}\right)\right],

which simplifies to (
3.27
). The proofs of (
3.28
), (
3.29
), and (
3.30
) are obtained in the same way from (
3.4
), (
3.5
), and (
3.6
), respectively.
∎

Lemma
3.11
has the following immediate consequence.

Corollary 3.12
.

With the notation in (
3.25
), for
*\in\{el,oa,deel,eoa\}
,

\displaystyle\Theta_{*}(\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2};q,t)=\Theta_{*}(\rho_{1};q,t)\Theta_{*}(\rho_{2};q,t)H(\rho_{1};\rho_{2};q,t).

Applying Lemma
3.11
together with the identity

\displaystyle\omega_{q,t}(p_{n})=(-1)^{n-1}\frac{1-q^{n}}{1-t^{n}}p_{n},

we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 3.13
.

For any specialization
\rho
,

(3.31)

\displaystyle\Theta_{el}(\omega_{q,t}\rho;q,t)

\displaystyle=\exp\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1-t^{n}}{1-q^{n}}\left(\frac{1-q^{2n-1}}{1-t^{2n-1}}\frac{\rho(p_{2n-1})}{2n-1}+\left(\frac{1-q^{n}}{1-t^{n}}\right)^{2}\frac{[\rho(p_{n})]^{2}}{2n}\right)\right],

\displaystyle\Theta_{oa}(\omega_{q,t}\rho;q,t)

\displaystyle=\exp\left[-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{q^{n}(q^{n}-t^{n})\rho(p_{2n})}{n(1-t^{2n})}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n+1}(1-q^{n})}{1-t^{n}}\frac{\rho(p_{n})}{n}\right.

(3.32)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\left.+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1-q^{n}}{1-t^{n}}\frac{[\rho(p_{n})]^{2}}{2n}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1+q^{n}}{1+t^{n}}\frac{\rho(p_{2n})}{2n}\right],

(3.33)

\displaystyle\Theta_{de,el}(\omega_{q,t}\rho;q,t)

\displaystyle=\exp\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1+q^{n}}{1+t^{n}}\frac{\rho(p_{2n})}{2n}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1-q^{n}}{1-t^{n}}\frac{[\rho(p_{n})]^{2}}{2n}\right],

(3.34)

\displaystyle\Theta_{e,oa}(\omega_{q,t}\rho;q,t)

\displaystyle=\exp\left[-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(1-t^{n}q^{n})\rho(p_{2n})}{n(1-t^{2n})}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1+q^{n}}{1+t^{n}}\frac{\rho(p_{2n})}{2n}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1-q^{n}}{1-t^{n}}\frac{[\rho(p_{n})]^{2}}{2n}\right].

Proposition 3.14

(Factorized partition function with terminal boundary normalization)
.

Assume
u,v\in(0,1)
. Then the partition function is given by

\displaystyle Z_{c_{l},c_{r}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\leq i<j\leq r\\
b_{i}=+,\,b_{j}=-\end{subarray}}z_{i,j}\left[\prod_{n\geq 1}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[l..r]\\
(a_{i},b_{i})=(L,+)\end{subarray}}\Theta_{c_{r}}(u^{2n-2}v^{2n-1}x_{i})\Theta_{c_{l}}(u^{2n-1}v^{2n}x_{i})\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[l..r]\\
(a_{i},b_{i})=(R,+)\end{subarray}}\omega_{q,t}\bigl[\Theta_{c_{r}}(u^{2n-2}v^{2n-1}x_{i})\Theta_{c_{l}}(u^{2n-1}v^{2n}x_{i})\bigr]

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[l..r]\\
(a_{i},b_{i})=(L,-)\end{subarray}}\Theta_{c_{l}}(u^{2n-1}v^{2n-2}x_{i})\Theta_{c_{r}}(u^{2n}v^{2n-1}x_{i})

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\left.\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[l..r]\\
(a_{i},b_{i})=(R,-)\end{subarray}}\omega_{q,t}\bigl[\Theta_{c_{l}}(u^{2n-1}v^{2n-2}x_{i})\Theta_{c_{r}}(u^{2n}v^{2n-1}x_{i})\bigr]\right]\mathfrak{Z}_{c_{l},c_{r}}(uv)

(3.35)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\times\left[\prod_{n\geq 1}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\leq i,j\leq r\\
b_{i}=+,\,b_{j}=-,\,a_{i}\neq a_{j}\end{subarray}}(1+u^{2n}v^{2n}x_{i}x_{j})\right.

\displaystyle\left.\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\leq i,j\leq r\\
b_{i}=+,\,b_{j}=-,\,a_{i}=a_{j}\end{subarray}}\Pi(u^{2n}v^{2n}x_{i},u^{2n-2}v^{2n-2}x_{j};q,t)\Pi(u^{2n}v^{2n}x_{i},u^{2n}v^{2n}x_{j};q,t)\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\times\left[\prod_{n\geq 1}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\in[l..r]\\
b_{i}=b_{j}=+,\,a_{i}\neq a_{j}\end{subarray}}(1+u^{2n-2}v^{2n}x_{i}x_{j})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\in[l..r]\\
b_{i}=b_{j}=-,\,a_{i}\neq a_{j}\end{subarray}}(1+u^{2n}v^{2n-2}x_{i}x_{j})\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\times\left[\prod_{n\geq 1}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\in[l..r]\\
b_{i}=b_{j}=+,\,a_{i}=a_{j}\end{subarray}}\Pi(u^{2n-2}v^{2n}x_{i},u^{2n-2}v^{2n-2}x_{j};q,t)\Pi(u^{2n}v^{2n}x_{i},u^{2n-2}v^{2n}x_{j};q,t)\right.

(3.36)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\left.\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\in[l..r]\\
b_{i}=b_{j}=-,\,a_{i}=a_{j}\end{subarray}}\Pi(u^{2n}v^{2n-2}x_{i},u^{2n-2}v^{2n-2}x_{j};q,t)\Pi(u^{2n}v^{2n}x_{i},u^{2n}v^{2n-2}x_{j};q,t)\right].

where

(3.37)

\displaystyle z_{i,j}=\begin{cases}1+x_{i}x_{j},&a_{i}\neq a_{j},\\
\Pi(x_{i},x_{j};q,t),&a_{i}=a_{j}.\end{cases}

Moreover, the partition function for dimer coverings on a rail-yard graph
RYG(l,r,\underline{a},\underline{b})
with free boundary condition on the left and empty partition on the right is

\displaystyle Z_{c_{l},c_{r},f,\emptyset}

\displaystyle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\leq i<j\leq r\\
b_{i}=+,\,b_{j}=-\end{subarray}}z_{i,j}\left[\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[l..r]\\
(a_{i},b_{i})=(L,-)\end{subarray}}\Theta_{c_{l}}(ux_{i})\right]\left[\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[l..r]\\
(a_{i},b_{i})=(R,-)\end{subarray}}\omega_{q,t}\bigl[\Theta_{c_{l}}(ux_{i})\bigr]\right]

(3.38)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\in[l..r]\\
b_{i}=b_{j}=-,\,a_{i}\neq a_{j}\end{subarray}}(1+u^{2}x_{i}x_{j})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\in[l..r]\\
b_{i}=b_{j}=-,\,a_{i}=a_{j}\end{subarray}}\Pi(u^{2}x_{i},x_{j};q,t).

Proof.

Consider a sequence of partitions

\displaystyle(\mu^{(l)},\mu^{(l+1)},\ldots,\mu^{(r+1)})\in{\mathbb{Y}}^{r-l+2}.

Recall that the interlacing relations between consecutive partitions are prescribed by conditions (a)–(d) above. These interlacing relations satisfy nontrivial commutation relations (Lemma
3.9
), trivial commutation relations (Lemma
3.10
), and reflection relations (Corollary
3.8
).

The strategy is to commute the interlacing relations, or equivalently the
+
and
-
signs, until the partition function factors into an explicit product.

Step 1.
Commute the interlacing relations so that every relation in
\{\prec,\prec^{\prime}\}
lies to the right of every relation in
\{\succ,\succ^{\prime}\}
. By Lemma
3.9
, these commutations contribute the factor

(3.39)

\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\leq i<j\leq r\\
b_{i}=+,\,b_{j}=-\end{subarray}}z_{i,j},\qquad z_{i,j}=\begin{cases}1+x_{i}x_{j},&a_{i}\neq a_{j},\\
\Pi(x_{i},x_{j};q,t),&a_{i}=a_{j}.\end{cases}

Step 2.
For each
b_{i}=-
(from left to right), move it to the left boundary, reflect it there, move it to the right boundary, reflect it again, and finally return it to its original position.

By Corollary
3.8
, reflection at the left boundary contributes

\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[l..r]\\
(a_{i},b_{i})=(L,-)\end{subarray}}\Theta_{c_{l}}(ux_{i})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[l..r]\\
(a_{i},b_{i})=(R,-)\end{subarray}}\omega_{q,t}\Theta_{c_{l}}(ux_{i}).

After this reflection, each parameter
x_{i}
is multiplied by
u^{2}
. Reflection at the right boundary contributes

\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[l..r]\\
(a_{i},b_{i})=(L,-)\end{subarray}}\Theta_{c_{r}}(u^{2}vx_{i})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[l..r]\\
(a_{i},b_{i})=(R,-)\end{subarray}}\omega_{q,t}\Theta_{c_{r}}(u^{2}vx_{i}).

After reflection at the left boundary,
b_{i}=-
becomes
+
; when it moves to the right, it interchanges with all the other
-
signs. By Lemma
3.9
, these interchanges contribute

\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\in[l..r]\\
b_{i}=b_{j}=-,\,a_{i}=a_{j}\end{subarray}}\Pi(u^{2}x_{i},x_{j};q,t)\Pi(u^{2}v^{2}x_{i},u^{2}x_{j};q,t)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\in[l..r]\\
b_{i}=b_{j}=-,\,a_{i}\neq a_{j}\end{subarray}}(1+u^{2}x_{i}x_{j})(1+u^{4}v^{2}x_{i}x_{j}).

After reflection at the right boundary, the resulting
+
becomes
-
and, as it moves back to the left, it interchanges with all the
+
signs. Again by Lemma
3.9
, this contributes

\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in[l..r]\\
b_{i}=+,\,b_{j}=-,\,a_{i}=a_{j}\end{subarray}}\Pi(u^{2}v^{2}x_{i},x_{j};q,t)\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in[l..r]\\
b_{i}=+,\,b_{j}=-,\,a_{i}\neq a_{j}\end{subarray}}(1+u^{2}v^{2}x_{i}x_{j}).

Step 3.
For each
b_{i}=+
(from right to left), reflect it at the right boundary, move it to the left boundary, reflect it there, and finally return it to its original position.

By Corollary
3.8
, reflection at the right boundary contributes

\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[l..r]\\
(a_{i},b_{i})=(L,+)\end{subarray}}\Theta_{c_{r}}(vx_{i})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[l..r]\\
(a_{i},b_{i})=(R,+)\end{subarray}}\omega_{q,t}\Theta_{c_{r}}(vx_{i}).

After this reflection, each parameter
x_{i}
is multiplied by
v^{2}
. Reflection at the left boundary contributes

\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[l..r]\\
(a_{i},b_{i})=(L,+)\end{subarray}}\Theta_{c_{l}}(uv^{2}x_{i})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[l..r]\\
(a_{i},b_{i})=(R,+)\end{subarray}}\omega_{q,t}\Theta_{c_{l}}(uv^{2}x_{i}).

After reflection at the right boundary,
b_{i}=+
becomes
-
; when it moves to the left, it interchanges with all the other
+
signs. By Lemma
3.9
, this contributes

\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\in[l..r]\\
b_{i}=b_{j}=+,\,a_{i}=a_{j}\end{subarray}}\Pi(v^{2}x_{i},x_{j};q,t)\Pi(u^{2}v^{2}x_{i},v^{2}x_{j};q,t)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\in[l..r]\\
b_{i}=b_{j}=+,\,a_{i}\neq a_{j}\end{subarray}}(1+v^{2}x_{i}x_{j})(1+u^{2}v^{4}x_{i}x_{j}).

After reflection at the left boundary, the resulting
-
becomes
+
and, as it moves back to the right, it interchanges with all the
-
signs. This contributes

\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in[l..r]\\
b_{i}=+,\,b_{j}=-,\,a_{i}=a_{j}\end{subarray}}\Pi(u^{2}v^{2}x_{i},u^{2}v^{2}x_{j};q,t)\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in[l..r]\\
b_{i}=+,\,b_{j}=-,\,a_{i}\neq a_{j}\end{subarray}}(1+u^{4}v^{4}x_{i}x_{j}).

Step 4.
Repeat Steps 2 and 3. After repeating them
n
times, the
(n+1)
st repetition contributes

\displaystyle R_{n}

\displaystyle:=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[l..r]\\
(a_{i},b_{i})=(L,-)\end{subarray}}\Theta_{c_{l}}(u^{2n+1}v^{2n}x_{i})\Theta_{c_{r}}(u^{2n+2}v^{2n+1}x_{i})

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[l..r]\\
(a_{i},b_{i})=(R,-)\end{subarray}}\omega_{q,t}\Theta_{c_{l}}(u^{2n+1}v^{2n}x_{i})\,\omega_{q,t}\Theta_{c_{r}}(u^{2n+2}v^{2n+1}x_{i})

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\in[l..r]\\
b_{i}=b_{j}=-,\,a_{i}=a_{j}\end{subarray}}\Pi(u^{2n+2}v^{2n}x_{i},u^{2n}v^{2n}x_{j};q,t)\Pi(u^{2n+2}v^{2n+2}x_{i},u^{2n+2}v^{2n}x_{j};q,t)

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\in[l..r]\\
b_{i}=b_{j}=-,\,a_{i}\neq a_{j}\end{subarray}}(1+u^{4n+2}v^{4n}x_{i}x_{j})(1+u^{4n+4}v^{4n+2}x_{i}x_{j})

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in[l..r]\\
b_{i}=+,\,b_{j}=-,\,a_{i}=a_{j}\end{subarray}}\Pi(u^{2n+2}v^{2n+2}x_{i},u^{2n}v^{2n}x_{j};q,t)\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in[l..r]\\
b_{i}=+,\,b_{j}=-,\,a_{i}\neq a_{j}\end{subarray}}(1+u^{4n+2}v^{4n+2}x_{i}x_{j})

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[l..r]\\
(a_{i},b_{i})=(L,+)\end{subarray}}\Theta_{c_{r}}(u^{2n}v^{2n+1}x_{i})\Theta_{c_{l}}(u^{2n+1}v^{2n+2}x_{i})

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[l..r]\\
(a_{i},b_{i})=(R,+)\end{subarray}}\omega_{q,t}\Theta_{c_{r}}(u^{2n}v^{2n+1}x_{i})\,\omega_{q,t}\Theta_{c_{l}}(u^{2n+1}v^{2n+2}x_{i})

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\in[l..r]\\
b_{i}=b_{j}=+,\,a_{i}=a_{j}\end{subarray}}\Pi(u^{2n}v^{2n+2}x_{i},u^{2n}v^{2n}x_{j};q,t)\Pi(u^{2n+2}v^{2n+2}x_{i},u^{2n}v^{2n+2}x_{j};q,t)

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\in[l..r]\\
b_{i}=b_{j}=+,\,a_{i}\neq a_{j}\end{subarray}}(1+u^{4n}v^{4n+2}x_{i}x_{j})(1+u^{4n+2}v^{4n+4}x_{i}x_{j})

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in[l..r]\\
b_{i}=+,\,b_{j}=-,\,a_{i}=a_{j}\end{subarray}}\Pi(u^{2n+2}v^{2n+2}x_{i},u^{2n+2}v^{2n+2}x_{j};q,t)\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in[l..r]\\
b_{i}=+,\,b_{j}=-,\,a_{i}\neq a_{j}\end{subarray}}(1+u^{4n+4}v^{4n+4}x_{i}x_{j}).

After iterating Steps 2 and 3
n+1
times, the diagonal-edge weights become
u^{2n+2}v^{2n+2}x_{i}
. Since
u,v\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}u^{2n+2}v^{2n+2}x_{i}=0.

Therefore, in the limit only configurations with no diagonal edges contribute. Under free boundary conditions, this limiting contribution is

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Z}_{c_{l},c_{r}}(uv).

Hence,

\displaystyle Z_{c_{l},c_{r}}=\mathfrak{Z}_{c_{l},c_{r}}(uv)\prod_{n=0}^{\infty}R_{n},

which gives (
3.36
). Formula (
3.38
) follows from (
3.36
) by taking the limit
v\downarrow 0
.
∎

Remark 3.15

(Terminal boundary normalization)
.

The factor
\mathfrak{Z}_{c_{l},c_{r}}(uv)
in Proposition
3.14
is the terminal no-diagonal-edge free-boundary scalar product left after the reflection–commutation procedure. We keep it as a separate normalization factor rather than claim a closed product formula for it. This is enough for the normalized observable identities used below: the same terminal scalar product is part of the partition function and is therefore included in the normalization.

Lemma 3.16

(Terminal normalization cancels from normalized observables)
.

The terminal factor
\mathfrak{Z}_{c_{l},c_{r}}(uv)
in
Proposition
3.14
does not contribute to any normalized Neguţ
observable used in Theorems
2.6
,
5.15
, and
2.9
. More
precisely, in the unnormalized expectations obtained by inserting the
Neguţ operators and commuting the same boundary and interlacing operators,
the final no-diagonal-edge boundary scalar product is again

\mathfrak{Z}_{c_{l},c_{r}}(uv)
, independent of the contour variables. Hence it
appears both in the numerator and in the partition-function denominator and
cancels identically.

Proof.

The proof of Proposition
3.14
commutes all interlacing operators through
the two boundary reflections until the diagonal parameters have been multiplied
by powers of
uv
tending to zero. The remaining scalar product is exactly

\mathfrak{Z}_{c_{l},c_{r}}(uv)
. In the observable formulae, the Neguţ
operators act before this final terminal step and contribute the contour
factors displayed in Propositions
5.11
and Theorem
5.15
. After
those contour factors have been extracted, the same reflection–commutation
procedure leaves the same terminal no-diagonal-edge scalar product. Since the
probability expectation is normalized by
Z_{c_{l},c_{r}}
, which contains the
identical terminal factor by Proposition
3.14
, the factor cancels.
∎

4.
Laplace Transform of Height Function

In this section, we derive an integral formula for the expectation of the Laplace transform of the random height function. The key idea is to relate the Laplace transform to the Neguţ operator and then evaluate its expectation by means of an explicit contour integral. The infinite-product formula for the partition function obtained in Section
3
makes it possible to carry out this program in the doubly free-boundary setting.

We begin by recalling a convenient Macdonald-process-type formalism adapted to the present model.

Definition 4.1
.

Let

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}

\displaystyle=(A^{(l)},A^{(l+1)},\ldots,A^{(r)},A^{(r+1)}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}

\displaystyle=(B^{(l)},B^{(l+1)},\ldots,B^{(r)},B^{(r+1)})

be two collections of countable sets of variables. Let
u,v
be two parameters, and let
\mathcal{P}=\{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}\}
be a partition of
[l..r]
, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\cup\mathcal{R}=[l..r],\qquad\mathcal{L}\cap\mathcal{R}=\emptyset.

For
c_{l},c_{r}\in\{el,oa,deel,eoa\}
and
q,t\in(0,1)
, define a formal probability measure on sequences of partitions

\displaystyle(\lambda^{(l)},\lambda^{(l+1)},\ldots,\lambda^{(r)},\lambda^{(r+1)})

by

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{MP}_{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B},\mathcal{P},c_{l},c_{r},q,t}(\lambda^{(l)},\ldots,\lambda^{(r+1)})\propto\mathbf{1}_{c_{l}}(\lambda^{(l)})\mathbf{1}_{c_{r}}(\lambda^{(r+1)})\frac{b_{\lambda^{(r+1)}}^{c_{r}}}{\overline{b}_{\lambda^{(l)}}^{\,c_{l}}}u^{|\lambda^{(l)}|}v^{|\lambda^{(r+1)}|}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\prod_{i\in\mathcal{L}}\Psi_{\lambda^{(i)},\lambda^{(i+1)}}(A^{(i)},B^{(i+1)};q,t)\prod_{j\in\mathcal{R}}\Phi_{[\lambda^{(j)}]^{\prime},[\lambda^{(j+1)}]^{\prime}}(A^{(j)},B^{(j+1)};q,t),

where, for partitions
\lambda,\mu\in{\mathbb{Y}}
and countable sets of variables
A,B
,

\displaystyle\Psi_{\lambda,\mu}(A,B;q,t)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\nu\in{\mathbb{Y}}}Q_{\lambda/\nu}(A;q,t)P_{\mu/\nu}(B;q,t),

\displaystyle\Phi_{\lambda,\mu}(A,B;q,t)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\nu\in{\mathbb{Y}}}P_{\lambda/\nu}(A;t,q)Q_{\mu/\nu}(B;t,q).

The factors
\mathbf{1}_{c_{l}}
and
\mathbf{1}_{c_{r}}
are the boundary-admissibility indicators defined in (
2.5
); equivalently, the measure is supported only on boundary-admissible sequences.

See Section
A
for the definitions of Macdonald polynomials
P_{\lambda}
,
Q_{\lambda}
,
P_{\lambda/\mu}
, and
Q_{\lambda/\mu}
.

Remark 4.2
.

In terms of the Macdonald scalar product (
A.3
), one may equivalently write

\displaystyle\Psi_{\lambda,\mu}(A,B;q,t)

\displaystyle=\bigl\langle Q_{\lambda}(A,Y;q,t),P_{\mu}(Y,B;q,t)\bigr\rangle_{Y;q,t},

\displaystyle\Phi_{\lambda,\mu}(A,B;q,t)

\displaystyle=\bigl\langle Q_{\mu}(Y,B;t,q),P_{\lambda}(A,Y;t,q)\bigr\rangle_{Y;t,q},

where
Y
is a countable set of variables.

Lemma 4.3

(Induced generalized Macdonald process)
.

Under the correspondence of Lemma
3.1
, the measure

\mathbb{P}_{c_{l},c_{r}}
in (
2.14
) induces on

\bigl(\lambda^{(M,l)},\lambda^{(M,l+1)},\ldots,\lambda^{(M,r)},\lambda^{(M,r+1)}\bigr)

the generalized Macdonald process (
4.1
) with

\mathcal{L}=\{i\in[l..r]:a_{i}=L\},\qquad\mathcal{R}=\{i\in[l..r]:a_{i}=R\},

and

\begin{array}[]{c|cc}b_{i}&A^{(i)}&B^{(i+1)}\\
\hline\cr+&\{0\}&\{x_{i}\}\\
-&\{x_{i}\}&\{0\}.\end{array}

The endpoint restrictions are the boundary indicators

\mathbf{1}_{c_{l}}(\lambda^{(M,l)})\mathbf{1}_{c_{r}}(\lambda^{(M,r+1)})

defined in (
2.5
).

Proof.

By Lemma
3.1
, it remains only to compare the
weights of a partition sequence

\boldsymbol{\mu}=(\mu^{(l)},\ldots,\mu^{(r+1)}).

With the choices of
\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R},A^{(i)},B^{(i+1)}
stated above, the product of
local
\Psi
- and
\Phi
-kernels in (
4.1
) is exactly the bulk factor

A(\boldsymbol{\mu})
in (
2.12
). The remaining endpoint factor in (
4.1
) is

\mathbf{1}_{c_{l}}(\mu^{(l)})\mathbf{1}_{c_{r}}(\mu^{(r+1)})u^{|\mu^{(l)}|}v^{|\mu^{(r+1)}|}\frac{b_{\mu^{(r+1)}}^{c_{r}}}{\overline{b}_{\mu^{(l)}}^{c_{l}}},

which is exactly the endpoint factor in the numerator of (
2.14
). Thus the
unnormalized weights in (
4.1
) and (
2.14
) agree term by term, and division by
the same normalizing constant gives the claimed induced process.
∎

For
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
and
\lambda\in{\mathbb{Y}}
, define

(4.2)

\displaystyle\gamma_{k}(\lambda;q,t):=(1-t^{-k})\sum_{i=1}^{l(\lambda)}q^{k\lambda_{i}}t^{k(-i+1)}+t^{-kl(\lambda)}.

We shall also use the abbreviations

\displaystyle\Pi_{L,L}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\Pi(X,Y;q,t),

(4.3)

\displaystyle\Pi_{R,R}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\Pi(X,Y;t,q),

\displaystyle\Pi_{L,R}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{R,L}(X,Y)=\Pi_{0}(X,Y),

where
\Pi
and
\Pi_{0}
are defined in (
3.7
) and (
3.22
).

To study the scaling limit, for each free-boundary dimer covering
M
we use the following column-wise coordinate system for the rail-yard graph.

Definition 4.4

(Charge-centered
(q,t)
-column coordinate)
.

Let
M
be a free-boundary dimer covering, and set

\rho:=\frac{\log q}{\log t}>0.

We construct the coordinate used in the height-Laplace observables column by column.

On each odd column
x=2m-1
, place the odd vertices on a vertical line, preserving their
original vertical order. The auxiliary vertical coordinate
\widetilde{y}
is determined up
to an additive constant by the following rule for consecutive odd vertices:

\text{distance}=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if both vertices are particles},\\[2.84526pt]
\rho,&\text{if both vertices are holes},\\[2.84526pt]
