\dfrac{1+\rho}{2},&\text{if one vertex is a particle and the other is a hole}.\end{cases}

Let
\widetilde{Y}_{i}
be the auxiliary
\widetilde{y}
-coordinate of the

i
-th highest particle on the column
x=2m-1
. The quantity

\widetilde{c}^{(M,m,q,t)}:=\widetilde{Y}_{i}+i-\frac{1}{2}-\rho\,\lambda_{i}^{(M,m)}

is independent of
i
; it is the
(q,t)
-charge of the auxiliary coordinate on this
column. The vertical coordinate used below is the centered coordinate

y:=\widetilde{y}-\widetilde{c}^{(M,m,q,t)}.

Equivalently, the
(q,t)
-charge of the column in the coordinate
y
is zero. Changing
the auxiliary origin translates both
\widetilde{y}
and

\widetilde{c}^{(M,m,q,t)}
by the same constant, and hence leaves
y
unchanged.

After the odd vertices have been placed, let
\mathfrak{v}=(2i,y_{0})
be an even vertex of
the original rail-yard graph, with horizontal odd neighbors

\mathfrak{v}^{-}=(2i-1,y_{0}),\qquad\mathfrak{v}^{+}=(2i+1,y_{0}).

Place
\mathfrak{v}
at the midpoint of the new positions of

\mathfrak{v}^{-}
and
\mathfrak{v}^{+}
. The edge set is unchanged: all
adjacencies are inherited from
RYG(l,r,a,b)
.

For a marked odd column
m
, write

h^{(q,t)}_{M}(m,y):=h_{M}\!\left(\widetilde{x}_{m},y+\widetilde{c}^{(M,m,q,t)}\right),

where
\widetilde{x}_{m}
is the coordinate vertical line associated with the original column

x=2m-1
. Thus
h^{(q,t)}_{M}
always denotes height in the charge-centered

(q,t)
-column coordinate. No assertion is made about the same observables in the original
deterministic geometric embedding.

Lemma 4.5

(Charge-centered height–Neguţ identity)
.

Let
M
be a free-boundary dimer covering and let
m\in[l..r+1]
. In the
charge-centered
(q,t)
-column coordinate of
Definition
4.4
, for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,

(4.4)

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}h^{(q,t)}_{M}(m,y)t^{ky}\,dy=\frac{2}{k^{2}\log t\log q}\,\gamma_{k}(\lambda^{(M,m)};q,t).

Proof.

Write
\lambda=\lambda^{(M,m)}
, and let
Y_{i}
be the ordinate, in the
charge-centered coordinate, of the
i
-th highest particle on the odd
column
x=2m-1
. Then
the definition of
\lambda
gives

(4.5)

\frac{\log q}{\log t}\lambda_{i}=Y_{i}+i-\frac{1}{2}.

Let

B:=Y_{l(\lambda)+1}+\frac{1}{2}.

Below
B
the normalized height is zero, while for
y>B
,

\frac{d}{dy}h^{(q,t)}_{M}(m,y)=\frac{2\log t}{\log q}\left(1-\sum_{i=1}^{l(\lambda)}\mathbf{1}_{[Y_{i}-\frac{1}{2},Y_{i}+\frac{1}{2}]}(y)\right).

Moreover (
4.5
) gives

B=-l(\lambda).

Since
t\in(0,1)
, integration by parts yields

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}h^{(q,t)}_{M}(m,y)t^{ky}\,dy

\displaystyle=\frac{2t^{kB}}{k^{2}\log t\log q}+\frac{2}{k^{2}\log t\log q}\sum_{i=1}^{l(\lambda)}\left(t^{k(Y_{i}+\frac{1}{2})}-t^{k(Y_{i}-\frac{1}{2})}\right).

Using (
4.5
), we have

t^{k(Y_{i}+\frac{1}{2})}-t^{k(Y_{i}-\frac{1}{2})}=(1-t^{-k})q^{k\lambda_{i}}t^{k(-i+1)}.

Together with
B=-l(\lambda)
, this gives

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}h^{(q,t)}_{M}(m,y)t^{ky}\,dy=\frac{2}{k^{2}\log t\log q}\left[t^{-kl(\lambda)}+(1-t^{-k})\sum_{i=1}^{l(\lambda)}q^{k\lambda_{i}}t^{k(-i+1)}\right],

which is (
4.4
).
∎

Lemma 4.6
.

For every partition
\lambda\in{\mathbb{Y}}
,

\displaystyle\gamma_{k}(\lambda^{\prime};t,q)=\gamma_{k}\!\left(\lambda;\frac{1}{q},\frac{1}{t}\right).

Proof.

This is the same argument as in Lemma 4.1 of
[
10
]
.
∎

Recall also the standard involutive symmetry of Macdonald polynomials (see p. 324 of
[
12
]
):

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}(X;q,t)

\displaystyle=P_{\lambda}\!\left(X;\frac{1}{q},\frac{1}{t}\right),

(4.6)

\displaystyle Q_{\lambda}(X;q,t)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{t}{q}\right)^{|\lambda|}Q_{\lambda}\!\left(X;\frac{1}{q},\frac{1}{t}\right).

Lemma 4.7
.

For any countable set of variables
X
and
c\in\{L,R\}
, define the specialization

\displaystyle\rho_{X,1,c}(p_{n}):=\begin{cases}p_{n}(X),&c=L,\\
\omega_{q,t}(p_{n}(X)),&c=R.\end{cases}

Write
\rho_{c}(B):=\rho_{B,1,c}
and
\rho_{d}(W):=\rho_{W,1,d}
. Then

\displaystyle\Pi(X,Y;q,t)

\displaystyle=H(\rho_{X,1,L};\rho_{Y,1,L};q,t),

\displaystyle\Pi_{0}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=H(\rho_{X,1,L};\rho_{Y,1,R};q,t),

\displaystyle L(W,A;q,t)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{q^{n}(1-t^{-n})}{n}p_{n}(A)p_{n}(W^{-1})\right),

\displaystyle\Pi_{c,d}(B,W)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1-t^{n}}{1-q^{n}}\frac{p_{n}(\rho_{c}(B))p_{n}(\rho_{d}(W))}{n}\right),

\displaystyle\frac{\Pi_{c,d}(B,W)}{\Pi_{c,d}(B,\xi(q,t,c)W)}

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(1-t^{n})(1-\xi(q,t,c)^{n})}{n(1-q^{n})}p_{n}(\rho_{c}(B))p_{n}(\rho_{d}(W))\right),

\displaystyle L\left(W,A;\frac{1}{t},\frac{1}{q}\right)

\displaystyle=H\bigl(\rho_{(t^{-1}W^{-1}),1,L}\cup((-1)*\rho_{W^{-1},1,L});A;t,q\bigr)

\displaystyle=H\bigl(\omega_{q,t}(\rho_{(t^{-1}W^{-1}),1,L}\cup((-1)*\rho_{W^{-1},1,L}));\omega_{q,t}(A);q,t\bigr).

Moreover,

\displaystyle\omega(q,t,a_{i})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(q,t),&a_{i}=L,\\
\left(\dfrac{1}{t},\dfrac{1}{q}\right),&a_{i}=R,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\omega_{*}(q,t,a_{i})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(q,t),&a_{i}=L,\\
(t,q),&a_{i}=R,\end{cases}

(4.7)

\displaystyle\xi(q,t,a_{i})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}q^{-1},&a_{i}=L,\\
t,&a_{i}=R.\end{cases}

Proof.

The first identity follows by comparing the exponential form of the Macdonald Cauchy kernel with the definition of the kernel
H
:

\Pi(X,Y;q,t)=\exp\left(\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{1-t^{n}}{n(1-q^{n})}p_{n}(X)p_{n}(Y)\right)=H(\rho_{X,1,L};\rho_{Y,1,L};q,t).

For the second identity, applying
\omega_{q,t}
to the second variable gives

\frac{1-t^{n}}{1-q^{n}}p_{n}(X)\omega_{q,t}(p_{n}(Y))=(-1)^{n-1}p_{n}(X)p_{n}(Y),

and hence

H(\rho_{X,1,L};\rho_{Y,1,R};q,t)=\exp\left(\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n}p_{n}(X)p_{n}(Y)\right)=\Pi_{0}(X,Y).

The formula for
L(W,A;q,t)
is obtained by expanding the logarithm of

\prod_{w\in W}\prod_{a\in A}\frac{w-qa/t}{w-qa}

in powers of
a/w
, which gives

\log L(W,A;q,t)=\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{q^{n}(1-t^{-n})}{n}p_{n}(A)p_{n}(W^{-1}).

The two displayed formulas involving
\Pi_{c,d}
then follow immediately from the same exponential definition of
H
, and from replacing
W
by
\xi(q,t,c)W
in the second one. Finally, the last two identities are obtained by writing the specialization

\rho_{(t^{-1}W^{-1}),1,L}\cup((-1)*\rho_{W^{-1},1,L})
in power sums and comparing its kernel with the preceding logarithmic expansion of
L(W,A;1/t,1/q)
. Applying Lemma
A.5
to both arguments gives the equivalent form with
\omega_{q,t}
.
∎

Define, for
d\in\{L,R\}
and
v\in\mathbb{C}
,

(4.8)

\displaystyle\rho_{v,2,d}(X;q,t):=\begin{cases}\rho_{\frac{q^{2}v}{t}X^{-1},1,L}\cup\bigl((-1)*\rho_{\frac{qv}{t}X^{-1},1,L}\bigr),&d=L,\\
\omega_{q,t}\!\left(\bigl((-1)*\rho_{vX^{-1},1,L}\bigr)\cup\rho_{t^{-1}vX^{-1},1,L}\right),&d=R,\end{cases}

and

(4.9)

\displaystyle\rho_{v,3,d}(X;q,t):=\begin{cases}\rho_{vX,1,L}\cup\bigl((-1)*\rho_{q^{-1}vX,1,L}\bigr),&d=L,\\
\rho_{vX,1,R}\cup\bigl((-1)*\rho_{tvX,1,R}\bigr),&d=R.\end{cases}

For
v=1
, we abbreviate these by
\rho_{2,d}(X;q,t)
and
\rho_{3,d}(X;q,t)
.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
g_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
for
i\in[l+1..r]
, and for each such
i
let
W^{(i)}
be an ordered set of variables with
|W^{(i)}|=g_{i}
. Define

\displaystyle\rho_{A}

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{i=l}^{r}\rho_{A^{(i)},1,a_{i}},\qquad\rho_{B}:=\bigcup_{i=l+1}^{r+1}\rho_{B^{(i)},1,a_{i-1}},

\displaystyle\rho_{W}

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{i=l+1}^{r}\rho_{2,a_{i}}(W^{(i)};q,t),\qquad\rho_{W}^{\circ}:=\bigcup_{i=l+1}^{r}\rho_{3,a_{i}}(W^{(i)};q,t).

Then

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{MP}_{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B},\mathcal{P},c_{l},c_{r},q,t}}\left[\prod_{i=l+1}^{r}\gamma_{g_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)};q,t)\right]

\displaystyle=\oint\cdots\oint\prod_{i=l+1}^{r}D\bigl(W^{(i)};\omega(q,t,a_{i})\bigr)\prod_{l+1\leq i\leq j\leq r}H\bigl(\rho_{2,a_{i}}(W^{(i)};q,t);\rho_{A^{(j)},1,a_{j}};q,t\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\times\prod_{l\leq i<j\leq r}H\bigl(\rho_{3,a_{j}}(W^{(j)};q,t);\rho_{B^{(i+1)},1,a_{i}};q,t\bigr)\prod_{l+1\leq i<j\leq r}H\bigl(\rho_{2,a_{i}}(W^{(i)};q,t);\rho_{3,a_{j}}(W^{(j)};q,t);q,t\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\times\prod_{k\geq 1}\Theta_{c_{l}}([u^{2k-2}v^{2k-1}]\rho_{W};q,t)\Theta_{c_{r}}([u^{2k-1}v^{2k}]\rho_{W};q,t)\Theta_{c_{l}}([u^{2k-1}v^{2k-2}]\rho_{W}^{\circ};q,t)\Theta_{c_{r}}([u^{2k}v^{2k-1}]\rho_{W}^{\circ};q,t)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\times\prod_{k\geq 1}H(u^{2k}\rho_{B};v^{2k}\rho_{W}^{\circ};q,t)H(u^{2k-2}\rho_{B};v^{2k}\rho_{W};q,t)H(u^{2k}\rho_{W};v^{2k}\rho_{W}^{\circ};q,t)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\times\prod_{k\geq 1}H(u^{2k}\rho_{A};v^{2k}\rho_{W};q,t)H(u^{2k}\rho_{A};v^{2k-2}\rho_{W}^{\circ};q,t).

Here the integral contours are given by
\{\mathcal{C}_{i,j}\}_{i\in[l+1..r],s\in[g_{i}]}
such that

(1)

\mathcal{C}_{i,s}
is the integral contour for the variable
w^{(i)}_{s}\in W^{(i)}
;

(2)

\mathcal{C}_{i,s}
encloses 0 and every singular point of

\displaystyle\prod_{i\leq j;i,j\in[l+1..r]}H(\rho_{2,a_{i}}(W^{(i)};q,t);\rho_{A^{(j)},1,a_{j}})\prod_{k\geq 1}\left[H(u^{2k}\rho_{A};v^{2k}\rho_{W}))H(u^{2k-2}\rho_{B};v^{2k}\rho_{W})\right]

\displaystyle\prod_{k\geq 1}\Theta_{cl}([u^{2k-2}v^{2k-1}]\rho_{W})\Theta_{cr}([u^{2k-1}v^{2k}]\rho_{W})

but no other singular points of the integrand.

(3)

the contour
\mathcal{C}_{i,j}
is contained in the domain bounded by
t\mathcal{C}_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}
whenever
(i,j)<(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
in lexicographical ordering;

D(W;q,t)
,
H(W,X;q,t)
, and
\Pi_{c,d}(X,Y)
are given by (
A.16
), (
A.17
), and (
4.3
).

Proof.

Let
Z
denote the normalization constant of (
4.1
). By Lemma
4.6
, whenever
a_{i}=R
we may rewrite the observable
\gamma_{g_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)};q,t)
in terms of the conjugate partition and the pair
(1/t,1/q)
. Using Definition
4.1
, Remark 4.2, and the identity (
4.6
), the expectation in the statement becomes a normalized nested Macdonald scalar product.

For
i\in[l+1..r]
, define the local kernels

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{i}:=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda^{(i)}\in{\mathbb{Y}}}\gamma_{g_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)};q,t)Q_{\lambda^{(i)}}(A^{(i)},Y^{(i)};q,t)P_{\lambda^{(i)}}(Y^{(i-1)},B^{(i)};q,t),&(a_{i-1},a_{i})=(L,L),\\[5.69054pt]
\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda^{(i)}\in{\mathbb{Y}}}\gamma_{g_{i}}\!\left([\lambda^{(i)}]^{\prime};\frac{1}{t},\frac{1}{q}\right)P_{[\lambda^{(i)}]^{\prime}}(A^{(i)},Y^{(i)};t,q)P_{\lambda^{(i)}}(Y^{(i-1)},B^{(i)};q,t),&(a_{i-1},a_{i})=(L,R),\\[5.69054pt]
\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda^{(i)}\in{\mathbb{Y}}}\gamma_{g_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)};q,t)Q_{\lambda^{(i)}}(A^{(i)},Y^{(i)};q,t)Q_{[\lambda^{(i)}]^{\prime}}(Y^{(i-1)},B^{(i)};t,q),&(a_{i-1},a_{i})=(R,L),\\[5.69054pt]
\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda^{(i)}\in{\mathbb{Y}}}\gamma_{g_{i}}\!\left([\lambda^{(i)}]^{\prime};\frac{1}{t},\frac{1}{q}\right)P_{[\lambda^{(i)}]^{\prime}}(A^{(i)},Y^{(i)};t,q)Q_{[\lambda^{(i)}]^{\prime}}(Y^{(i-1)},B^{(i)};t,q),&(a_{i-1},a_{i})=(R,R).\end{cases}

Similarly, define the boundary terms

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{l}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda^{(l)}\in{\mathbb{Y}}}\mathbf{1}_{c_{l}}(\lambda^{(l)})u^{|\lambda^{(l)}|}\bigl[\overline{b}_{\lambda^{(l)}}^{\,c_{l}}\bigr]^{-1}Q_{\lambda^{(l)}}(A^{(l)},Y^{(l)};q,t),&a_{l}=L,\\[5.69054pt]
\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda^{(l)}\in{\mathbb{Y}}}\mathbf{1}_{c_{l}}(\lambda^{(l)})u^{|\lambda^{(l)}|}\bigl[\overline{b}_{\lambda^{(l)}}^{\,c_{l}}\bigr]^{-1}P_{[\lambda^{(l)}]^{\prime}}(A^{(l)},Y^{(l)};t,q),&a_{l}=R,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{r+1}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda^{(r+1)}\in{\mathbb{Y}}}\mathbf{1}_{c_{r}}(\lambda^{(r+1)})v^{|\lambda^{(r+1)}|}b_{\lambda^{(r+1)}}^{c_{r}}P_{\lambda^{(r+1)}}(Y^{(r)},B^{(r+1)};q,t),&a_{r}=L,\\[5.69054pt]
\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda^{(r+1)}\in{\mathbb{Y}}}\mathbf{1}_{c_{r}}(\lambda^{(r+1)})v^{|\lambda^{(r+1)}|}b_{\lambda^{(r+1)}}^{c_{r}}Q_{[\lambda^{(r+1)}]^{\prime}}(Y^{(r)},B^{(r+1)};t,q),&a_{r}=R.\end{cases}

The indicators in
\mathbf{E}_{l}
and
\mathbf{E}_{r+1}
are essential when
c_{l}
or
c_{r}
equals
deel
or
eoa
; they are exactly the support restrictions in Definition
4.1
and are retained until the final boundary scalar product in Lemma
4.11
.
Then

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{MP}_{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B},\mathcal{P},c_{l},c_{r},q,t}}\left[\prod_{i=l+1}^{r}\gamma_{g_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)};q,t)\right]=\frac{1}{Z}\Bigl\langle\mathbf{E}_{l},\bigl\langle\mathbf{E}_{l+1},\ldots,\langle\mathbf{E}_{r},\mathbf{E}_{r+1}\rangle_{Y^{(r)}}\cdots\bigr\rangle_{Y^{(l+1)}}\Bigr\rangle_{Y^{(l)}}.

For each
i\in[l+1..r]
, the kernel
\mathbf{E}_{i}
is obtained by applying the Neguţ operator to the corresponding Cauchy kernel:

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{i}=\begin{cases}D_{-g_{i},(A^{(i)},Y^{(i)});q,t}\Pi_{a_{i-1},a_{i}}\bigl((A^{(i)},Y^{(i)}),(Y^{(i-1)},B^{(i)})\bigr),&a_{i}=L,\\[5.69054pt]
D_{-g_{i},(A^{(i)},Y^{(i)});1/t,1/q}\Pi_{a_{i-1},a_{i}}\bigl((A^{(i)},Y^{(i)}),(Y^{(i-1)},B^{(i)})\bigr),&a_{i}=R.\end{cases}

By Lemma
4.3
, the dimer measure induces the generalized Macdonald
process (
4.1
). We apply Proposition
A.8
to each inserted
observable
\mathbf{E}_{i}
. After interchanging the resulting contour
integrals with the scalar products, Lemma
4.7
rewrites all

\Pi
-,
\Pi_{0}
-, and
L
-kernels as
H
-kernels. We obtain a multiple
contour integral whose integrand contains the
D
-kernels and the nested
scalar product

\Bigl\langle F_{l},\,\bigl\langle F_{l+1},\ldots,\bigl\langle F_{r},F_{r+1}\bigr\rangle_{Y^{(r)}}\cdots\bigr\rangle_{Y^{(l+1)}}\Bigr\rangle_{Y^{(l)}}.

Here

F_{l}=\widetilde{H}\bigl(\rho_{uA^{(l)},1,a_{l}}\cup\rho_{uY^{(l)},1,a_{l}}\bigr),\qquad F_{r+1}=\widetilde{H}\bigl(\rho_{vB^{(r+1)},1,a_{r}}\cup\rho_{vY^{(r)},1,a_{r}}\bigr),

and, for
i\in[l+1..r]
,

\displaystyle F_{i}

\displaystyle=H\bigl(\rho_{Y^{(i-1)},1,a_{i-1}};\rho_{A^{(i)},1,a_{i}}\bigr)H\bigl(\rho_{Y^{(i-1)},1,a_{i-1}};\rho_{Y^{(i)},1,a_{i}}\bigr)H\bigl(\rho_{Y^{(i)},1,a_{i}};\rho_{B^{(i)},1,a_{i-1}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\times H\bigl(\rho_{2,a_{i}}(W^{(i)};q,t);\rho_{Y^{(i)},1,a_{i}}\bigr)H\bigl(\rho_{3,a_{i}}(W^{(i)};q,t);\rho_{Y^{(i-1)},1,a_{i-1}}\bigr).

We now evaluate the scalar products from right to left. Each active alphabet

Y^{(j)}
occurs only through
H
-kernels and the endpoint

\widetilde{H}
-terms. Hence Lemma
4.9
contracts the
H
-kernels,
while Lemma
4.10
transports the endpoint
\widetilde{H}
-terms through
the contraction. Repeating this for
j=r,r-1,\ldots,l
gives three types
of factors.

(1)

the pair-interaction factors

\prod_{l+1\leq i<j\leq r}H\bigl(\rho_{2,a_{i}}(W^{(i)};q,t);\rho_{3,a_{j}}(W^{(j)};q,t);q,t\bigr);

(2)

the one-body factors

\prod_{l+1\leq i\leq j\leq r}H\bigl(\rho_{2,a_{i}}(W^{(i)};q,t);\rho_{A^{(j)},1,a_{j}};q,t\bigr),

and

\prod_{l\leq i<j\leq r}H\bigl(\rho_{3,a_{j}}(W^{(j)};q,t);\rho_{B^{(i+1)},1,a_{i}};q,t\bigr);

(3)

the final boundary contraction

\left\langle\widetilde{H}\bigl(\rho_{uA^{(l)},1,a_{l}}\cup\rho_{uY^{(l)},1,a_{l}}\bigr),\widetilde{H}\bigl(\rho_{vB^{(r+1)},1,a_{r}}\cup\rho_{vY^{(r)},1,a_{r}}\bigr)\right\rangle,

after all intermediate
Y
-variables have been contracted.

The boundary indicators remain attached to the endpoint sums throughout this
recursion. Hence the last scalar product is precisely the boundary scalar
product evaluated in Lemma
4.11
, which gives the four infinite products
of
\Theta
- and
H
-factors appearing in the statement.

Finally, the normalizing constant
Z
is the partition function in
Proposition
3.14
. The boundary normalization produced by
Lemma
4.11
is the same one appearing in
Z
, and therefore cancels in
the normalized expectation. The remaining factors are precisely the

D
-kernels, one-body factors, pair-interaction factors, and boundary
products displayed in the statement.
∎

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
\{d_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
,
\{s_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
, and
\{u_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
be sequences in graded algebras such that

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathrm{ldeg}(d_{k})=\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathrm{ldeg}(s_{k})=\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathrm{ldeg}(u_{k})=\infty.

Then

\displaystyle\left\langle\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{d_{k}p_{k}(Y)+s_{k}[p_{k}(Y)]^{2}}{k}\right),\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{u_{k}p_{k}(Y)}{k}\right)\right\rangle_{Y;q,t}

\displaystyle\qquad=\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1-q^{k}}{1-t^{k}}\frac{d_{k}u_{k}}{k}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1-q^{k}}{1-t^{k}}\right)^{2}\frac{s_{k}u_{k}^{2}}{k}\right).

Proof.

If
s_{k}=0
, the lemma follows from Proposition 2.3 of
[
4
]
. For general
s_{k}
, let

\displaystyle c_{(k_{1},l_{1}),(k_{2},l_{2}),\ldots,(k_{m},l_{m})}[p_{k_{1}}(Y)]^{l_{1}}[p_{k_{2}}(Y)]^{l_{2}}\cdots[p_{k_{m}}(Y)]^{l_{m}}:=f(Y)

be a monomial in the expansion of

\displaystyle\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{d_{k}p_{k}(Y)+s_{k}[p_{k}(Y)]^{2}}{k}\right);

such that
k_{1}<k_{2}<\ldots<k_{m}
. Note that

(4.10)

\displaystyle R(Y):=\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{u_{k}p_{k}(Y)}{k}\right)\right)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{u_{k}p_{k}(Y)}{k}\right)\right)^{n}

Let
L:=l_{1}+\ldots+l_{m}
. The monomial

[p_{k_{1}}(Y)]^{l_{1}}[p_{k_{2}}(Y)]^{l_{2}}\cdots[p_{k_{m}}(Y)]^{l_{m}}
has coefficient

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L!}\frac{L!}{(l_{1})!(l_{2})!\cdot\ldots\cdot(l_{m})!}\frac{u_{k_{1}}^{l_{1}}u_{k_{2}}^{l_{2}}\cdot\ldots\cdot u_{k_{m}}^{l_{m}}}{k_{1}^{l_{1}}k_{2}^{l_{2}}\cdot\ldots\cdot k_{m}^{l_{m}}}:=\frac{g(Y)}{[p_{k_{1}}(Y)]^{l_{1}}[p_{k_{2}}(Y)]^{l_{2}}\cdots[p_{k_{m}}(Y)]^{l_{m}}}

in (
3.4
).
Then by (
A.3
)

\displaystyle\langle f(Y),R(Y)\rangle_{Y;q,t}

\displaystyle=\langle f(Y),g(Y)\rangle_{Y;q,t}=c_{(k_{1},l_{1}),(k_{2},l_{2}),\ldots,(k_{m},l_{m})}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\left(\frac{(1-q^{k_{i}})u_{k_{i}}}{1-t^{k_{i}}}\right)^{l_{i}}

which can be obtained from
f(Y)
by replacing each
p_{k_{i}}(Y)
by
\frac{(1-q^{k_{i}})u_{k_{i}}}{1-t^{k_{i}}}
for
1\leq i\leq m
. Then the lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\rho_{3}
be specializations such that
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{3}
are independent of the variables in
Y
, and assume that
\rho_{2}(p_{k})=p_{k}(Y)
for all
k\geq 1
. Let
*\in\{oa,el,deel,eoa\}
. Then

(4.11)

\displaystyle\left\langle\Theta_{*}(\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2};q,t),H(\rho_{2};\rho_{3};q,t)\right\rangle_{Y;q,t}

\displaystyle=\Theta_{*}(\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{3};q,t),

(4.12)

\displaystyle\left\langle\Theta_{*}(\rho_{1}\cup\omega_{q,t}(\rho_{2});q,t),H(\rho_{2};\omega_{t,q}(\rho_{3});t,q)\right\rangle_{Y;t,q}

\displaystyle=\Theta_{*}(\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{3};q,t).

Proof.

Insert the explicit exponential expressions for
\Theta_{*}
from Lemma
3.11
and for
H
from (
3.26
), and then apply Lemma
4.9
. For
*=deel
and
*=eoa
this uses
\Theta_{deel}=\Theta_{de,el}
and
\Theta_{eoa}=\Theta_{e,oa}
; the same linear-quadratic exponential computation applies, with the missing one-body factor in the even cases corresponding to a zero linear coefficient. The quadratic and linear terms match exactly, yielding (
4.11
) and (
4.12
).
∎

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
be specializations, let
c_{l},c_{r}\in\{oa,el,deel,eoa\}
, and let
Y
be a countable set of variables. Let
\rho
be the specialization defined by

(4.13)

\displaystyle\rho(p_{k})=p_{k}(Y).

Let
u,v\in(0,1)
. Then

\displaystyle\left\langle\Theta_{c_{l}}(u[\rho_{1}\cup\rho];q,t),\Theta_{c_{r}}(v[\rho_{2}\cup\rho];q,t)\right\rangle_{Y;q,t}

(4.14)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt=\left[\prod_{i\geq 1}\Theta_{c_{l}}([u^{2i-1}v^{2i-2}]\rho_{1};q,t)\Theta_{c_{r}}([u^{2i}v^{2i-1}]\rho_{1};q,t)\Theta_{c_{r}}([u^{2i-2}v^{2i-1}]\rho_{2};q,t)\Theta_{c_{l}}([u^{2i-1}v^{2i}]\rho_{2};q,t)\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\times\left[\prod_{j\geq 1}H([u^{2j}v^{2j}]\rho_{1};\rho_{2};q,t)\right]\mathfrak{Z}_{c_{l},c_{r}}(uv).

If instead
\rho
is defined by

(4.15)

\displaystyle\rho(p_{k})=\omega_{q,t}(p_{k}(Y)),

then the scalar product

\displaystyle\left\langle\Theta_{c_{l}}(u[\rho_{1}\cup\rho];q,t),\Theta_{c_{r}}(v[\rho_{2}\cup\rho];q,t)\right\rangle_{Y;t,q}

is given by the same right-hand side.

Proof.

Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2}):=\sum_{\lambda,\mu,\nu\in{\mathbb{Y}}}\mathbf{1}_{c_{l}}(\lambda)\mathbf{1}_{c_{r}}(\mu)\frac{b_{\mu}^{c_{r}}}{\overline{b}_{\lambda}^{\,c_{l}}}u^{|\lambda|}v^{|\mu|}Q_{\lambda/\nu}(\rho_{1};q,t)P_{\mu/\nu}(\rho_{2};q,t).

By Corollary
3.8
with
\eta=\emptyset
,

\displaystyle\Theta_{c_{l}}(u[\rho_{1}\cup\rho];q,t)=\sum_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{Y}}}\mathbf{1}_{c_{l}}(\lambda)\frac{u^{|\lambda|}}{\overline{b}_{\lambda}^{\,c_{l}}}Q_{\lambda}(\rho_{1}\cup\rho;q,t),

and by Corollary
3.6
with
\eta=\emptyset
,

\displaystyle\Theta_{c_{r}}(v[\rho_{2}\cup\rho];q,t)=\sum_{\mu\in{\mathbb{Y}}}\mathbf{1}_{c_{r}}(\mu)b_{\mu}^{c_{r}}v^{|\mu|}P_{\mu}(\rho_{2}\cup\rho;q,t).

Therefore the left-hand side of (
4.14
) is exactly
\mathcal{I}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
. The same conclusion holds in the case (
4.15
), because

\displaystyle\bigl\langle P_{\nu}(\omega_{q,t}),Q_{\nu}(\omega_{q,t})\bigr\rangle_{Y;t,q}=1

for every
\nu\in{\mathbb{Y}}
.

We now derive a recursion for
\mathcal{I}
. First apply Corollary
3.8
to the sum over
\lambda
and Corollary
3.6
to the sum over
\mu
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=\Theta_{c_{l}}(u\rho_{1};q,t)\Theta_{c_{r}}(v\rho_{2};q,t)\sum_{\eta,\zeta\in{\mathbb{Y}}}\mathbf{1}_{c_{l}}(\eta)\mathbf{1}_{c_{r}}(\zeta)\frac{u^{|\eta|}}{\overline{b}_{\eta}^{\,c_{l}}}b_{\zeta}^{c_{r}}v^{|\zeta|}\sum_{\nu\in{\mathbb{Y}}}P_{\nu/\eta}(u^{2}\rho_{1};q,t)Q_{\nu/\zeta}(v^{2}\rho_{2};q,t).

Next, by Lemma
3.9
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu\in{\mathbb{Y}}}P_{\nu/\eta}(u^{2}\rho_{1};q,t)Q_{\nu/\zeta}(v^{2}\rho_{2};q,t)=H([u^{2}v^{2}]\rho_{1};\rho_{2};q,t)\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbb{Y}}}P_{\zeta/\sigma}(u^{2}\rho_{1};q,t)Q_{\eta/\sigma}(v^{2}\rho_{2};q,t).

Applying Corollary
3.6
to the sum over
\zeta
and Corollary
3.8
to the sum over
\eta
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=\Theta_{c_{l}}(u\rho_{1};q,t)\Theta_{c_{r}}(v\rho_{2};q,t)\Theta_{c_{r}}(u^{2}v\rho_{1};q,t)\Theta_{c_{l}}(uv^{2}\rho_{2};q,t)H([u^{2}v^{2}]\rho_{1};\rho_{2};q,t)\mathcal{J}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2}),

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2}):=\sum_{\mu,\tau,\sigma\in{\mathbb{Y}}}\mathbf{1}_{c_{l}}(\tau)\mathbf{1}_{c_{r}}(\mu)\frac{b_{\mu}^{c_{r}}}{\overline{b}_{\tau}^{\,c_{l}}}u^{|\tau|}v^{|\mu|}P_{\sigma/\tau}([u^{2}v^{2}]\rho_{2};q,t)Q_{\sigma/\mu}([u^{2}v^{2}]\rho_{1};q,t).

A second application of Lemma
3.9
shows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})=H([u^{4}v^{4}]\rho_{1};\rho_{2};q,t)\mathcal{I}([u^{2}v^{2}]\rho_{1},[u^{2}v^{2}]\rho_{2}).

Hence

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=\Theta_{c_{l}}(u\rho_{1};q,t)\Theta_{c_{r}}(u^{2}v\rho_{1};q,t)\Theta_{c_{r}}(v\rho_{2};q,t)\Theta_{c_{l}}(uv^{2}\rho_{2};q,t)

\displaystyle\qquad\times H([u^{2}v^{2}]\rho_{1};\rho_{2};q,t)H([u^{4}v^{4}]\rho_{1};\rho_{2};q,t)\mathcal{I}([u^{2}v^{2}]\rho_{1},[u^{2}v^{2}]\rho_{2}).

Iterating this identity
N
times yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=\left[\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Theta_{c_{l}}([u^{2i-1}v^{2i-2}]\rho_{1};q,t)\Theta_{c_{r}}([u^{2i}v^{2i-1}]\rho_{1};q,t)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\Theta_{c_{r}}([u^{2i-2}v^{2i-1}]\rho_{2};q,t)\Theta_{c_{l}}([u^{2i-1}v^{2i}]\rho_{2};q,t)\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left[\prod_{j=1}^{N}H([u^{2j}v^{2j}]\rho_{1};\rho_{2};q,t)\right]\mathcal{I}([u^{2N}v^{2N}]\rho_{1},[u^{2N}v^{2N}]\rho_{2}).

Since
u,v\in(0,1)
, the specializations
[u^{2N}v^{2N}]\rho_{1}
and
[u^{2N}v^{2N}]\rho_{2}
converge to the zero specialization as
N\to\infty
, and therefore

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{I}([u^{2N}v^{2N}]\rho_{1},[u^{2N}v^{2N}]\rho_{2})=\mathfrak{Z}_{c_{l},c_{r}}(uv).

Passing to the limit proves (
4.14
). The case (
4.15
) is identical, since the reduction to
\mathcal{I}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
is unchanged. The parity constraints for
deel
and
eoa
are preserved at each reflection step by Corollaries
3.6
and
3.8
; at the terminal step they give precisely the two indicators in (
3.2
).
∎

Combining Lemmas
4.3
,
4.5
,
4.6
, and
4.8
yields the moment formula for the Laplace transform of the height function under the doubly free-boundary Gibbs measure.

5.
Scaling Limits

In this section, we analyze the asymptotic behavior of the observables introduced in
Section
4
and prove that the centered fluctuations are Gaussian in the scaling
limit. The main inputs are the moment formula from Lemma
4.8
, the periodicity
assumptions from Section
2
, and the
q
-Pochhammer ratio estimate in
Lemma
A.9
. A central point is that, after a careful reorganization of the integrand,
the one-point free-boundary correction factors converge uniformly to the infinite product
encoded by the functions
\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}
; this is the content of Lemma
5.6
.
At the two-point level the quotient by the one-point factors has a non-trivial
second-order limit, which gives the covariance correction kernel
\mathscr{B}_{u,v}
.

5.1.
The interaction kernel

Lemma 5.1

(Finite non-reflected pair factor between two Neguţ blocks)
.

Let
Z
and
U
be the ordered Neguţ variable sets attached to two
marked columns of types
c,d\in\{L,R\}
. In the multi-point contour
formula of Lemma
4.8
, the finite non-reflected two-block factor comes
from the ordered-column product

\prod_{l+1\leq i<j\leq r}H\bigl(\rho_{2,a_{i}}(W^{(i)};q,t);\rho_{3,a_{j}}(W^{(j)};q,t);q,t\bigr).

For two fixed blocks
Z=W^{(i)}
and
U=W^{(j)}
, with
i<j
,

a_{i}=c
, and
a_{j}=d
, this factor is

H\bigl(\rho_{2,c}(Z;q,t);\rho_{3,d}(U;q,t);q,t\bigr)=T_{c,d}(Z,U)=\prod_{z\in Z}\prod_{u\in U}t_{c,d}(z,u),

where

t_{c,d}(z,u):=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{(z-u)(qz-tu)}{(z-tu)(qz-u)},&(c,d)=(L,L),\\[8.53581pt]
\displaystyle\frac{(u+z)^{2}}{(u+tz)\left(u+\frac{z}{t}\right)},&(c,d)=(L,R),\\[8.53581pt]
\displaystyle\frac{(tu+q^{2}z)(tu+z)}{(tu+qz)^{2}},&(c,d)=(R,L),\\[8.53581pt]
\displaystyle\frac{(u-qz)(tu-z)}{(u-z)(tu-qz)},&(c,d)=(R,R).\end{cases}

This factor is the finite bulk/Neguţ interaction between the two marked
blocks; it does not include the boundary reflection factors coming from the
two free endpoints in Lemma
4.8
.

Proof.

Since
H
is multiplicative over unions of one-variable
specializations, the contribution of two fixed blocks factors over pairs

(z,u)\in Z\times U
. It is therefore enough to compute the contribution
of one pair.

For
(c,d)=(L,L)
, the definitions (
4.8
)–(
4.9
) and the

H
-kernel identity of Lemma
4.7
give the quotient

\frac{1-u/z}{1-tu/z}\cdot\frac{1-(t/q)u/z}{1-(1/q)u/z}=\frac{(z-u)(qz-tu)}{(z-tu)(qz-u)}.

This is
t_{L,L}(z,u)
.

For
(c,d)=(R,R)
, the same computation with conjugated specializations,
using the ordered-column convention in Lemma
4.8
, gives

t_{R,R}(z,u)=\frac{(u-qz)(tu-z)}{(u-z)(tu-qz)}.

For the mixed cases, Lemma
4.7
converts the conjugate Cauchy factor
into the corresponding plus-kernel. The single-pair contributions are

t_{L,R}(z,u)=\frac{(u+z)^{2}}{(u+tz)(u+z/t)}

and

t_{R,L}(z,u)=\frac{(tu+q^{2}z)(tu+z)}{(tu+qz)^{2}}.

Multiplying these single-pair contributions over all

(z,u)\in Z\times U
gives the claimed formula for
T_{c,d}(Z,U)
.
∎

5.2.
Moment formulas specialized to dimer coverings

Lemma 5.2

(Moment formula after the dimer specialization)
.

Let

i_{1}\leq i_{2}\leq\cdots\leq i_{m}

be integers in
[l+1..r]
, and let
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
.
For each occurrence
s\in[m]
, let
W^{[s]}
be an ordered set of variables
with
|W^{[s]}|=g_{s}
, attached to the marked column
i_{s}
. Write

\mathbf{i}:=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}).

All unmarked columns are understood to have empty Neguţ variable sets. Thus
the active marked-block specializations are

\rho_{W,\mathbf{i}}:=\bigcup_{s=1}^{m}\rho_{2,a_{i_{s}}}\!\left(W^{[s]};q,t\right),\qquad\rho_{W,\mathbf{i}}^{\circ}:=\bigcup_{s=1}^{m}\rho_{3,a_{i_{s}}}\!\left(W^{[s]};q,t\right).

We keep the graph specializations
\rho_{A},\rho_{B}
from Lemma
4.8
.
For
k\geq 1
, define

\Theta_{k,\mathbf{i}}=\Theta_{k,\mathbf{i}}(W^{[1]},\ldots,W^{[m]})
by

\displaystyle\Theta_{k,\mathbf{i}}

\displaystyle:=\Theta_{c_{l}}\!\left([u^{2k-2}v^{2k-1}]\rho_{W,\mathbf{i}};q,t\right)\Theta_{c_{r}}\!\left([u^{2k-1}v^{2k}]\rho_{W,\mathbf{i}};q,t\right)

\displaystyle\quad\times\Theta_{c_{l}}\!\left([u^{2k-1}v^{2k-2}]\rho_{W,\mathbf{i}}^{\circ};q,t\right)\Theta_{c_{r}}\!\left([u^{2k}v^{2k-1}]\rho_{W,\mathbf{i}}^{\circ};q,t\right)

\displaystyle\quad\times H\!\left(u^{2k}\rho_{B};v^{2k}\rho_{W,\mathbf{i}}^{\circ};q,t\right)H\!\left(u^{2k-2}\rho_{B};v^{2k}\rho_{W,\mathbf{i}};q,t\right)H\!\left(u^{2k}\rho_{W,\mathbf{i}};v^{2k}\rho_{W,\mathbf{i}}^{\circ};q,t\right)

(5.1)

\displaystyle\quad\times H\!\left(u^{2k}\rho_{A};v^{2k}\rho_{W,\mathbf{i}};q,t\right)H\!\left(u^{2k}\rho_{A};v^{2k-2}\rho_{W,\mathbf{i}}^{\circ};q,t\right).

Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Pr}\Biggl[\prod_{s=1}^{m}\gamma_{g_{s}}\bigl(\lambda^{(i_{s})};q,t\bigr)\Biggr]

\displaystyle=\oint\cdots\oint\prod_{s=1}^{m}D\bigl(W^{[s]};\omega(q,t,a_{i_{s}})\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\times\prod_{s=1}^{m}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[i_{s}..r]\\
b_{j}=-\end{subarray}}H\bigl(\rho_{2,a_{i_{s}}}(W^{[s]};q,t);\rho_{\{x_{j}\},1,a_{j}};q,t\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\times\prod_{s=1}^{m}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[l..i_{s}-1]\\
b_{j}=+\end{subarray}}H\bigl(\rho_{3,a_{i_{s}}}(W^{[s]};q,t);\rho_{\{x_{j}\},1,a_{j}};q,t\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\times\prod_{1\leq p<q\leq m}T_{a_{i_{p}},a_{i_{q}}}\bigl(W^{[p]},W^{[q]}\bigr)\prod_{k\geq 1}\Theta_{k,\mathbf{i}}.

Here
\Pr=\mathbb{P}_{c_{l},c_{r}}
is the probability measure (
2.14
), and
the contours are those inherited from Lemma
4.8
under the specialization
of Lemma
4.3
.

Proof.

Apply Lemma
4.8
and specialize the generalized Macdonald-process
parameters as in Lemma
4.3
. All unmarked columns have empty Neguţ
variable sets, so the global specializations
\rho_{W},\rho_{W}^{\circ}
of
Lemma
4.8
reduce to the active finite unions

\rho_{W,\mathbf{i}},\rho_{W,\mathbf{i}}^{\circ}
above.

Under the dimer specialization, the nonzero
A
-specializations occur
exactly at indices
j
with
b_{j}=-
, while the nonzero
B
-specializations
occur exactly at indices
j
with
b_{j}=+
. This gives the two displayed
one-body products. The finite interaction between two marked blocks is

T_{a_{i_{p}},a_{i_{q}}}
by Lemma
5.1
. Finally,
the reflected boundary part of Lemma
4.8
is precisely the product over

k\geq 1
of the factors
\Theta_{k,\mathbf{i}}
in (
5.1
).
The normalization is the same as in (
2.14
), so the stated formula follows.
∎

For later use, we record the explicit one-body kernels coming from the specialization by a
single edge weight:

(5.2)

\displaystyle G_{1,LL}(W,x;q,t)

\displaystyle:=\prod_{w\in W}\frac{w-\frac{qx}{t}}{w-qx},

\displaystyle G_{1,LR}(W,x;q,t)

\displaystyle:=\prod_{w\in W}\frac{tw+q^{2}x}{tw+qx},

(5.3)

\displaystyle G_{1,RL}(W,x;q,t)

\displaystyle:=\prod_{w\in W}\frac{w+t^{-1}x}{w+x},

\displaystyle G_{1,RR}(W,x;q,t)

\displaystyle:=\prod_{w\in W}\frac{tw-qx}{tw-x},

(5.4)

\displaystyle G_{0,LL}(W,x;q,t)

\displaystyle:=\prod_{w\in W}\frac{q-wx}{q-twx},

\displaystyle G_{0,LR}(W,x;q,t)

\displaystyle:=\prod_{w\in W}\frac{1+wx}{1+q^{-1}wx},

(5.5)

\displaystyle G_{0,RL}(W,x;q,t)

\displaystyle:=\prod_{w\in W}\frac{1+wx}{1+twx},

\displaystyle G_{0,RR}(W,x;q,t)

\displaystyle:=\prod_{w\in W}\frac{1-twx}{1-qtwx}.

Lemma 5.3

(Left-chart moment formula)
.

Let
i_{1}\leq\cdots\leq i_{m}
be integers in
[l+1..r]
, and assume

a_{i_{1}}=\cdots=a_{i_{m}}=L.

For each occurrence
s\in[m]
, let
W^{[s]}
be an ordered set of variables
with
|W^{[s]}|=g_{s}
. Let
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{m})
, and let

\Theta_{k,\mathbf{i}}
be the active reflected factor defined in
(
5.1
). Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Pr}\Biggl[\prod_{s=1}^{m}\gamma_{g_{s}}\bigl(\lambda^{(i_{s})};q,t\bigr)\Biggr]

\displaystyle=\oint\cdots\oint\prod_{s=1}^{m}D\bigl(W^{[s]};q,t\bigr)\,J_{s}\bigl(W^{[s]}\bigr)\prod_{1\leq p<q\leq m}T_{L,L}\bigl(W^{[p]},W^{[q]}\bigr)\prod_{k\geq 1}\Theta_{k,\mathbf{i}},

where

\displaystyle J_{s}(W)

\displaystyle:=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[i_{s}..r]\\
b_{j}=-,\ a_{j}=L\end{subarray}}G_{1,LL}(W,x_{j};q,t)\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[i_{s}..r]\\
b_{j}=-,\ a_{j}=R\end{subarray}}G_{1,LR}(W,x_{j};q,t)

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[l..i_{s}-1]\\
b_{j}=+,\ a_{j}=L\end{subarray}}G_{0,LL}(W,x_{j};q,t)\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[l..i_{s}-1]\\
b_{j}=+,\ a_{j}=R\end{subarray}}G_{0,LR}(W,x_{j};q,t).

Proof.

This is the
L
-marked specialization of Lemma
5.2
. Since

a_{i_{s}}=L
for every marked occurrence
s
, the
D
-kernel becomes

D(W^{[s]};q,t)
, the one-body
H
-factors reduce to the four
G
-kernels
appearing in the definition of
J_{s}
, and the finite interaction between two
marked blocks is always
T_{L,L}
. The reflected boundary factors are
unchanged and are exactly the active factors
\Theta_{k,\mathbf{i}}
. This
gives the formula.
∎

5.3.
Periodic regrouping and asymptotic one-body factors

Let
\epsilon>0
and suppose we are in the setting of Assumption
2.5
. For

p\in[m]
,
j\in[n]
, and
i\in[l^{(\epsilon)}..r^{(\epsilon)}]
, define

\displaystyle I_{j,p,1,L}^{(\epsilon)}

\displaystyle:=\Bigl\{u\in[v_{p-1}^{(\epsilon)}+1..v_{p}^{(\epsilon)}]\cap(n{\mathbb{Z}}+j)\cap[i..r^{(\epsilon)}]:b_{u}^{(\epsilon)}=-,\ a_{u}=L\Bigr\},

\displaystyle I_{j,p,0,L}^{(\epsilon)}

\displaystyle:=\Bigl\{u\in[v_{p-1}^{(\epsilon)}+1..v_{p}^{(\epsilon)}]\cap(n{\mathbb{Z}}+j)\cap[l^{(\epsilon)}..i-1]:b_{u}^{(\epsilon)}=+,\ a_{u}=L\Bigr\},

\displaystyle I_{j,p,1,R}^{(\epsilon)}

\displaystyle:=\Bigl\{u\in[v_{p-1}^{(\epsilon)}+1..v_{p}^{(\epsilon)}]\cap(n{\mathbb{Z}}+j)\cap[i..r^{(\epsilon)}]:b_{u}^{(\epsilon)}=-,\ a_{u}=R\Bigr\},

\displaystyle I_{j,p,0,R}^{(\epsilon)}

\displaystyle:=\Bigl\{u\in[v_{p-1}^{(\epsilon)}+1..v_{p}^{(\epsilon)}]\cap(n{\mathbb{Z}}+j)\cap[l^{(\epsilon)}..i-1]:b_{u}^{(\epsilon)}=+,\ a_{u}=R\Bigr\}.

Lemma 5.4
.

Suppose Assumption
2.5
and (
2.17
) hold. Fix
j\in[n]
and
p\in[m]
. Then, for
each of the four families

I_{j,p,1,L}^{(\epsilon)},\qquad I_{j,p,0,L}^{(\epsilon)},\qquad I_{j,p,1,R}^{(\epsilon)},\qquad I_{j,p,0,R}^{(\epsilon)},

either the set is empty for all sufficiently small
\epsilon
, or it is nonempty for all
sufficiently small
\epsilon
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from the piecewise periodicity in Assumption
2.5
(1) and
the fact that the residue class
(i^{(\epsilon)})_{\equiv_{n}}
is fixed in
\epsilon
by
(
2.17
).
∎

Accordingly, we define the eventual nonemptiness indicators

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,1,L}}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}1,&I_{j,p,1,L}^{(\epsilon)}\neq\emptyset\ \text{for all sufficiently small }\epsilon,\\
0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,0,L}}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}1,&I_{j,p,0,L}^{(\epsilon)}\neq\emptyset\ \text{for all sufficiently small }\epsilon,\\
0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,1,R}}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}1,&I_{j,p,1,R}^{(\epsilon)}\neq\emptyset\ \text{for all sufficiently small }\epsilon,\\
0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,0,R}}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}1,&I_{j,p,0,R}^{(\epsilon)}\neq\emptyset\ \text{for all sufficiently small }\epsilon,\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Lemma 5.5
.

Suppose Assumption
2.5
and (
2.17
) hold, and let

i^{(\epsilon)}\in[l^{(\epsilon)}..r^{(\epsilon)}]
satisfy

\epsilon i^{(\epsilon)}\to\chi,\qquad a_{i^{(\epsilon)}}=L.

Assume

(5.6)

\displaystyle q=t^{\alpha}

for some fixed
\alpha>0
. Define

\displaystyle G_{1,L}^{(\epsilon)}(W)

\displaystyle:=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[i^{(\epsilon)}..r^{(\epsilon)}]\\
b_{j}^{(\epsilon)}=-,\,a_{j}=L\end{subarray}}G_{1,LL}(W,x_{j}^{(\epsilon)};q,t),

\displaystyle G_{0,L}^{(\epsilon)}(W)

\displaystyle:=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[l^{(\epsilon)}..i^{(\epsilon)}-1]\\
b_{j}^{(\epsilon)}=+,\,a_{j}=L\end{subarray}}G_{0,LL}(W,x_{j}^{(\epsilon)};q,t),

\displaystyle G_{1,R}^{(\epsilon)}(W)

\displaystyle:=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[i^{(\epsilon)}..r^{(\epsilon)}]\\
b_{j}^{(\epsilon)}=-,\,a_{j}=R\end{subarray}}G_{1,LR}(W,x_{j}^{(\epsilon)};q,t),

\displaystyle G_{0,R}^{(\epsilon)}(W)

\displaystyle:=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[l^{(\epsilon)}..i^{(\epsilon)}-1]\\
b_{j}^{(\epsilon)}=+,\,a_{j}=R\end{subarray}}G_{0,LR}(W,x_{j}^{(\epsilon)};q,t).

Further define

(5.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{>\chi,L}(w)

\displaystyle:=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in[m]\\
V_{p}>\chi\end{subarray}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\left(\frac{1-e^{V_{p}}(w\tau_{j})^{-1}}{1-e^{\max\{V_{p-1},\chi\}}(w\tau_{j})^{-1}}\right)^{\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,1,L}}},

(5.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{<\chi,L}(w)

\displaystyle:=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in[m]\\
V_{p-1}<\chi\end{subarray}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\left(\frac{1-we^{-V_{p-1}}\tau_{j}}{1-we^{-\min\{V_{p},\chi\}}\tau_{j}}\right)^{\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,0,L}}},

(5.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{>\chi,R}(w)

\displaystyle:=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in[m]\\
V_{p}>\chi\end{subarray}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\left(\frac{1+e^{\max\{V_{p-1},\chi\}}(w\tau_{j})^{-1}}{1+e^{V_{p}}(w\tau_{j})^{-1}}\right)^{\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,1,R}}},

(5.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{<\chi,R}(w)

\displaystyle:=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in[m]\\
V_{p-1}<\chi\end{subarray}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\left(\frac{1+we^{-\min\{V_{p},\chi\}}\tau_{j}}{1+we^{-V_{p-1}}\tau_{j}}\right)^{\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,0,R}}}.

Then, uniformly for
W
on a fixed compact set avoiding the singularities,

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}G_{1,L}^{(\epsilon)}(W)

\displaystyle=\left[\prod_{w\in W}\mathcal{G}_{>\chi,L}(w)\right]^{\beta},

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}G_{0,L}^{(\epsilon)}(W)

\displaystyle=\left[\prod_{w\in W}\mathcal{G}_{<\chi,L}(w)\right]^{\beta},

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}G_{1,R}^{(\epsilon)}(W)

\displaystyle=\left[\prod_{w\in W}\mathcal{G}_{>\chi,R}(w)\right]^{\alpha\beta},

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}G_{0,R}^{(\epsilon)}(W)

\displaystyle=\left[\prod_{w\in W}\mathcal{G}_{<\chi,R}(w)\right]^{\alpha\beta}.

We choose the logarithmic branches so that
\log z
is real on the positive real axis.

Proof.

Let
N_{j,p,1,L}^{(\epsilon)}:=|I_{j,p,1,L}^{(\epsilon)}|
, and similarly for the other three
families. Also let
q_{j,p,1,L}^{(\epsilon)}:=\max I_{j,p,1,L}^{(\epsilon)}
when the set is
nonempty, and use the same convention for the other families; when a set is empty we set
the corresponding endpoint to
\pm\infty
and interpret the associated weight as
0
.

Grouping the product according to the periodic blocks and residue classes modulo
n

gives, for example,

\displaystyle G_{1,L}^{(\epsilon)}(W)=\prod_{p=1}^{m}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\prod_{w\in W}\frac{\left(\frac{q}{t}w^{-1}x_{q_{j,p,1,L}^{(\epsilon)}}^{(\epsilon)};e^{-n\epsilon}\right)_{N_{j,p,1,L}^{(\epsilon)}}}{\left(qw^{-1}x_{q_{j,p,1,L}^{(\epsilon)}}^{(\epsilon)};e^{-n\epsilon}\right)_{N_{j,p,1,L}^{(\epsilon)}}},

and analogous formulas for
G_{0,L}^{(\epsilon)}
,
G_{1,R}^{(\epsilon)}
, and

G_{0,R}^{(\epsilon)}
. Here

(5.11)

\displaystyle(a;q)_{N}=\prod_{i=0}^{N-1}(1-aq^{i}).

Applying Lemma
A.9
and using Assumption
2.5
(2) we
obtain

\displaystyle n\epsilon N_{j,p,1,L}^{(\epsilon)}

\displaystyle\to V_{p}-\max\{V_{p-1},\chi\},

\displaystyle n\epsilon N_{j,p,0,L}^{(\epsilon)}

\displaystyle\to\min\{V_{p},\chi\}-V_{p-1},

\displaystyle n\epsilon N_{j,p,1,R}^{(\epsilon)}

\displaystyle\to V_{p}-\max\{V_{p-1},\chi\},

\displaystyle n\epsilon N_{j,p,0,R}^{(\epsilon)}

\displaystyle\to\min\{V_{p},\chi\}-V_{p-1},

whenever the corresponding sets are eventually nonempty. At the same time, when the corresponding set is nonempty,

\displaystyle x_{q_{j,p,1,L}^{(\epsilon)}}^{(\epsilon)}

\displaystyle\to\tau_{j}^{-1}e^{V_{p}},

\displaystyle x_{q_{j,p,0,L}^{(\epsilon)}}^{(\epsilon)}

\displaystyle\to\tau_{j}e^{-\min\{V_{p},\chi\}},

\displaystyle x_{q_{j,p,1,R}^{(\epsilon)}}^{(\epsilon)}

\displaystyle\to\tau_{j}^{-1}e^{V_{p}},

\displaystyle x_{q_{j,p,0,R}^{(\epsilon)}}^{(\epsilon)}

\displaystyle\to\tau_{j}e^{-\min\{V_{p},\chi\}}.

Combining these limits gives the stated formulas.
∎

For
w\in\mathbb{C}
and
\chi\in{\mathbb{R}}
, define

(5.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w):=\mathcal{G}_{>\chi,L}(w)\,\mathcal{G}_{<\chi,L}(w)\,\Bigl[\mathcal{G}_{>\chi,R}(w)\mathcal{G}_{<\chi,R}(w)\Bigr]^{\alpha}.

The following zero/pole sets will be used to specify the limiting contours:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\chi,1,1}

\displaystyle:=\left\{e^{\max\{V_{p-1},\chi\}}\tau_{j}^{-1}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}V_{p}>\chi,\ j\in[n]\\
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,1,L}}=1\end{subarray}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\chi,1,2}

\displaystyle:=\left\{e^{V_{p}}\tau_{j}^{-1}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}V_{p}>\chi,\ j\in[n]\\
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,1,L}}=1\end{subarray}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\chi,2,1}

\displaystyle:=\left\{e^{\min\{V_{p},\chi\}}\tau_{j}^{-1}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}V_{p-1}<\chi,\ j\in[n]\\
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,0,L}}=1\end{subarray}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\chi,2,2}

\displaystyle:=\left\{e^{V_{p-1}}\tau_{j}^{-1}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}V_{p-1}<\chi,\ j\in[n]\\
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,0,L}}=1\end{subarray}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\chi,3,1}

\displaystyle:=\left\{-e^{V_{p}}\tau_{j}^{-1}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}V_{p}>\chi,\ j\in[n]\\
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,1,R}}=1\end{subarray}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\chi,3,2}

\displaystyle:=\left\{-e^{\max\{V_{p-1},\chi\}}\tau_{j}^{-1}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}V_{p}>\chi,\ j\in[n]\\
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,1,R}}=1\end{subarray}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\chi,4,1}

\displaystyle:=\left\{-e^{V_{p-1}}\tau_{j}^{-1}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}V_{p-1}<\chi,\ j\in[n]\\
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,0,R}}=1\end{subarray}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\chi,4,2}

\displaystyle:=\left\{-e^{\min\{V_{p},\chi\}}\tau_{j}^{-1}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}V_{p-1}<\chi,\ j\in[n]\\
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,0,R}}=1\end{subarray}}.

For
k_{1},k_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
, define also

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{5,1,k_{1},k_{2}}

\displaystyle:=\left\{u^{2k_{1}}v^{2k_{2}}e^{V_{p-1}}\tau_{j}^{-1}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in[m],\ j\in[n]\\
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,1,L}}=1\end{subarray}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{5,2,k_{1},k_{2}}

\displaystyle:=\left\{u^{2k_{1}}v^{2k_{2}}e^{V_{p}}\tau_{j}^{-1}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in[m],\ j\in[n]\\
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,1,L}}=1\end{subarray}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{6,1,k_{1},k_{2}}

\displaystyle:=\left\{u^{-2k_{1}}v^{-2k_{2}}e^{V_{p}}\tau_{j}^{-1}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in[m],\ j\in[n]\\
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,0,L}}=1\end{subarray}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{6,2,k_{1},k_{2}}

\displaystyle:=\left\{u^{-2k_{1}}v^{-2k_{2}}e^{V_{p-1}}\tau_{j}^{-1}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in[m],\ j\in[n]\\
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,0,L}}=1\end{subarray}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{7,1,k_{1},k_{2}}

\displaystyle:=\left\{-u^{2k_{1}}v^{2k_{2}}e^{V_{p}}\tau_{j}^{-1}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in[m],\ j\in[n]\\
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,1,R}}=1\end{subarray}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{7,2,k_{1},k_{2}}

\displaystyle:=\left\{-u^{2k_{1}}v^{2k_{2}}e^{V_{p-1}}\tau_{j}^{-1}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in[m],\ j\in[n]\\
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,1,R}}=1\end{subarray}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{8,1,k_{1},k_{2}}

\displaystyle:=\left\{-u^{-2k_{1}}v^{-2k_{2}}e^{V_{p-1}}\tau_{j}^{-1}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in[m],\ j\in[n]\\
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,0,R}}=1\end{subarray}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{8,2,k_{1},k_{2}}

\displaystyle:=\left\{-u^{-2k_{1}}v^{-2k_{2}}e^{V_{p}}\tau_{j}^{-1}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in[m],\ j\in[n]\\
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j,p,0,R}}=1\end{subarray}}.

5.4.
One-point asymptotics

We shall obtain asymptotic formulas only for columns of type
L
. This is enough for the
proof of the Gaussian fluctuation theorem, and the case of type
R
follows from the same
argument together with Lemma
4.6
.
Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{G}_{>V_{0},L}(u^{-2k}v^{-2k}w)\,\mathcal{G}_{<V_{m},L}(u^{2k}v^{2k}w)\,\Bigl[\mathcal{G}_{>V_{0},R}(u^{-2k}v^{-2k}w)\,\mathcal{G}_{<V_{m},R}(u^{2k}v^{2k}w)\Bigr]^{\alpha}

(5.13)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\times\mathcal{G}_{>V_{0},L}(u^{2-2k}v^{-2k}w)\,\mathcal{G}_{<V_{m},L}(u^{2k}v^{2k-2}w)\,\Bigl[\mathcal{G}_{>V_{0},R}(u^{2-2k}v^{-2k}w)\,\mathcal{G}_{<V_{m},R}(u^{2k}v^{2k-2}w)\Bigr]^{\alpha}.

Lemma 5.6
.

Fix
g\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, and let

W=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{g})

range over the product of an admissible one-point Neguţ contour family. We assume that
the contour paths are contained in a fixed compact annulus

\{R_{-}<|w|<R_{+}\}.

Fix the marked column
i=i^{(\epsilon)}
. In the one-point specialization of
Lemma
4.8
, we take

W^{(i)}=W,\qquad W^{(j)}=\varnothing\quad\text{for }j\neq i.

Set

\rho_{W,i}:=\rho_{2,a_{i}}(W;q,t),\qquad\rho_{W,i}^{\circ}:=\rho_{3,a_{i}}(W;q,t).

The graph specializations
\rho_{A},\rho_{B}
are the full graph specializations from
Lemma
4.8
, after the dimer specialization of Lemma
4.3
.

For
k\geq 1
, define the
k
-th reflected one-point correction factor by

\displaystyle\Theta_{k}^{(\epsilon)}(W)

\displaystyle:=\Theta_{c_{l}}\!\left([u^{2k-2}v^{2k-1}]\rho_{W,i};q,t\right)\Theta_{c_{r}}\!\left([u^{2k-1}v^{2k}]\rho_{W,i};q,t\right)

\displaystyle\quad\times\Theta_{c_{l}}\!\left([u^{2k-1}v^{2k-2}]\rho_{W,i}^{\circ};q,t\right)\Theta_{c_{r}}\!\left([u^{2k}v^{2k-1}]\rho_{W,i}^{\circ};q,t\right)

\displaystyle\quad\times H\!\left(u^{2k}\rho_{B};v^{2k}\rho_{W,i}^{\circ};q,t\right)H\!\left(u^{2k-2}\rho_{B};v^{2k}\rho_{W,i};q,t\right)H\!\left(u^{2k}\rho_{W,i};v^{2k}\rho_{W,i}^{\circ};q,t\right)

(5.14)

\displaystyle\quad\times H\!\left(u^{2k}\rho_{A};v^{2k}\rho_{W,i};q,t\right)H\!\left(u^{2k}\rho_{A};v^{2k-2}\rho_{W,i}^{\circ};q,t\right).

Here
q,t,\rho_{A},\rho_{B},\rho_{W,i},\rho_{W,i}^{\circ}
depend on

\epsilon
, but this dependence is suppressed from the notation.

If
u,v\in(0,1)
are sufficiently small, then:

(1)

for each fixed
k\geq 1
,

\Theta_{k}^{(\epsilon)}(W)\longrightarrow\prod_{s=1}^{g}\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w_{s})\bigr]^{\beta}

uniformly on the product contour;

(2)

there exist constants
C>0
and
\mathfrak{r}\in(0,1)
, independent of

k
and
\epsilon
, such that for all sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
,

\sup_{W}\bigl|\log\Theta_{k}^{(\epsilon)}(W)\bigr|\leq C\mathfrak{r}^{k};

(3)

consequently,

\prod_{k\geq 1}\Theta_{k}^{(\epsilon)}(W)\longrightarrow\prod_{s=1}^{g}\prod_{k\geq 1}\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w_{s})\bigr]^{\beta}

uniformly on the product contour.

Proof.

Throughout the proof we omit the repeated parameters
(q,t)
from

H
,
\Theta_{\ast}
, and the specializations; all of them are evaluated at
the finite-
\epsilon
parameters

q=t^{\alpha},\qquad t=e^{-n\beta\epsilon}.

We first record the two power-sum estimates used below. Let
d\geq 1
. Since
the
W
-contours lie in a fixed compact annulus, there is a constant

A>1
such that

|w_{s}^{d}|+|w_{s}^{-d}|\leq A^{d},\qquad s=1,\ldots,g.

In this one-point specialization only the marked block
W
is active, so

\rho_{W,i}
and
\rho_{W,i}^{\circ}
contain only the fixed set

W=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{g})
, not a union over
O(\epsilon^{-1})
columns.
Using (
4.8
)–(
4.9
) and the power-sum rules for scaled and

(-1)
-specializations, we have

\displaystyle p_{d}(\rho_{2,L}(W;q,t))

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{q}{t}\right)^{d}(q^{d}-1)\,p_{d}(W^{-1}),

\displaystyle p_{d}(\rho_{3,L}(W;q,t))

\displaystyle=(1-q^{-d})\,p_{d}(W),

\displaystyle p_{d}(\rho_{2,R}(W;q,t))

\displaystyle=(-1)^{d-1}\frac{1-q^{d}}{1-t^{d}}(t^{-d}-1)\,p_{d}(W^{-1}),

\displaystyle p_{d}(\rho_{3,R}(W;q,t))

\displaystyle=(-1)^{d-1}(1-q^{d})\,p_{d}(W).

Under the exact Jack scaling, the difference factors

q^{d}-1,\qquad 1-q^{-d},\qquad t^{-d}-1,\qquad 1-q^{d}

are bounded by
CA^{d}\epsilon
, after increasing
A
. Also

\left|\frac{1-q^{d}}{1-t^{d}}\right|\leq CA^{d}.

Hence there exist constants
C_{0},A_{0}>0
, independent of
d,k,\epsilon
, and

W
, such that

(5.15)

|p_{d}(\rho_{W,i})|+|p_{d}(\rho_{W,i}^{\circ})|\leq C_{0}A_{0}^{d}\epsilon.

For the graph specializations there is no difference factor. Under the dimer
specialization of Lemma
4.3
, the nonzero parts of
\rho_{A}
and

\rho_{B}
are the graph one-variable specializations. For a single graph
variable
x
,

p_{d}(\rho_{\{x\},1,L})=x^{d},\qquad p_{d}(\rho_{\{x\},1,R})=(-1)^{d-1}\frac{1-q^{d}}{1-t^{d}}x^{d}.

The periodic weight assumption on bounded macroscopic intervals gives

|x_{j}|^{d}\leq CA^{d}

uniformly in
j
and
\epsilon
. Since the number of graph variables is

O(\epsilon^{-1})
, after increasing
C_{0},A_{0}
we get

(5.16)

\epsilon\bigl(|p_{d}(\rho_{A})|+|p_{d}(\rho_{B})|\bigr)\leq C_{0}A_{0}^{d}.

We now prove the summable bound in
k
. By Lemma
3.11
, each
boundary factor
\Theta_{\ast}
in (
5.14
) is the
exponential of a linear-quadratic expression in the power sums of its
argument. If

\eta=\sigma_{k}\rho_{W,i}\quad\text{or}\quad\eta=\sigma_{k}\rho_{W,i}^{\circ},

where
\sigma_{k}
is one of

u^{2k-2}v^{2k-1},\quad u^{2k-1}v^{2k},\quad u^{2k-1}v^{2k-2},\quad u^{2k}v^{2k-1},

then (
5.15
) gives

|p_{d}(\eta)|\leq C_{0}(A_{0}\sigma_{k})^{d}\epsilon.

Choosing
u,v
sufficiently small, the corresponding linear and quadratic
power-sum series are uniformly summable. Thus the logarithm of the product
of the four boundary
\Theta
-factors is bounded by

C\epsilon\mathfrak{r}^{k}

for some
\mathfrak{r}\in(0,1)
. In particular, these boundary factors tend
to
1
for fixed
k
, and satisfy the weaker bound
C\mathfrak{r}^{k}

uniformly in
\epsilon
.

For the Cauchy factors we use

\log H(\rho_{1};\rho_{2})=\sum_{d\geq 1}\frac{1-t^{d}}{1-q^{d}}\frac{p_{d}(\rho_{1})p_{d}(\rho_{2})}{d}.

The factor

H(u^{2k}\rho_{W,i};v^{2k}\rho_{W,i}^{\circ})

has two
W
-type specializations. By (
5.15
), its logarithm
is
O(\epsilon^{2}\mathfrak{r}^{k})
, after decreasing
\mathfrak{r}
if necessary.

The remaining four
H
-factors are different. In each of them, one
specialization is of graph type and the other is of
W
-type. Thus the
graph side contributes
O(\epsilon^{-1})
by (
5.16
), while the

W
-side contributes
O(\epsilon)
by (
5.15
). These powers
of
\epsilon
cancel. For instance,

p_{d}(u^{2k}\rho_{A})\,p_{d}(v^{2k}\rho_{W,i})=u^{2kd}v^{2kd}\,p_{d}(\rho_{A})p_{d}(\rho_{W,i})

is bounded by a geometrically summable majorant in
d
, uniformly in

\epsilon
. The same argument applies to the other three graph–
W

factors, with scale factors

u^{2k}v^{2k},\qquad u^{2k-2}v^{2k},\qquad u^{2k}v^{2k-2}.

For
u,v
sufficiently small, these scale factors give geometric decay in

k
. Combining the four boundary terms, the
W
–
W
Cauchy factor, and
the four graph–
W
Cauchy factors, we obtain

\sup_{W}\bigl|\log\Theta_{k}^{(\epsilon)}(W)\bigr|\leq C\mathfrak{r}^{k},

for all sufficiently small
\epsilon
. This proves (2).

It remains to identify the fixed-
k
limit. The four boundary factors have
logarithms
O(\epsilon)
, and the
W
–
W
Cauchy factor has logarithm

O(\epsilon^{2})
. Hence these five factors converge to
1
. The only
nontrivial limits come from the four graph–
W
factors

H(u^{2k}\rho_{B};v^{2k}\rho_{W,i}^{\circ}),\qquad H(u^{2k-2}\rho_{B};v^{2k}\rho_{W,i}),

H(u^{2k}\rho_{A};v^{2k}\rho_{W,i}),\qquad H(u^{2k}\rho_{A};v^{2k-2}\rho_{W,i}^{\circ}).

By Lemma
5.5
, the logarithms of these four factors have uniform
periodic Riemann-sum limits on the admissible contour neighborhoods. Splitting
the graph columns into
L
- and
R
-types gives precisely the eight
reflected products appearing in the definition (
5.13
) of

\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}
. Therefore, for each fixed
k
,

\Theta_{k}^{(\epsilon)}(W)\longrightarrow\prod_{s=1}^{g}\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w_{s})\bigr]^{\beta}

uniformly on the product contour. This proves (1).

Finally, the estimate in (2) gives a summable majorant in
k
,
uniformly for all sufficiently small
\epsilon
and uniformly on the product
contour. Hence, for any
N
,

\prod_{k=1}^{N}\Theta_{k}^{(\epsilon)}(W)\longrightarrow\prod_{s=1}^{g}\prod_{k=1}^{N}\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w_{s})\bigr]^{\beta}

uniformly, and the tails are controlled by

\sum_{k>N}C\mathfrak{r}^{k}.

Letting first
\epsilon\to 0
and then
N\to\infty
proves (3).
∎

One-point pole sets.

For the one-point asymptotics below, we use the limiting pole families

\mathcal{R}_{\chi,a,b}
and
\mathcal{R}_{j,a,k_{1},k_{2}}
defined above.
The
required
limiting pole set is

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\chi}

\displaystyle:=(\mathcal{R}_{\chi,1,1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{\chi,1,2})\cup(\mathcal{R}_{\chi,3,1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{\chi,3,2})

\displaystyle\quad\cup\bigcup_{k\geq 1}\Bigl[(\mathcal{R}_{5,1,k,k}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{5,2,k,k})\cup(\mathcal{R}_{7,1,k,k}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{7,2,k,k})

(5.17)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\cup(\mathcal{R}_{5,1,k-1,k}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{5,2,k-1,k})\cup(\mathcal{R}_{7,1,k-1,k}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{7,2,k-1,k})\Bigr].

The set
\mathcal{D}_{\chi}
consists of the limiting poles which must be
enclosed by the one-point Neguţ contour.

Excluded one-point family.

Besides the required pole set
\mathcal{D}_{\chi}
, we use the following
excluded point family:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\chi}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{R}_{\chi,2,1}\cup\mathcal{R}_{\chi,2,2}\cup\mathcal{R}_{\chi,4,1}\cup\mathcal{R}_{\chi,4,2}

\displaystyle\quad\cup\bigcup_{k\geq 1}\Bigl[\mathcal{R}_{6,1,k,k}\cup\mathcal{R}_{6,2,k,k}\cup\mathcal{R}_{8,1,k,k}\cup\mathcal{R}_{8,2,k,k}

(5.18)

\displaystyle\hskip 91.04881pt\cup\mathcal{R}_{6,1,k,k-1}\cup\mathcal{R}_{6,2,k,k-1}\cup\mathcal{R}_{8,1,k,k-1}\cup\mathcal{R}_{8,2,k,k-1}\Bigr].

The elements of
\mathcal{E}_{\chi}
are the points which must remain outside
the one-point contour. They are not assumed to be all poles.

Contour and branch convention for the one-point asymptotics.

In Proposition
5.11
and in the subsequent one-point limit-shape and
Stieltjes-transform formulae, we work under the one-point contour and branch
admissibility hypotheses of
Assumption
5.7
. Thus the admissible
one-point contour
\mathcal{C}_{\chi}
separates the required pole set

\mathcal{D}_{\chi}
from the excluded point family
\mathcal{E}_{\chi}
. The
integration path lies in a pole-free annular neighborhood. It may enclose
infinitely many reflected required poles accumulating at
0
, while excluded
reflected points may escape to
\infty
.

On the annular neighborhood of
\mathcal{C}_{\chi}
, the
function

S_{\chi}(w)=\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)

is required to be holomorphic and non-vanishing, and all powers of
S_{\chi}

used below are taken with the admissible logarithm specified in
Assumption
5.7
.

5.5.
Contour and branch admissibility

The one-point and multi-point contour formulae below are stated under the
following analytic admissibility hypothesis.

Assumption 5.7

(Contour and zero-winding branch admissibility)
.

Fix boundary types
c_{l},c_{r}\in\{el,oa,deel,eoa\}
and

u,v\in(0,1)
.

(i)

One-point contours.

For every macroscopic
L
-type marked column
\chi
used below, there is a
positively oriented contour
\mathcal{C}_{\chi}
enclosing
0
and separating
the one-point families

\mathcal{D}_{\chi}\subset\operatorname{Int}(\mathcal{C}_{\chi}),\qquad\mathcal{E}_{\chi}\cap\overline{\operatorname{Int}(\mathcal{C}_{\chi})}=\varnothing.

Moreover, there is an open annular neighborhood
U_{\chi}
of

\mathcal{C}_{\chi}
, independent of
\epsilon
for all sufficiently small

\epsilon
, which contains no finite-
\epsilon
pole of the one-point
integrand and no pole of the limiting one-point integrand.

(ii)

Branches.

On
U_{\chi}
, the function

S_{\chi}(w):=\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)

is holomorphic and non-vanishing. Moreover,

(5.19)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\mathcal{C}_{\chi}}\frac{S_{\chi}^{\prime}(w)}{S_{\chi}(w)}\,dw=0.

Thus
S_{\chi}
admits a single-valued logarithm on
U_{\chi}
, denoted

\mathrm{Log}_{\chi,\mathcal{C}_{\chi}}S_{\chi}
, and all non-integer powers
below are defined through this logarithm.

(iii)

Multi-point contours.

For every finite family of
L
-type marked columns used in the Gaussian
theorem, the admissible one-point contours may be chosen simultaneously,
nested when necessary, so that each one-point pole-separation and branch
condition is preserved and every product contour avoids the two-variable
singularities of the
LL
-covariance kernel. The branch choices are kept
fixed along the contour deformations used in the proof.

Proposition 5.8

(Automatic one-point branch from separated zero–pole data)
.

Fix a macroscopic
L
-type marked location

\chi\in(V_{0},V_{m})\setminus\{V_{1},\ldots,V_{m-1}\}.

For
K\geq 1
, let

S_{\chi,K}(w):=\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{k=1}^{K}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)

and let

\omega_{\chi,K}:=d\log S_{\chi,K}

be its logarithmic-derivative one-form, obtained by differentiating the
logarithms of the elementary factors.

Assume that there exist a positively oriented simple contour
C_{\chi}
, a
connected annular neighbourhood
U_{\chi}\Subset\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
of
C_{\chi}
,
and
K_{0}\geq 1
, such that the following hold.

(a)

The contour separates the required and excluded one-point families:

\mathcal{D}_{\chi}\subset\operatorname{Int}(C_{\chi}),\qquad\mathcal{E}_{\chi}\cap\operatorname{Int}(C_{\chi})=\varnothing.

(b)

The closure of
U_{\chi}
contains no limiting zero or pole location of
the one-point factors. For every
K\geq K_{0}
, no zero or pole of the finite
truncation lies on
U_{\chi}
. Moreover,

\omega_{\chi,K}\longrightarrow\omega_{\chi}

locally uniformly on
U_{\chi}
, where

\omega_{\chi}:=d\log G_{\chi}+\sum_{k\geq 1}d\log F_{u,v,k}.

In the contour neighbourhoods used below, this convergence follows from
Lemma
5.6
.

(c)

For every
K\geq K_{0}
, the finite logarithmic derivative has a
factorization by exponent classes:

\omega_{\chi,K}=\alpha\left(\frac{A_{K}^{\prime}(w)}{A_{K}(w)}-\frac{B_{K}^{\prime}(w)}{B_{K}(w)}\right)dw+\left(\frac{C_{K}^{\prime}(w)}{C_{K}(w)}-\frac{D_{K}^{\prime}(w)}{D_{K}(w)}\right)dw,

where
A_{K},B_{K},C_{K},D_{K}
are polynomials with no zero on
U_{\chi}
. Their
zeros inside
C_{\chi}
, counted with multiplicity, cancel separately in the
two exponent classes:

N_{A}(C_{\chi})=N_{B}(C_{\chi}),\qquad N_{C}(C_{\chi})=N_{D}(C_{\chi}).

Then

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{C_{\chi}}\omega_{\chi}=0.

Consequently
\omega_{\chi}
has a single-valued holomorphic primitive on

U_{\chi}
. After fixing its value at one point of
U_{\chi}
, this primitive
defines a holomorphic non-vanishing branch

S_{\chi}^{U}(w):=\exp\!\left(\int^{w}\omega_{\chi}\right)

of the formal infinite product

G_{\chi}(w)\prod_{k\geq 1}F_{u,v,k}(w)

on
U_{\chi}
. Hence the one-point logarithmic-branch condition (
5.19
) in
Assumption
5.7
(ii)
holds at

\chi
.

Proof.

For finite
K
, the logarithmic derivative on
U_{\chi}
is

\frac{S_{\chi,K}^{\prime}(w)}{S_{\chi,K}(w)}=\alpha\left(\frac{A_{K}^{\prime}(w)}{A_{K}(w)}-\frac{B_{K}^{\prime}(w)}{B_{K}(w)}\right)+\left(\frac{C_{K}^{\prime}(w)}{C_{K}(w)}-\frac{D_{K}^{\prime}(w)}{D_{K}(w)}\right).

Therefore, by the argument principle,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{C_{\chi}}\frac{S_{\chi,K}^{\prime}(w)}{S_{\chi,K}(w)}\,dw

\displaystyle=\alpha\bigl(N_{A}(C_{\chi})-N_{B}(C_{\chi})\bigr)+\bigl(N_{C}(C_{\chi})-N_{D}(C_{\chi})\bigr)

\displaystyle=0.

This calculation is made at the level of logarithmic derivatives and therefore
does not require
\alpha
to be an integer.

By the normal convergence of the reflected products on
U_{\chi}
,

S_{\chi,K}\longrightarrow S_{\chi}

locally uniformly on
U_{\chi}
. Since
\overline{U}_{\chi}
is disjoint from
the limiting zero and pole locations, the limit
S_{\chi}
is holomorphic and
non-vanishing on
U_{\chi}
. The logarithmic derivatives converge uniformly
on
C_{\chi}
, for instance by Cauchy’s estimates applied on a slightly
smaller annular neighbourhood. Passing to the limit in the finite-
K

zero-winding identity gives (
5.19
).
Thus
S_{\chi}
has zero winding on the annular neighbourhood
U_{\chi}
, and
therefore admits a single-valued holomorphic logarithm there.
∎

Proposition 5.9

(Multi-point admissibility from annular one-point contours)
.

Fix finitely many
L
-type marked locations

\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{s}.

Assume that for each
\chi_{d}
the one-point contour and branch conditions
of Assumption
5.7
(i)–(ii)
hold.
Assume moreover that the one-point contours may be chosen as pairwise disjoint
simple contours

C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}

contained in the annulus

(5.20)

\displaystyle A_{u,v}:=\{w\in\mathbb{C}:\ v<|w|<u^{-1}\},

with sufficiently small annular neighbourhoods preserving the one-point
separation and branch conditions. Repeated marked locations are represented by
sufficiently close nested parallel copies.

Then the contours satisfy Assumption
5.7

(iii)
. In particular, for every
d\neq e
, the product contour

C_{d}\times C_{e}
avoids all two-variable singularity loci of the

LL
-covariance kernel.

Proof.

Let
z\in C_{d}
,
w\in C_{e}
, with
d\neq e
. Since the contours are disjoint,

z\neq w
. Put

\mathfrak{q}=(uv)^{2},\qquad c_{r}=\mathfrak{q}^{r},\qquad a_{r}=v^{2}\mathfrak{q}^{r-1},\qquad b_{r}=u^{2}\mathfrak{q}^{r-1},\qquad r\geq 1.

Because
C_{d},C_{e}\subset A_{u,v}
, we have

v<|z|,|w|<u^{-1}.

Therefore

|c_{r}w|<c_{r}u^{-1}=v(uv)^{2r-1}<v<|z|,

so
z\neq c_{r}w
. Interchanging
z
and
w
gives
w\neq c_{r}z
.
Moreover,

|zw|>v^{2}\geq v^{2}\mathfrak{q}^{r-1}=a_{r},

so
zw\neq a_{r}
. Finally,

|b_{r}zw|<b_{r}u^{-2}=\mathfrak{q}^{r-1}\leq 1,

with strict inequality in the first step; hence
b_{r}zw\neq 1
. Thus none of
the
LL
-interaction singularities

z=w,\qquad z=c_{r}w,\qquad w=c_{r}z,\qquad zw=a_{r},\qquad b_{r}zw=1

occurs on
C_{d}\times C_{e}
.

The one-point pole-separation and branch conditions are preserved by the
choice of annular neighbourhoods. Since all product contours are compact and
the limiting
LL
-singular loci are avoided by a positive distance, the
finite-
\epsilon
two-variable singularities in the Macdonald contour formula
are also avoided for all sufficiently small
\epsilon
. Hence the simultaneous
multi-point contour condition in Assumption
5.7

(iii) holds.
∎

Remark 5.10

(Concrete separated zero–pole criteria)
.

Assumption
5.7
is stated in analytic form
because the one-point and multi-point asymptotic theorems only need the existence
of suitable contours, logarithmic branches, and product contours. Propositions

5.8
and

5.9
give convenient sufficient criteria for
the two nontrivial parts of this assumption.

A concrete source of these criteria is the separated zero–pole setup of the
Schur two-free-boundary model. More precisely, suppose that the finite
truncations have the separated inward/outward zero–pole family structure of

[
11
, Assumptions 5.7–5.8 and Lemma 5.9]
. Then the family order
there gives, inside each separating one-point contour
C_{\chi}
, equality of
the numerator and denominator counts separately in the two exponent classes
appearing in the Jack logarithmic derivative. In the notation of
Proposition
5.8
,

d\log S_{\chi,K}=\alpha\,d\log\frac{A_{K}}{B_{K}}+d\log\frac{C_{K}}{D_{K}},

and the separated family order gives

N_{A}(C_{\chi})=N_{B}(C_{\chi}),\qquad N_{C}(C_{\chi})=N_{D}(C_{\chi}).

The proof of Proposition
5.8
shows that the one-point
branch condition (
5.19
) in
Assumption
5.7
(ii)
follows.

For a finite collection of marked
L
-type columns, the same separated
zero–pole setup in
[
11
, Assumptions 5.7–5.8 and Lemma 5.9]
allows the one-point contours, including repeated marked
locations, to be chosen as pairwise disjoint nested parallel copies inside (
5.20
)
Proposition
5.9
then excludes all
two-variable singularity loci of the
LL
-covariance kernel on the product
contours. Thus the multi-point contour condition in
Assumption
5.7
(iii)
also follows.

The point of keeping Assumption
5.7
in its
analytic form is that the asymptotic formulas apply whenever such contours and
branches exist, not only in this particular separated family regime. The
separated zero–pole assumptions above are only a concrete sufficient criterion.

Proposition 5.11

(One-point Neguţ asymptotics)
.

Fix boundary types
c_{l},c_{r}\in\{el,oa,deel,eoa\}
and parameters

u,v\in(0,1)
. Suppose Assumption
2.5
holds. Let

i^{(\epsilon)}
be a marked column satisfying (
2.17
), and assume that

a_{i^{(\epsilon)}}=L

for all sufficiently small
\epsilon
. Write

\chi=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon i^{(\epsilon)}\in(V_{0},V_{m})\setminus\{V_{1},\ldots,V_{m-1}\}.

Let

\Pr^{(\epsilon)}=\Pr^{(\epsilon)}_{c_{l},c_{r},u,v}

be the Macdonald-deformed probability measure (
2.14
). Assume that the
one-point contour and branch admissibility hypotheses of
Assumption
5.7
hold at
\chi
.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{\chi}
be an admissible positively oriented contour enclosing

0
and separating the one-point families

\mathcal{D}_{\chi}\subset\operatorname{Int}(\mathcal{C}_{\chi}),\qquad\mathcal{E}_{\chi}\cap\overline{\operatorname{Int}(\mathcal{C}_{\chi})}=\varnothing,

where
\mathcal{D}_{\chi}
is the required pole set defined in
(
5.17
), and
\mathcal{E}_{\chi}
is the excluded point
family defined in (
5.18
).

Set

S_{\chi}(w):=\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w),

where
\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}
is defined in (
5.13
). Let

T_{\chi}(w):=\exp\!\left\{\beta\,\mathrm{Log}_{\chi,\mathcal{C}_{\chi}}S_{\chi}(w)\right\}

be the admissible spectral branch supplied by
Assumption
5.7
. Then, for every

g\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,

(5.21)

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{\Pr^{(\epsilon)}}\bigl[\gamma_{g}(\lambda^{(M,i^{(\epsilon)})};q,t)\bigr]=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\mathcal{C}_{\chi}}T_{\chi}(w)^{g}\frac{dw}{w}.

Proof.

Apply Lemma
5.3
with
m=1
and
g_{1}=g
. Let

W=(w_{1},\dots,w_{g})
and let
\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{g}
be nested contours satisfying
the contour conditions in Lemma
4.8
; choose them once and for all inside the region
bounded by
\mathcal{C}
.

By the description of the limiting pole sets above, the poles of the prelimit integrand
converge to the limiting pole sets. Hence for all sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
, the same
family of contours remains admissible for the prelimit integrand. Let

\Theta_{k}^{(\epsilon)}(W)
denote the
k
-th correction factor from the one-point
specialization of Lemma
5.3
. By Lemma
5.5
,

G_{1,L}^{(\epsilon)}(W)\,G_{0,L}^{(\epsilon)}(W)\,G_{1,R}^{(\epsilon)}(W)\,G_{0,R}^{(\epsilon)}(W)\longrightarrow\prod_{s=1}^{g}\bigl[\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w_{s})\bigr]^{\beta}

uniformly on
\mathcal{C}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}_{g}
. By Lemma
5.6
,

\prod_{k\geq 1}\Theta_{k}^{(\epsilon)}(W)\longrightarrow\prod_{s=1}^{g}\prod_{k\geq 1}\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w_{s})\bigr]^{\beta}

uniformly on the same product contour. Therefore dominated convergence applies to the

g
-fold contour integral.

Finally, Lemma
A.10
collapses the limiting
g
-fold contour integral to a single contour
integral and yields (
5.21
).
∎

Remark 5.12

(Boundary degenerations)
.

In the empty-boundary degeneration
u=v=0
, the reflected factors in
(
5.21
) disappear and the formula reduces to the one-point asymptotic
formula for pure rail-yard dimer coverings in
[
10
, Proposition 5.7]
.
If
v=0
and
u\in(0,1)
, the right boundary becomes empty while the left
boundary remains free. This is the one-free-boundary, or half-space,
degeneration of the doubly free-boundary formula; under the exact Jack scaling

q=t^{\alpha}
,
t=e^{-n\beta\epsilon}
, it gives the Jack specialization of
the half-space Macdonald-process framework of
Barraquand–Borodin–Corwin
[
3
]
.

5.6.
Gaussian fluctuations and the annular kernel

For later use define, for
\chi\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
g\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
,

(5.22)

\Phi_{\chi,g}(z):=\bigl[\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(z)\bigr]^{g\beta}\prod_{k\geq 1}\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(z)\bigr]^{g\beta}.

We shall also use the following free-boundary covariance kernel. For
r\geq 1
, set

(5.23)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{r}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k\geq 1}\left(u^{4k-4}v^{4k-2}\right)^{r}+\sum_{k\geq 1}\left(u^{4k-2}v^{4k}\right)^{r}=\frac{v^{2r}+u^{2r}v^{4r}}{1-u^{4r}v^{4r}},

(5.24)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{b}_{r}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k\geq 1}\left(u^{4k-2}v^{4k-4}\right)^{r}+\sum_{k\geq 1}\left(u^{4k}v^{4k-2}\right)^{r}=\frac{u^{2r}+u^{4r}v^{2r}}{1-u^{4r}v^{4r}},

(5.25)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}_{r}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k\geq 1}\left(u^{2k}v^{2k}\right)^{r}=\frac{u^{2r}v^{2r}}{1-u^{2r}v^{2r}}.

For
z,w
on the contours used below, define

(5.26)

\mathscr{B}_{u,v}(z,w):=\sum_{r\geq 1}r\left[\mathfrak{a}_{r}z^{-r}w^{-r}+\mathfrak{b}_{r}z^{r}w^{r}+\mathfrak{c}_{r}\left(z^{-r}w^{r}+z^{r}w^{-r}\right)\right].

The contours are contained in a fixed compact annulus. We assume
u,v
are sufficiently
small so that the series in (
5.26
) is absolutely and uniformly convergent on every
product of contours appearing below.

Definition 5.13

(Annular free-boundary image covariance for Laplace tests)
.

Let
u,v\in(0,1)
and set

\mathfrak{q}:=u^{2}v^{2}.

We use the
q
-Pochhammer notation of (
A.6
), with
q
replaced by

\mathfrak{q}
. On contour neighborhoods where the products below converge
normally and are non-vanishing, define the annular image potential

(5.27)

\mathscr{L}_{u,v}(z,w):=\log\frac{(w/z;\mathfrak{q})_{\infty}(\mathfrak{q}z/w;\mathfrak{q})_{\infty}}{(u^{2}zw;\mathfrak{q})_{\infty}(v^{2}/(zw);\mathfrak{q})_{\infty}},

with the logarithmic branch prescribed by the contour system. We use the
term
annular free-boundary image covariance
for the Laplace-test
Gaussian covariance whose potential on the tested contour algebra is

\mathscr{L}_{u,v}
.

Proposition 5.14

(Annular free-boundary image kernel)
.

Set
\mathfrak{q}:=u^{2}v^{2}
and

\mathscr{A}_{\mathfrak{q}}(x):=\sum_{r\geq 1}\frac{rx^{r}}{1-\mathfrak{q}^{r}}=(x\partial_{x})^{2}\!\left[-\log(x;\mathfrak{q})_{\infty}\right].

On every product of contours on which the following series converge normally, define

(5.28)

\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{u,v}(z,w):=\mathscr{A}_{\mathfrak{q}}\!\left(\frac{w}{z}\right)+\mathscr{A}_{\mathfrak{q}}\!\left(\mathfrak{q}\frac{z}{w}\right)+\mathscr{A}_{\mathfrak{q}}(u^{2}zw)+\mathscr{A}_{\mathfrak{q}}\!\left(\frac{v^{2}}{zw}\right).

Then
\mathscr{B}_{u,v}(z,w)
as defined in (
5.26
), satisfies

(5.29)

\mathscr{B}_{u,v}(z,w)=\mathscr{A}_{\mathfrak{q}}\!\left(\frac{v^{2}}{zw}\right)+\mathscr{A}_{\mathfrak{q}}(u^{2}zw)+\mathscr{A}_{\mathfrak{q}}\!\left(\mathfrak{q}\frac{w}{z}\right)+\mathscr{A}_{\mathfrak{q}}\!\left(\mathfrak{q}\frac{z}{w}\right).

Moreover, on nested contours with
|w|<|z|
,

(5.30)

\frac{zw}{(z-w)^{2}}+\mathscr{B}_{u,v}(z,w)=\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{u,v}(z,w).

Equivalently,

(5.31)

(z\partial_{z})(w\partial_{w})\mathscr{L}_{u,v}(z,w)=\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{u,v}(z,w),

where
\mathscr{L}_{u,v}
is the potential in
(
5.27
).

Thus the full bracketed covariance kernel in Theorems
5.15
and

2.9
is the logarithmic-derivative image kernel associated with the
annular free-boundary potential in Definition
5.13
.

Proof.

The coefficient identities

\mathfrak{a}_{r}=\frac{v^{2r}}{1-\mathfrak{q}^{r}},\qquad\mathfrak{b}_{r}=\frac{u^{2r}}{1-\mathfrak{q}^{r}},\qquad\mathfrak{c}_{r}=\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{r}}{1-\mathfrak{q}^{r}}

follow from (
5.23
)–(
5.25
). Substituting these identities
into (
5.26
) gives (
5.29
). If
|w|<|z|
, then

\frac{zw}{(z-w)^{2}}=\sum_{r\geq 1}r\left(\frac{w}{z}\right)^{r}.

Adding this to the

\mathscr{A}_{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathfrak{q}w/z)
-term in
(
5.29
) changes the coefficient of
(w/z)^{r}
to

r/(1-\mathfrak{q}^{r})
. This gives the

\mathscr{A}_{\mathfrak{q}}(w/z)
-term in (
5.28
), while
the other three terms are already the remaining image terms. Hence
(
5.30
) follows.

It remains to prove (
5.31
). If

x=cz^{a}w^{b}
, then

(z\partial_{z})(w\partial_{w})\log(x;\mathfrak{q})_{\infty}=-ab\,\mathscr{A}_{\mathfrak{q}}(x),

and the sign is reversed for
-\log(x;\mathfrak{q})_{\infty}
. Applying this
identity to the four factors

x=\frac{w}{z},\qquad x=\mathfrak{q}\frac{z}{w},\qquad x=u^{2}zw,\qquad x=\frac{v^{2}}{zw}

in (
5.27
) gives (
5.31
).
Normal convergence justifies the termwise differentiations and summations.
∎

Theorem 5.15
.

Fix boundary types
c_{l},c_{r}\in\{el,oa,deel,eoa\}
and parameters

u,v\in(0,1)
. Suppose Assumption
2.5
holds. Let

s\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, and let

i_{1}^{(\epsilon)},\ldots,i_{s}^{(\epsilon)}\in[l^{(\epsilon)}..r^{(\epsilon)}]

satisfy

\epsilon i_{d}^{(\epsilon)}\to\chi_{d},\qquad\chi_{1}\leq\chi_{2}\leq\cdots\leq\chi_{s},\qquad d\in[s],

with each residue class
(i_{d}^{(\epsilon)})_{\equiv n}
independent of

\epsilon
. Assume that all marked columns are of
L
-type:

(5.32)

a_{i_{1}^{(\epsilon)}}=a_{i_{2}^{(\epsilon)}}=\cdots=a_{i_{s}^{(\epsilon)}}=L

for all sufficiently small
\epsilon
.

Assume further that the multi-point contour and branch admissibility
hypotheses of Assumption
5.7
hold for the
finite family of marked columns

\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{s}.

Let

\Pr^{(\epsilon)}=\Pr^{(\epsilon)}_{c_{l},c_{r},u,v}

be the Macdonald-deformed probability measure (
2.14
). For

g_{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, define

Q_{g_{d}}^{(\epsilon)}(\epsilon i_{d}^{(\epsilon)}):=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\left(\gamma_{g_{d}}(\lambda^{(M,i_{d}^{(\epsilon)})};q,t)-\mathbb{E}_{\Pr^{(\epsilon)}}\bigl[\gamma_{g_{d}}(\lambda^{(M,i_{d}^{(\epsilon)})};q,t)\bigr]\right),\qquad d\in[s].

For each
d
, set

S_{\chi_{d}}(z):=\mathcal{G}_{\chi_{d}}(z)\prod_{r\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,r}(z),

and let

T_{\chi_{d}}(z):=\exp\!\left\{\beta\,\mathrm{Log}_{\chi_{d},\mathcal{C}_{d}}S_{\chi_{d}}(z)\right\}

be the admissible spectral branch supplied by
Assumption
5.7
. We write

\Phi_{\chi_{d},g_{d}}(z):=T_{\chi_{d}}(z)^{g_{d}}.

Then, as
\epsilon\to 0
,

\bigl(Q_{g_{1}}^{(\epsilon)}(\epsilon i_{1}^{(\epsilon)}),\ldots,Q_{g_{s}}^{(\epsilon)}(\epsilon i_{s}^{(\epsilon)})\bigr)

converges in distribution to a centered Gaussian vector

\bigl(Q_{g_{1}}(\chi_{1}),\ldots,Q_{g_{s}}(\chi_{s})\bigr).

Its covariances are

\displaystyle\operatorname{Cov}\bigl[Q_{g_{d}}(\chi_{d}),Q_{g_{h}}(\chi_{h})\bigr]

(5.33)

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{n^{2}\alpha\beta^{2}g_{d}g_{h}}{(2\pi\mathbf{i})^{2}}\oint_{\mathcal{C}_{d}}\oint_{\mathcal{C}_{h}}\Phi_{\chi_{d},g_{d}}(z)\,\Phi_{\chi_{h},g_{h}}(w)\left[\frac{zw}{(z-w)^{2}}+\mathscr{B}_{u,v}(z,w)\right]\frac{dz}{z}\frac{dw}{w}.

Here
\mathcal{C}_{d}
and
\mathcal{C}_{h}
are admissible pairwise contours
from Assumption
5.7
: each

\mathcal{C}_{d}
is positively oriented, encloses
0
and the required pole
set
\mathcal{D}_{\chi_{d}}
, and separates it from the excluded point family

\mathcal{E}_{\chi_{d}}
. The pair of contours is chosen disjoint and
pairwise admissible for the kernel in (
5.33
). If

\chi_{d}=\chi_{h}
, one may take two disjoint nested copies of the same
one-point contour; the value of the integral is independent of this choice
under admissible contour deformations.

Lemma 5.16

(Two-point free-boundary correction)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
5.15
, let

Z=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{g_{d}}),\qquad W=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{g_{h}})

lie on the product contours used in the two-point formula. Set

\rho_{2}^{Z}:=\rho_{2,L}(Z;q,t),\qquad\rho_{3}^{Z}:=\rho_{3,L}(Z;q,t),

and define
\rho_{2}^{W},\rho_{3}^{W}
analogously.

Let

\Theta_{k}^{Z,W}:=\Theta_{k,(i_{d},i_{h})}(Z,W),\qquad\Theta_{k}^{Z}:=\Theta_{k,(i_{d})}(Z),\qquad\Theta_{k}^{W}:=\Theta_{k,(i_{h})}(W)

denote the
k
-th reflected factors from Lemma
5.3
in the
two-point and one-point specializations. Define

(5.34)

\Xi_{k}^{(\epsilon)}(Z,W):=\frac{\Theta_{k}^{Z,W}}{\Theta_{k}^{Z}\Theta_{k}^{W}},\qquad\Xi^{(\epsilon)}(Z,W):=\prod_{k\geq 1}\Xi_{k}^{(\epsilon)}(Z,W).

Equivalently, by multiplicativity of
H
, the graph–marked Cauchy factors
cancel in this quotient, and

\displaystyle\Xi_{k}^{(\epsilon)}(Z,W)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Theta_{c_{l}}\!\left([u^{2k-2}v^{2k-1}](\rho_{2}^{Z}\cup\rho_{2}^{W});q,t\right)}{\Theta_{c_{l}}\!\left([u^{2k-2}v^{2k-1}]\rho_{2}^{Z};q,t\right)\Theta_{c_{l}}\!\left([u^{2k-2}v^{2k-1}]\rho_{2}^{W};q,t\right)}

\displaystyle\quad\times\frac{\Theta_{c_{r}}\!\left([u^{2k-1}v^{2k}](\rho_{2}^{Z}\cup\rho_{2}^{W});q,t\right)}{\Theta_{c_{r}}\!\left([u^{2k-1}v^{2k}]\rho_{2}^{Z};q,t\right)\Theta_{c_{r}}\!\left([u^{2k-1}v^{2k}]\rho_{2}^{W};q,t\right)}

\displaystyle\quad\times\frac{\Theta_{c_{l}}\!\left([u^{2k-1}v^{2k-2}](\rho_{3}^{Z}\cup\rho_{3}^{W});q,t\right)}{\Theta_{c_{l}}\!\left([u^{2k-1}v^{2k-2}]\rho_{3}^{Z};q,t\right)\Theta_{c_{l}}\!\left([u^{2k-1}v^{2k-2}]\rho_{3}^{W};q,t\right)}

\displaystyle\quad\times\frac{\Theta_{c_{r}}\!\left([u^{2k}v^{2k-1}](\rho_{3}^{Z}\cup\rho_{3}^{W});q,t\right)}{\Theta_{c_{r}}\!\left([u^{2k}v^{2k-1}]\rho_{3}^{Z};q,t\right)\Theta_{c_{r}}\!\left([u^{2k}v^{2k-1}]\rho_{3}^{W};q,t\right)}

(5.35)

\displaystyle\quad\times\frac{H\!\left(u^{2k}(\rho_{2}^{Z}\cup\rho_{2}^{W});v^{2k}(\rho_{3}^{Z}\cup\rho_{3}^{W});q,t\right)}{H\!\left(u^{2k}\rho_{2}^{Z};v^{2k}\rho_{3}^{Z};q,t\right)H\!\left(u^{2k}\rho_{2}^{W};v^{2k}\rho_{3}^{W};q,t\right)}.

Then, uniformly on the product contours,

(5.36)

\frac{\Xi^{(\epsilon)}(Z,W)-1}{\epsilon^{2}}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{K}_{u,v}(Z,W),

where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}_{u,v}(Z,W):=\alpha n^{2}\beta^{2}\sum_{r\geq 1}r\Big[

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{r}\,p_{r}(Z^{-1})p_{r}(W^{-1})+\mathfrak{b}_{r}\,p_{r}(Z)p_{r}(W)

(5.37)

\displaystyle+\mathfrak{c}_{r}\bigl(p_{r}(Z^{-1})p_{r}(W)+p_{r}(Z)p_{r}(W^{-1})\bigr)\Big].

Here

p_{r}(Z)=\sum_{a=1}^{g_{d}}z_{a}^{r},\qquad p_{r}(Z^{-1})=\sum_{a=1}^{g_{d}}z_{a}^{-r},

and similarly for
W
.

Proof.

The definition (
5.34
) takes the
k
-th reflected
factor in the two-point formula of Lemma
5.3
and divides it by
the two corresponding one-point reflected factors. We first verify the
explicit expression (
5.35
).

In the two-point formula the active marked specializations are

\rho_{2}^{Z}\cup\rho_{2}^{W},\qquad\rho_{3}^{Z}\cup\rho_{3}^{W}.

The boundary
\Theta
-terms give the four
\Theta
-quotients in
(
5.35
). The marked–marked Cauchy term gives the last quotient
in (
5.35
). The remaining graph–marked Cauchy terms cancel.
For example,

\frac{H\!\left(u^{2k}\rho_{B};v^{2k}(\rho_{3}^{Z}\cup\rho_{3}^{W});q,t\right)}{H\!\left(u^{2k}\rho_{B};v^{2k}\rho_{3}^{Z};q,t\right)H\!\left(u^{2k}\rho_{B};v^{2k}\rho_{3}^{W};q,t\right)}=1,

by multiplicativity of
H
in the second specialization. The other three
graph–marked Cauchy factors cancel in the same way. This proves
(
5.35
).

We now compute the second-order limit. From (
4.8
)–(
4.9
), for
every
r\geq 1
,

p_{r}(\rho_{2}^{Z})=\left(\frac{q}{t}\right)^{r}(q^{r}-1)p_{r}(Z^{-1}),\qquad p_{r}(\rho_{3}^{Z})=(1-q^{-r})p_{r}(Z),

and the same formula holds with
Z
replaced by
W
. Since

q=t^{\alpha}
and
t=e^{-n\beta\epsilon}
,

(5.38)

\frac{1-t^{r}}{1-q^{r}}\to\frac{1}{\alpha},\qquad\frac{p_{r}(\rho_{2}^{Z})}{\epsilon}\to-\alpha n\beta r\,p_{r}(Z^{-1}),\qquad\frac{p_{r}(\rho_{3}^{Z})}{\epsilon}\to-\alpha n\beta r\,p_{r}(Z),

uniformly on the product contours, and similarly for
W
.

We now take logarithms in (
5.35
). By the exponential formula for
the boundary factors in Lemma
3.11
, for each boundary type
c
there
are coefficients
A_{c,r}(q,t)
and
B_{c,r}(q,t)
such that, for every
specialization
\eta
,

\log\Theta_{c}(\eta;q,t)=\sum_{r\geq 1}\frac{A_{c,r}(q,t)}{r}p_{r}(\eta)+\sum_{r\geq 1}\frac{B_{c,r}(q,t)}{r}p_{r}(\eta)^{2}.

Thus, for any scale
\sigma
and any two specializations

\eta_{Z},\eta_{W}
,

\displaystyle\log\frac{\Theta_{c}(\sigma(\eta_{Z}\cup\eta_{W});q,t)}{\Theta_{c}(\sigma\eta_{Z};q,t)\Theta_{c}(\sigma\eta_{W};q,t)}

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{r\geq 1}\frac{B_{c,r}(q,t)}{r}\,\sigma^{2r}\left[(p_{r}(\eta_{Z})+p_{r}(\eta_{W}))^{2}-p_{r}(\eta_{Z})^{2}-p_{r}(\eta_{W})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=2\sum_{r\geq 1}\frac{B_{c,r}(q,t)}{r}\,\sigma^{2r}p_{r}(\eta_{Z})p_{r}(\eta_{W}).

In particular, all linear terms cancel in the boundary quotients, and only

Z
-
W
cross terms remain.

The marked–marked Cauchy quotient is handled similarly. By multiplicativity
of
H
and by (
3.26
),
