. Indeed, away from its poles,

S_{\chi,K}
is real-valued on the real axis, while at a pole one
has

Q_{K}(w)=0,\qquad P_{K}(w)\neq 0,

because
P_{K}/Q_{K}
is reduced. Consequently, as
\zeta
varies
in the connected domain
\mathbb{C}_{+}
, the number of roots of

F_{K,\zeta}
in
\mathbb{C}_{+}
, counted with multiplicity, is
constant: this number can change only if a root reaches the real
axis or escapes to infinity, neither of which is possible.

For
\zeta=\mathbf{i}R
with
R
sufficiently large, the pole
expansions above and the degree count show that exactly the
N_{K}

roots issuing from

\mathcal{D}_{\chi,K}=\{p_{1,K},\ldots,p_{N_{K},K}\}

lie in
\mathbb{C}_{+}
. Hence, for every
\zeta\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
,
the equation

S_{\chi,K}(w)=\zeta

has exactly
N_{K}
upper-half-plane roots, counted with
multiplicity.

Suppose now that
\zeta\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
is such that the equation

S_{\chi,K}(w)=\zeta
has no multiple root. Continue the
pole-labelled branches

w_{\xi,\chi,K}(\zeta),\qquad\xi\in\mathcal{D}_{\chi,K},

from
\zeta=\mathbf{i}R
,
R\gg 1
, along a path in
\mathbb{C}_{+}
which avoids the finitely many
values of
\zeta
for which

S_{\chi,K}(w)=\zeta

has a multiple root. Every such branch starts in

\mathbb{C}_{+}
and cannot cross the real axis while the spectral
parameter remains in
\mathbb{C}_{+}
. Since there are exactly

N_{K}
upper-half-plane roots, we obtain

(6.50)

\left\{w_{\xi,\chi,K}(\zeta):\xi\in\mathcal{D}_{\chi,K}\right\}=\left\{w\in\mathbb{C}_{+}:S_{\chi,K}(w)=\zeta\right\}

as unordered multisets.

We next take the upper boundary value

\zeta=x+\mathbf{i}\delta,\qquad\delta\downarrow 0.

Since
C_{\chi,K}\neq x
, the polynomial

F_{K,x}(w)=P_{K}(w)-xQ_{K}(w)

has degree
M_{K}
.

At two consecutive inward poles, the negative residue signs give

\lim_{w\to p_{j,K}^{+}}\bigl(S_{\chi,K}(w)-x\bigr)=-\infty,\qquad\lim_{w\to p_{j+1,K}^{-}}\bigl(S_{\chi,K}(w)-x\bigr)=+\infty.

Therefore, for every
j=1,\ldots,N_{K}-1
, the equation

S_{\chi,K}(w)=x

has at least one root in

(p_{j,K},p_{j+1,K}).

Similarly, the positive residue signs at the outward poles give

\lim_{w\to q_{l,K}^{+}}\bigl(S_{\chi,K}(w)-x\bigr)=+\infty,\qquad\lim_{w\to q_{l+1,K}^{-}}\bigl(S_{\chi,K}(w)-x\bigr)=-\infty.

Hence there is at least one root in every interval

(q_{l,K},q_{l+1,K}),\qquad l=1,\ldots,M_{K}-N_{K}-1.

The number of these pairwise disjoint intervals is

(N_{K}-1)+(M_{K}-N_{K}-1)=M_{K}-2.

By hypothesis, the equation
S_{\chi,K}(w)=x
also has one
nonreal conjugate pair of total multiplicity two. Since

F_{K,x}
has degree
M_{K}
, these
M_{K}-2
real roots and the
two nonreal roots exhaust all roots, counted with multiplicity.
It follows that every interval above contains exactly one root and
that each such root is simple.

Write

u_{j,K}\in(p_{j,K},p_{j+1,K}),\qquad j=1,\ldots,N_{K}-1,

for the inward roots, and

v_{l,K}\in(q_{l,K},q_{l+1,K}),\qquad l=1,\ldots,M_{K}-N_{K}-1,

for the outward roots. The endpoint signs and uniqueness imply

S_{\chi,K}^{\prime}(u_{j,K})>0,\qquad S_{\chi,K}^{\prime}(v_{l,K})<0.

All roots of
F_{K,x}
are simple. Therefore, for all sufficiently
small
\delta>0
, the equation

S_{\chi,K}(w)=x+\mathbf{i}\delta

also has only simple roots, and
(
6.50
) applies.

For a simple real root
r
of
S_{\chi,K}(w)=x
, the implicit
function theorem gives a local solution of

S_{\chi,K}(w)=x+\mathbf{i}\delta

of the form

w_{r}(\delta)=r+\frac{\mathbf{i}\delta}{S_{\chi,K}^{\prime}(r)}+O(\delta^{2}).

Consequently, for all sufficiently small
\delta>0
,

w_{u_{j,K}}(\delta)\in\mathbb{C}_{+},\qquad w_{v_{l,K}}(\delta)\in\mathbb{C}_{-}.

The simple nonreal root
w_{+,K}
also has a unique nearby
continuation which remains in
\mathbb{C}_{+}
, while its conjugate
remains in
\mathbb{C}_{-}
.

Thus, for sufficiently small
\delta>0
, the complete multiset

W_{K}(\delta)
consists of one root converging to
w_{+,K}
and
the
N_{K}-1
roots converging respectively to

u_{1,K},\ldots,u_{N_{K}-1,K}.

Therefore

W_{K}(\delta)\longrightarrow\{w_{+,K},u_{1,K},\ldots,u_{N_{K}-1,K}\}

as multisets.

Finally, by
(
6.50
), for all sufficiently
small
\delta>0
,

\left\{w_{\xi,\chi,K}(x+\mathbf{i}\delta):\xi\in\mathcal{D}_{\chi,K}\right\}=W_{K}(\delta)

as unordered multisets. All these roots lie in
\mathbb{C}_{+}
.
Hence their logarithms, obtained by continuation from large
imaginary
\zeta
, agree with the principal logarithm on

\mathbb{C}_{+}
. Since the poles
p_{j,K}
and roots

u_{j,K}
are positive,

\arg p_{j,K}=0,\qquad\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\arg w_{u_{j,K}}(\delta)=0.

The remaining upper-half-plane root converges to
w_{+,K}
, with

\arg w_{+,K}\in(0,\pi).

Possible permutations of the pole labels do not affect the sum.
Therefore

\operatorname{Im}\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal{D}_{\chi,K}}\left(\log w_{\xi,\chi,K}(x+\mathbf{i}0)-\log\xi\right)=\arg w_{+,K}.

∎

Proof of Theorem
2.8
.

Fix a regular slope point satisfying the stated conditions and put

x=e^{-n\kappa}.

By Lemma
6.8
,
Assumptions
6.6
–
6.7
imply
Assumption
6.5
. In addition,
if

0<\xi_{1}<\cdots<\xi_{M_{K}}

are the positive poles of
S_{\chi,K}
, then

\mathcal{D}_{\chi,K}=\{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{r_{0}}\}.

By condition (iii) and
Lemma
6.10
, applied with

(\chi_{0},x_{0})=(\chi,x),

there exist a
pole-free open set
U\subset\mathbb{C}^{*}
and a compact set

\Omega\Subset U

such that

S_{\chi,K}\longrightarrow S_{\chi}

locally uniformly on
U
, and all nonreal roots of the finite and
limiting equations in a neighborhood of
(\chi,x)
lie in

\Omega
.

By Lemma
6.11
, each finite equation

S_{\chi,K}(w)=x

has at most one nonreal conjugate pair. If the limiting equation had
at least two upper-half-plane roots, counted with multiplicity,
Rouché’s theorem on disjoint disks around these roots would imply
that the finite equations have at least two upper-half-plane roots
for all sufficiently large
K
, a contradiction. Thus the
limiting equation has at most one nonreal conjugate pair.

Suppose first that
(\chi,\kappa)
is liquid. If along an infinite
subsequence the finite equation had only real roots, every argument
in (
6.48
) would belong to
\pi\mathbb{Z}
. Since the
argument sums converge, their limit would also belong to

\pi\mathbb{Z}
, and (
6.48
), together with

0\leq\partial_{\kappa}\mathcal{H}\leq\frac{2}{\alpha},

would force

\partial_{\kappa}\mathcal{H}\in\left\{0,\frac{2}{\alpha}\right\},

contradicting liquidness.

Therefore, for all sufficiently large
K
, the finite equation has
a unique nonreal conjugate pair

w_{+,K},\overline{w_{+,K}},\qquad\operatorname{Im}w_{+,K}>0.

The endpoint cases
r_{0}=0
and
r_{0}=M_{K}
in
Assumption
6.5
are then impossible, because in those cases
the finite equation has no nonreal root.

Lemma
6.12
gives

S_{\chi,K}(0)>x,

and Lemma
6.14
gives

\operatorname{Im}\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal{D}_{\chi,K}}\left(\log w_{\xi,\chi,K}(x+\mathbf{i}0)-\log\xi\right)=\arg w_{+,K}.

Since

S_{\chi,K}(0)\longrightarrow S_{\chi}(0)

and
S_{\chi}(0)\neq x
, it follows that

S_{\chi}(0)>x.

Consequently (
6.48
) becomes

\partial_{\kappa}\mathcal{H}(\chi,\kappa)=\frac{2}{\alpha}-\lim_{K\to\infty}\frac{2\arg w_{+,K}}{\pi\alpha}.

Because the point is liquid, the limiting argument belongs to

(0,\pi)
.

By Lemma
6.10
, there is a fixed compact
set

\Omega\Subset U,

independent of
K
, such that

w_{+,K}\in\Omega

for all sufficiently large
K
. Hence every subsequence of

\{w_{+,K}\}
has a further subsequence converging to some

w_{*}\in\Omega.

Since

\arg w_{+,K}\longrightarrow\theta\qquad\text{for some }\theta\in(0,\pi),

and since
\Omega\Subset\mathbb{C}^{*}
, every such limit satisfies

w_{*}\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

Along the convergent further subsequence, the local uniform
convergence

S_{\chi,K}\longrightarrow S_{\chi}\qquad\text{on }U

and the identities

S_{\chi,K}(w_{+,K})=x

give

S_{\chi}(w_{*})=x.

Thus every subsequential limit is an upper-half-plane root of the
limiting equation. Since that equation has at most one
upper-half-plane root, every subsequential limit is equal to the
same point
w_{+}
. Consequently,

w_{+,K}\longrightarrow w_{+}.

This proves

\partial_{\kappa}\mathcal{H}(\chi,\kappa)=\frac{2}{\alpha}-\frac{2\arg w_{+}}{\pi\alpha},\qquad\arg w_{+}\in(0,\pi).

Conversely, suppose that

S_{\chi}(w)=x

has a nonreal conjugate pair

w_{+},\overline{w_{+}},\qquad\operatorname{Im}w_{+}>0.

Since

w_{+},\overline{w_{+}}\in\Omega\Subset U,

choose disjoint open disks
D_{+}
and
D_{-}
, centered at

w_{+}
and
\overline{w_{+}}
, respectively, such that

\overline{D_{+}}\subset U\cap\mathbb{C}_{+},\qquad\overline{D_{-}}\subset U\cap\mathbb{C}_{-}.

Rouché’s theorem implies that, for all
sufficiently large
K
, the finite equation has a nonreal conjugate
pair

w_{+,K},\overline{w_{+,K}},\qquad w_{+,K}\longrightarrow w_{+}.

By Lemma
6.11
, this pair is unique.
Lemmas
6.12
and

6.14
therefore give

S_{\chi,K}(0)>x

and

\operatorname{Im}\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal{D}_{\chi,K}}\left(\log w_{\xi,\chi,K}(x+\mathbf{i}0)-\log\xi\right)=\arg w_{+,K}.

Passing to the limit in (
6.48
) yields

\partial_{\kappa}\mathcal{H}(\chi,\kappa)=\frac{2}{\alpha}-\frac{2\arg w_{+}}{\pi\alpha}.

Since
0<\arg w_{+}<\pi
, this slope belongs to

\left(0,\frac{2}{\alpha}\right).

Hence
(\chi,\kappa)
is liquid.

We have proved the liquid equivalence. At a regular slope point the
slope lies in
[0,2/\alpha]
. Therefore a point is frozen exactly
when it is not liquid, which, by the preceding equivalence and the
upper bound of one nonreal pair, is equivalent to the all-real-root
condition.

Finally, fix a point

(\chi_{0},\kappa_{0})

in the above nonexceptional region, set

x_{0}:=e^{-n\kappa_{0}},\qquad F_{0}(w):=S_{\chi_{0}}(w)-x_{0},

and suppose that
F_{0}
has no multiple real zero.

Apply Lemma
6.10
at

(\chi_{0},x_{0})
. Then there exist open intervals

I\ni\chi_{0},\qquad X\ni x_{0},

a pole-free open set

U\subset\mathbb{C}^{*},

and a compact set

\Omega\Subset U

such that, whenever

\chi\in I,\qquad e^{-n\kappa}\in X,

every nonreal zero of

F_{\chi,\kappa}(w):=S_{\chi}(w)-e^{-n\kappa}

lies in
\Omega
.

Since

\chi_{0}\in(V_{p-1},V_{p})

for some
p\in[m]
, shrink
I
, if necessary, so that

\overline{I}\Subset(V_{p-1},V_{p}).

Then, for every
\chi\in I
, the same factors occur in the
finite products defining
\mathcal{G}_{\chi}
; only their

\chi
-dependent zero and pole locations vary continuously with

\chi
. After
shrinking
I
once more, the same numerator–denominator factors
are cancelled in the reduced functions for every
\chi\in I
.
Each remaining unreflected zero or pole is therefore either
independent of
\chi
or varies continuously with
\chi
. Since the reflected
factors are independent of
\chi
and their product converges
normally on compact subsets of
U
, it follows that

S_{\chi}\longrightarrow S_{\chi_{0}}

locally uniformly on
U
as
\chi\to\chi_{0}
.
Consequently,

S_{\chi}(w)-e^{-n\kappa}\longrightarrow S_{\chi_{0}}(w)-e^{-n\kappa_{0}}

locally uniformly on
U
as

(\chi,\kappa)\longrightarrow(\chi_{0},\kappa_{0}).

Together with the normal convergence of the reflected product on
compact subsets of
U
, this gives

F_{\chi,\kappa}\longrightarrow F_{0}

locally uniformly on
U
as

(\chi,\kappa)\longrightarrow(\chi_{0},\kappa_{0}).

Choose an open set
V
such that

\Omega\subset V\Subset U

and

F_{0}(w)\neq 0,\qquad w\in\partial V.

The function
F_{0}
has only finitely many zeros in

\overline{V}
. Around each of its distinct nonreal zeros choose
a small open disk whose closure is contained in
V
, lies entirely
in the corresponding half-plane, and contains no other zero of

F_{0}
. Around each real zero choose a small open disk centered on
the real axis, symmetric under complex conjugation, whose closure is
contained in
V
and which contains no other zero of
F_{0}
.
Choose all these disks with pairwise disjoint closures.

By hypothesis, every real zero of
F_{0}
is simple. Hence each
real-centered disk contains exactly one zero of
F_{0}
, counted with
multiplicity. Let
D_{1},\ldots,D_{N}
be the chosen open disks and set

K:=\overline{V}\setminus\bigcup_{j=1}^{N}D_{j}.

By construction,
F_{0}
has no zero on
K
. Since
K
is
compact,

m:=\min_{w\in K}|F_{0}(w)|>0.

After shrinking the parameter neighborhood, the locally uniform
convergence gives

\sup_{w\in K}|F_{\chi,\kappa}(w)-F_{0}(w)|<\frac{m}{2}.

Hence

|F_{\chi,\kappa}(w)|\geq\frac{m}{2},\qquad w\in K,

so
F_{\chi,\kappa}
has no zero outside the chosen disks in

\overline{V}
. The locally uniform convergence above therefore implies
that, for all
(\chi,\kappa)
sufficiently close to

(\chi_{0},\kappa_{0})
,

(1)

F_{\chi,\kappa}
has no zero on that compact complement;

(2)

by Rouché’s theorem, every chosen disk contains the same
number of zeros of
F_{\chi,\kappa}
, counted with multiplicity,
as it contains zeros of
F_{0}
.

A disk centered at a nonreal zero is contained in one of the two
open half-planes, so the number of upper-half-plane zeros contributed
by such a disk is unchanged. A disk centered at a real zero contains
exactly one nearby zero. Since

F_{\chi,\kappa}(\overline{w})=\overline{F_{\chi,\kappa}(w)},

this unique zero must remain real: otherwise its conjugate would be
a second distinct zero in the same conjugation-invariant disk.

Thus the number of zeros of
F_{\chi,\kappa}
in

\mathbb{C}_{+}
, counted with multiplicity, is unchanged inside

V
. Since Lemma
6.10
places every
nearby nonreal zero in

\Omega\subset V,

there are no additional upper-half-plane zeros outside
V
.
Consequently, the number of upper-half-plane roots of the
characteristic equation is locally constant at

(\chi_{0},\kappa_{0})
.

At every point of
\operatorname{int}\mathscr{N}
, the number of
upper-half-plane roots is locally constant whenever the
characteristic equation has no multiple real root. Consequently,
the portion of the regular frozen boundary lying in

\operatorname{int}\mathscr{N}
is contained in the real
multiple-root locus (
2.24
). At such a boundary point there
exists
w\in\mathbb{R}
such that

S_{\chi}(w)=e^{-n\kappa},\qquad S_{\chi}^{\prime}(w)=0.

Since

S_{\chi}(w)=e^{-n\kappa}>0,

the second equation is equivalent to

\frac{d}{dw}\mathrm{Log}_{\chi,\mathcal{C}_{\chi}}S_{\chi}(w)=0.

At a nondegenerate fold point this multiple root has multiplicity
two, so (
2.24
) is the usual double-root system. This proves the
multiple-root assertion.
∎

7.
Height Laplace-Test Fluctuations and the Annular Image Kernel

In this section we prove Theorem
2.9
. The two-point asymptotics yield a finite-dimensional Gaussian theorem for height Laplace-test observables. The charge-centered coordinate of Definition
4.4
removes the random charge factor in Lemma
4.5
exactly, so the covariance is obtained directly from the Neguţ-observable covariance and is the annular image kernel of Proposition
5.14
. Thus the limiting Laplace-test covariance is not the pullback of the standard upper-half-plane covariance; it is the annular free-boundary image covariance generated by the two reflected boundary images.

Proof of Theorem
2.9
.

Fix points

\chi_{1},\dots,\chi_{s}\in(V_{0},V_{m})\setminus\{V_{1},\ldots,V_{m-1}\}

and positive integers
k_{1},\dots,k_{s}
. For each
d\in[s]
, choose a sequence

i_{d}^{(\epsilon)}\in[l^{(\epsilon)}..r^{(\epsilon)}]
such that

\epsilon i_{d}^{(\epsilon)}\to\chi_{d}

and such that the residue class of
i_{d}^{(\epsilon)}
modulo
n
is independent of
\epsilon
.

Define

X_{k_{d}}^{(\epsilon)}(\chi_{d}):=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(h^{(q,t),\circ}_{M^{(\epsilon)}}\!\left(i_{d}^{(\epsilon)},\frac{\eta}{\epsilon}\right)-{\mathbb{E}}\left[h^{(q,t),\circ}_{M^{(\epsilon)}}\!\left(i_{d}^{(\epsilon)},\frac{\eta}{\epsilon}\right)\right]\right)e^{-n\beta k_{d}\eta}\,d\eta.

By Corollary
4.5
, with the change of variables

\eta=\epsilon y
, we have the exact centered identity

X_{k_{d}}^{(\epsilon)}(\chi_{d})=\frac{2\epsilon^{2}}{k_{d}^{2}\log t\log q}Q_{k_{d}}^{(\epsilon)}(\epsilon i_{d}^{(\epsilon)}).

Indeed, the factor
t^{k_{d}\tilde{c}^{(M^{(\epsilon)},i_{d}^{(\epsilon)},q,t)}}
in Lemma
4.5
is cancelled by the charge-centered vertical translation. Moreover,

\log t=-n\beta\epsilon,\qquad\log q=-\alpha n\beta\epsilon,

so

\frac{2\epsilon^{2}}{k_{d}^{2}\log t\log q}\longrightarrow\frac{2}{\alpha n^{2}\beta^{2}k_{d}^{2}}.

Theorem
5.15
therefore implies that the vector

\bigl(X_{k_{1}}^{(\epsilon)}(\chi_{1}),\dots,X_{k_{s}}^{(\epsilon)}(\chi_{s})\bigr)

converges to a centered Gaussian vector. Multiplying the covariance in (
5.33
) with
g_{d}=k_{d}
and
g_{h}=k_{h}
by the two normalization factors

\frac{2}{\alpha n^{2}\beta^{2}k_{d}^{2}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{2}{\alpha n^{2}\beta^{2}k_{h}^{2}}

gives exactly (
2.25
). This proves Theorem
2.9
.
∎

8.
Example: Half-space Macdonald Process

The degeneration corresponding to the rail-yard realization of the half-space Macdonald-process geometry is obtained as the
v\downarrow 0
limit of the present model with free left boundary and empty right boundary, namely

\displaystyle u>0,\qquad v\downarrow 0,\qquad c_{l}=deel.

All formulas in this section are understood as limits of the doubly free-boundary formulas; this avoids substituting
v=0
directly into expressions that contain powers
v^{-1}
. We do not use a separate identification theorem with the notation of
[
3
]
; the statements below should be read as asymptotic consequences for this rail-yard specialization.

Lemma 8.1

(Uniform half-space degeneration)
.

For the contour families fixed in the preceding sections, the limits below are taken in domains where the one-point and two-point integrands are normally dominated, uniformly for all sufficiently small
v
. In particular,

\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{u,v,k}(w)\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{u,0,1}(w)

normally on the one-point contour neighborhoods, and

\mathscr{B}_{u,v}(z,w)\longrightarrow\mathscr{B}_{u,0}(z,w)

normally on the two-point product contour neighborhoods.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of the geometric estimates in Lemma
5.6
and the annular expansion (
5.29
). The factors with
k\geq 2
contain positive powers of
v
after the cancellations already used in Lemma
5.6
, hence converge normally to one in the one-point product. For the covariance correction, (
5.29
) gives uniform domination by a convergent majorant for all small
v
, and termwise convergence yields the stated limit.
∎

In this regime the partition function is given by (
3.38
). Moreover, the infinite product in Theorem
2.6
collapses substantially: for every
k\geq 2
one has

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{u,0,k}(w)=1,

and for
k=1
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{u,0,1}(w)=\mathcal{G}_{<V_{m},L}(u^{2}w)\bigl[\mathcal{G}_{<V_{m},R}(u^{2}w)\bigr]^{\alpha}.

Consequently Theorem
2.6
gives the following half-space degeneration of the Laplace-transform observable. For
\gamma=k\beta
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
, define

L_{u,0}^{(\epsilon)}(i^{(\epsilon)},\gamma):=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-n\gamma\kappa}\epsilon h^{(q,t)}_{M^{(\epsilon)}}\!\left(i^{(\epsilon)},\frac{\kappa}{\epsilon}\right)d\kappa.

Then, under the same contour and branch-admissibility hypotheses as Theorem
2.6
,

(8.1)

\displaystyle L_{u,0}^{(\epsilon)}(i^{(\epsilon)},\gamma)\xrightarrow[\epsilon\to 0]{\mathbb{P}}\frac{1}{n^{2}\alpha\gamma^{2}\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\mathcal{C}}\bigl[\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\mathcal{F}_{u,0,1}(w)\bigr]^{\gamma}\frac{dw}{w}.

Applying the moment-determinacy argument of Theorem
2.6
in this degeneration gives the corresponding limit shape in the natural variable
x=e^{-n\beta\kappa}
.

When
\beta=1
, under the
v\downarrow 0
analogues of
Assumptions
6.6
–
6.7
, the relevant one-point contour
and branch conditions, and the nonexceptionality conditions of
Theorem
2.8
, the regular multiple-root condition becomes

(8.2)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\mathcal{F}_{u,0,1}(w)=e^{-n\kappa},\\[8.53581pt]
\displaystyle\frac{d}{dw}\log\!\left(\mathcal{G}_{\chi}(w)\mathcal{F}_{u,0,1}(w)\right)=0,\end{cases}\qquad w\in\mathbb{R}.

This statement is conditional in the same sense as
Theorem
2.8
: the limit-shape realization follows from the
preceding moment argument, whereas the
v\downarrow 0
analogues
of the separated zero–pole, boundary-value, and endpoint
nonexceptionality conditions are not verified separately here.
Finally, under the same
L
-type marked-column, contour, branch,
and normal-convergence hypotheses as Theorem
2.9
, with height
observables written in the charge-centered
(q,t)
-column
coordinate, the annular Laplace-test Gaussian fluctuation theorem
specializes to this half-space setting with

\mathscr{B}_{u,0}(z,w)=\sum_{r\geq 1}ru^{2r}z^{r}w^{r}.

Thus the limiting Laplace-test covariance is the one-boundary degeneration of the annular free-boundary image covariance. It is not the standard upper-half-plane kernel in general; the displayed series is the surviving reflected-image contribution from the remaining free boundary.

Appendix A
Technical Results

In this appendix we collect the background on Macdonald polynomials and the technical lemmas used in Sections
4
and
5
.

A.1.
Macdonald polynomials and scalar products

Let
\mathcal{A}=\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}\mathcal{A}_{k}
be a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
-graded algebra. Its topological completion is the algebra of formal series

\displaystyle a=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{k},\qquad a_{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{k}.

For
a\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}
, define the lower degree by

(A.1)

\displaystyle\operatorname{ldeg}(a):=\inf\{k\geq 0:a_{k}\neq 0\},

with the convention
\operatorname{ldeg}(0)=+\infty
.

Definition A.1
.

Let
\Lambda_{X}
be the algebra of symmetric functions in a countable set of variables
X=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots)
. A
specialization
is an algebra homomorphism

\displaystyle\rho:\Lambda_{X}\to\mathcal{B},

where
\mathcal{B}
is a commutative
\mathbb{C}
-algebra. For
f\in\Lambda_{X}
, we write
f(\rho):=\rho(f)
.

Define the power-sum symmetric functions by

(A.2)

\displaystyle p_{n}(X):=\begin{cases}1,&n=0,\\
\displaystyle\sum_{x\in X}x^{n},&n\geq 1.\end{cases}

Then
\Lambda_{X}
is generated by
\{p_{n}(X)\}_{n\geq 1}
, so a specialization is determined by the values
\{p_{n}(\rho)\}_{n\geq 1}
.

For a partition
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots)
, let

\displaystyle p_{\lambda}:=\prod_{i\geq 1}p_{\lambda_{i}},\qquad z_{\lambda}:=\prod_{r\geq 1}r^{m_{r}(\lambda)}m_{r}(\lambda)!,

where
m_{r}(\lambda)
is the multiplicity of the part
r
in
\lambda
. For fixed
q,t\in(0,1)
, define the Macdonald scalar product on
\Lambda_{X}
by

(A.3)

\displaystyle\langle p_{\lambda},p_{\mu}\rangle_{q,t}:=\delta_{\lambda\mu}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{l(\lambda)}\frac{1-q^{\lambda_{i}}}{1-t^{\lambda_{i}}}\right]z_{\lambda}.

For each
\lambda\in{\mathbb{Y}}
, let
P_{\lambda}(X;q,t)
denote the normalized Macdonald polynomial characterized by the triangular expansion

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}(X;q,t)=m_{\lambda}(X)+\sum_{\mu<\lambda}u_{\lambda\mu}(q,t)m_{\mu}(X),

where
m_{\mu}
is the monomial symmetric function. Define
Q_{\lambda}(X;q,t)
by the normalization

\displaystyle\langle P_{\lambda}(X;q,t),Q_{\mu}(X;q,t)\rangle_{q,t}=\delta_{\lambda\mu}.

The skew Macdonald polynomials are defined by

(A.4)

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}(X,Y;q,t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu\in{\mathbb{Y}}}P_{\lambda/\mu}(X;q,t)P_{\mu}(Y;q,t),

(A.5)

\displaystyle Q_{\lambda}(X,Y;q,t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu\in{\mathbb{Y}}}Q_{\lambda/\mu}(X;q,t)Q_{\mu}(Y;q,t).

We also use the
q
-Pochhammer symbols

(A.6)

\displaystyle(a;q)_{N}:=\prod_{r=0}^{N-1}(1-aq^{r}),\qquad(a;q)_{\infty}:=\prod_{r=0}^{\infty}(1-aq^{r}).

Lemma A.2
.

Let
X
be a countable set of variables. Then
P_{\lambda/\mu}(X;q,t)=Q_{\lambda/\mu}(X;q,t)=0
unless
\mu\subseteq\lambda
. If
\mu\subseteq\lambda
, then both
P_{\lambda/\mu}(X;q,t)
and
Q_{\lambda/\mu}(X;q,t)
are homogeneous of degree
|\lambda|-|\mu|
.

Proof.

See
[
12
, (7.7), p. 344]
.
∎

In particular, when
q=t
,

(A.7)

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}(X;t,t)=Q_{\lambda}(X;t,t)=s_{\lambda}(X),\qquad P_{\lambda/\mu}(X;t,t)=Q_{\lambda/\mu}(X;t,t)=s_{\lambda/\mu}(X).

See
[
12
, (4.14), p. 324]
.

Lemma A.3
.

One has

(A.8)

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{Y}}}P_{\lambda}(X;q,t)Q_{\lambda}(Y;q,t)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i,j}\frac{(tx_{i}y_{j};q)_{\infty}}{(x_{i}y_{j};q)_{\infty}}=:\Pi(X,Y;q,t),

(A.9)

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{Y}}}P_{\lambda}(X;q,t)P_{\lambda^{\prime}}(Y;t,q)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{Y}}}Q_{\lambda}(X;q,t)Q_{\lambda^{\prime}}(Y;t,q)=\prod_{i,j}(1+x_{i}y_{j}).

Proof.

Equation (
A.8
) is the Cauchy identity; see
[
12
, (4.13), p. 324]
. Equation (
A.9
) is the dual Cauchy identity; see
[
12
, (5.4), p. 330]
.
∎

Lemma A.4
.

Let
\{a_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
and
\{b_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
be sequences in completed graded algebras such that

\displaystyle\operatorname{ldeg}(a_{k})\to\infty,\qquad\operatorname{ldeg}(b_{k})\to\infty\qquad(k\to\infty).

Then

\displaystyle\Biggl\langle\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{a_{k}p_{k}(Y)}{k}\right),\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{b_{k}p_{k}(Y)}{k}\right)\Biggr\rangle_{Y;q,t}=\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1-q^{k}}{1-t^{k}}\frac{a_{k}b_{k}}{k}\right),

where the coefficients
a_{k},b_{k}
are independent of the variables in
Y
.

Proof.

See
[
4
, Proposition 2.3]
.
∎

Lemma A.5
.

The kernels in Lemma
A.3
admit the exponential representations

(A.10)

\displaystyle\Pi(X,Y;q,t)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1-t^{n}}{1-q^{n}}\frac{p_{n}(X)p_{n}(Y)}{n}\right),

(A.11)

\displaystyle\prod_{i,j}(1+x_{i}y_{j})

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n+1}}{n}p_{n}(X)p_{n}(Y)\right).

Proof.

The first identity is standard; see
[
12
, p. 310]
. The second follows from the expansion of
\log(1+z)
.
∎

A.2.
Duality

Let
q,t
be algebraically independent, and write
F:={\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
. For
u,v\in F
with
v\notin\{\pm 1\}
, define the
F
-algebra endomorphism
\omega_{u,v}:\Lambda_{X}\otimes F\to\Lambda_{X}\otimes F
by

(A.12)

\displaystyle\omega_{u,v}(p_{r})=(-1)^{r+1}\frac{1-u^{r}}{1-v^{r}}p_{r},\qquad r\geq 1.

Equivalently,

\displaystyle\omega_{u,v}(p_{\lambda})=\epsilon_{\lambda}p_{\lambda}\prod_{i=1}^{l(\lambda)}\frac{1-u^{\lambda_{i}}}{1-v^{\lambda_{i}}},\qquad\epsilon_{\lambda}=(-1)^{|\lambda|-l(\lambda)}.

In particular,
\omega_{v,u}=\omega_{u,v}^{-1}
.

Lemma A.6
.

One has

\displaystyle\omega_{q,t}\Pi(X,Y;q,t)=\Pi_{0}(X,Y):=\prod_{i,j}(1+x_{i}y_{j}),

and

(A.13)

\displaystyle\omega_{q,t}P_{\lambda/\mu}(X;q,t)

\displaystyle=Q_{\lambda^{\prime}/\mu^{\prime}}(X;t,q),

(A.14)

\displaystyle\omega_{q,t}Q_{\lambda/\mu}(X;q,t)

\displaystyle=P_{\lambda^{\prime}/\mu^{\prime}}(X;t,q).

Proof.

See
[
12
, (2.18), p. 313]
for the first identity and
[
12
, (7.16)–(7.17), p. 347]
for (
A.13
)–(
A.14
).
∎

A.3.
The Neguţ operator and auxiliary contour lemmas

Definition A.7
.

Let
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
and
q,t>0
. For an analytic symmetric function

\displaystyle F(X)=\sum_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{Y}}}c_{\lambda}P_{\lambda}(X;q,t),

define the Macdonald difference operator
D_{-k,X;q,t}
by

(A.15)

\displaystyle D_{-k,X;q,t}F(X):=\sum_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{Y}}}c_{\lambda}\left[(1-t^{-k})\sum_{i=1}^{l(\lambda)}(q^{\lambda_{i}}t^{-i+1})^{k}+t^{-kl(\lambda)}\right]P_{\lambda}(X;q,t).

Let
W=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{k})
be an ordered set of variables. Define

(A.16)

\displaystyle D(W;q,t):=\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{(2\pi\mathbf{i})^{k}}\frac{\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{w_{k}t^{k-i}}{w_{i}q^{k-i}}}{\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\left(1-\frac{tw_{i+1}}{qw_{i}}\right)}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq k}\frac{\left(1-\frac{w_{i}}{w_{j}}\right)\left(1-\frac{qw_{i}}{tw_{j}}\right)}{\left(1-\frac{w_{i}}{tw_{j}}\right)\left(1-\frac{qw_{i}}{w_{j}}\right)}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{dw_{i}}{w_{i}},

and

(A.17)

\displaystyle L(W,X;q,t):=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\prod_{x\in X}\frac{w_{i}-\frac{q}{t}x}{w_{i}-qx}.

Proposition A.8
.

Assume either
q,t\in(0,1)
or
q,t\in(1,\infty)
. Let
f:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
be analytic in a neighborhood of
0
with
f(0)\neq 0
, and let
g:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
be analytic in a neighborhood of
0
such that

\displaystyle g(z)=\frac{f(z)}{f(q^{-1}z)}

for
z
sufficiently close to
0
. Then

(A.18)

\displaystyle D_{-k,X;q,t}\left(\prod_{x\in X}f(x)\right)=\left(\prod_{x\in X}f(x)\right)\oint\cdots\oint D(W;q,t)L(W,X;q,t)\prod_{i=1}^{k}g(w_{i}).

Here the integration contours are positively oriented simple closed curves such that:

(1)

all contours lie in a common neighborhood of
0
where both
f
and
g
are analytic;

(2)

each contour encloses
0
and all points
qx
with
x\in X
, but no other singularities of the integrand;

(3)

if
q,t\in(0,1)
, then the contour for
w_{i}
lies inside the contour for
tw_{i+1}
for every
i\in[k-1]
;

(4)

if
q,t\in(1,\infty)
, then the contour for
w_{i}
lies inside the contour for
q^{-1}w_{i+1}
for every
i\in[k-1]
.

Proof.

This is a slightly more general form of
[
7
, Proposition 4.10]
; see also
[
10
, Proposition A.2]
.
∎

Lemma A.9
.

Let
\theta\in(0,\pi)
and
\xi>0
. Define

\displaystyle R_{\epsilon,\theta,\xi}:=\bigl\{w\in\mathbb{C}:\operatorname{dist}(w,[1,\infty))\leq\xi\bigr\}\cap\bigl\{w\in\mathbb{C}:|\arg(w-(1-\epsilon))|\leq\theta\bigr\}.

Let
\alpha>0
and suppose
N(\epsilon)\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
satisfies

\displaystyle\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon N(\epsilon)>0.

Then for every fixed
\theta\in(0,\pi)
and
\xi>0
one has

\displaystyle\frac{(z;e^{-\epsilon})_{N(\epsilon)}}{(e^{-\epsilon\alpha}z;e^{-\epsilon})_{N(\epsilon)}}=\left(\frac{1-z}{1-e^{-\epsilon N(\epsilon)}z}\right)^{\alpha}\exp\left(O\left(\frac{\epsilon\min\{|z|,|z|^{2}\}}{|1-z|}\right)\right),

uniformly for
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus R_{\epsilon,\theta,\xi}
as
\epsilon\to 0
.

Proof.

See
[
1
, Lemma 5.7]
.
∎

Lemma A.10
.

Let
d,h,k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
, and let
f,g_{1},\ldots,g_{d}
be meromorphic functions with all poles contained in
\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{h}\}
. Then for
k\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathbf{i})^{k}}\oint\cdots\oint\frac{1}{(v_{2}-v_{1})\cdots(v_{k}-v_{k-1})}\prod_{j=1}^{d}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}g_{j}(v_{i})\right)\prod_{i=1}^{k}f(v_{i})\,dv_{i}

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{k^{d-1}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint f(v)^{k}\prod_{j=1}^{d}g_{j}(v)\,dv,

where all contours enclose
\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{h}\}
, and on the left-hand side the contour for
v_{i}
is required to lie inside the contour for
v_{j}
whenever
i<j
.

Proof.

See
[
7
, Corollary A.2]
.
∎
